React + TypeScript: Build a Modern Web App · Answer Key

Part 1: TypeScript and React Foundations

Sessions 1 to 9. Modules 1 to 3: why React and TypeScript belong together and a working local workspace (Node LTS, VS Code, the shoreline-api mock back end, a Vite react-ts app), the TypeScript you need before React (types and inference, object types and unions, functions and generics, typing real API data), then React in TypeScript from the first component to props, lists, state and events, forms with actions, styling and accessible components. Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 project check-in and assessment.

Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 1: Why React + TypeScript and Your Workspace

Answers and reasoning for every Session 1 item: the diagnostic, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 1 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Diagnostic

Not graded. Each reason also says where the skill comes back in this course. JavaScript's own answers to D1, D2 and D3 are in the diagnostic practice's output below; D4 and D5 are real runs too (D4 with the API running).

D1
What does this print?
const sites = ['Driftwood Cove', 'North Jetty', 'Seal Rock Beach']
console.log(sites.length, sites[0])
A) 3 Driftwood Cove
B) 3 North Jetty
C) 2 Driftwood Cove
D) 3 Seal Rock Beach
Why: length counts the three items and arrays count from 0, so sites[0] is the first. The API sends every list as an array like this one. Skill: arrays. Where it comes back: every list of cleanups from Lesson 3.3 on (map, keys).
D2
What does this print?
const cleanup = { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', site: { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' } }
console.log(cleanup.site)
A) Driftwood Cove
B) undefined
C) { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' }
D) An error
Why: cleanup.site is itself an object; its name is one level down, in cleanup.site.name. Lesson 2.2 describes exactly this nesting as types. Skill: objects inside objects. Where it comes back: Lesson 2.2 (object types) and every component that reads a cleanup's site.
D3
What does this print?
const events = [
  { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 18 },
  { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 },
]
console.log(events.filter((e) => e.spotsTaken < e.capacity).map((e) => e.title))
A) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]
B) [ 9 ]
C) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
D) [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
Why: filter keeps the items whose test is true (9 is under 10; 18 is not under 18); map turns each kept item into its title. React renders every list with map. Skill: filter and map. Where it comes back: Lesson 2.3 (typed functions over lists) and lists in Lesson 3.3.
D4
With the API running, what does this print?
const response = fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events')
console.log(response)
A) Promise { <pending> }
B) The JSON of the cleanups
C) undefined
D) Nothing: it waits for the answer
Why: fetch() returns a Promise at once; without await the code moves on and prints the Promise. Lab 1.2's debug handout has the same bug on response.json(). Skill: promises. Where it comes back: Lesson 4.1 (effects and fetching) and every request after it.
D5
What does this print?
const event = { capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 12 }
console.log(`${event.capcity - event.spotsTaken} spots left`)
A) 6 spots left
B) NaN spots left
C) undefined spots left
D) An error: capcity is not defined
Why: event.capcity does not exist, so it is undefined, and undefined - 12 is NaN. Plain JavaScript says nothing: Lesson 1.1's first experiment, and the reason TypeScript exists. Skill: template literals and undefined. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.1 (the NaN Node printed) and Lesson 2.1 (inference).
D6
Your terminal is in your home folder; the course folder is shoreline inside it. Which runs the environment check?
A) node check_environment.mjs
B) cd shoreline, then node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
C) npm run check_environment
D) sudo node labs/check_environment.mjs
Why: A path is looked up from the folder you are in. Every course command starts in the course folder or in one of its projects (the prompt shows which). Skill: folders and the terminal. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.2 (two terminals, the right folder) and every npm command after it.

D4 and D5, run for real:

Promise { <pending> }
NaN spots left

1.1 · Why React + TypeScript: self-check

Q1
What does node spots.ts do with the types in spots.ts?
A) Checks them, then runs the file if they are right
B) Removes them and runs the JavaScript that is left
C) Turns them into runtime checks that stop on a wrong value
D) Refuses to run the file until you install tsc
Why: Node 24 runs a .ts file by stripping the types. That is why the typo printed NaN instead of an error: only tsc (or your editor) reads the types (Lesson 1.1, the same file twice).
A is wrong: Nothing checks types while the code runs; that was the whole point of the NaN.
C is wrong: Types disappear completely: they cost nothing at run time and protect nothing at run time.
D is wrong: Node 24 runs .ts files directly; older Node versions are the ones that refuse.
Q2
Predict both: what does node p1.ts print for this file, and does npx tsc --noEmit p1.ts accept it?
const capacity = 18
const spotsTaken = '12'
console.log(capacity - spotsTaken)
Node prints 6; tsc rejects it with TS2363
Why: JavaScript turns the text '12' into a number for -, so Node prints 6 and looks fine. TypeScript refuses arithmetic on a string: error TS2363: The right-hand side of an arithmetic operation must be of type .... Item 4 is the case it cannot see.
Q3
tsc prints this for the cleanup script. Which change fixes it?
spots.ts:9:7 - error TS2741: Property 'spotsTaken' is missing in type '{ id: string; title: string; startTime: string; capacity: number; }' but required in type 'CleanupEvent'.

9 const cleanup: CleanupEvent = {
        ~~~~~~~

  spots.ts:6:3 - 'spotsTaken' is declared here.
    6   spotsTaken: number
        ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in spots.ts:9
A) Delete spotsTaken from the interface on line 6
B) Change CleanupEvent to any on line 9
C) Rename the variable cleanup on line 9
D) Add spotsTaken: 12, to the object that starts on line 9
Why: The first location (9:7) is where the problem shows up: the object is missing a required property. The indented follow-up (6:3) is where TypeScript's knowledge came from: the interface says every cleanup has one. Fix the data, not the description (Lesson 1.1, Step 3).
A is wrong: That silences the error by making the description wrong: every screen that needs spots taken loses it.
B is wrong: any switches checking off; the lesson's smoke-alarm-without-batteries.
C is wrong: The name is fine; the object's contents are not.
Q4
Which of these mistakes can TypeScript NOT catch on its own?
A) Reading event.capcity instead of event.capacity
B) Writing capacity: '18' where the interface says number
C) Writing capacity + spotsTaken where you meant capacity - spotsTaken
D) Leaving spotsTaken out of a cleanup
Why: Both expressions are perfectly typed numbers; only tests (Module 9) and you can tell which one is right. TypeScript also cannot see data that arrives while the app runs (Lesson 2.4, Module 8).
A is wrong: TS2551, with a Did you mean.
B is wrong: TS2322.
D is wrong: TS2741.
Q5
What does npx tsc --noEmit statuses.ts report for the last line?
type EventStatus = 'draft' | 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'

let current: EventStatus = 'scheduled'
current = 'canceled'

console.log(current)
A) Nothing: 'canceled' is a string, and EventStatus is made of strings
B) error TS2820: 'canceled' is not assignable to EventStatus. Did you mean 'cancelled'?
C) error TS2551: Property 'canceled' does not exist
D) Nothing at build time; the browser throws later
Why: A union of string literals allows exactly those four words, so the misspelling is caught, with a suggestion: statuses.ts:4:1 - error TS2820: Type '"canceled"' is not assignable to type 'EventStatus'. Did you mean '"cancelled"'?. Statuses, roles and screen states use this all course (Lesson 2.2).
A is wrong: That would be true for type EventStatus = string; the union is the point.
C is wrong: TS2551 is about properties of an object; here a value is assigned.
D is wrong: The check happens before anything runs.
Q6
Name the two places where TypeScript checks your code, and the one place it never does.
In the editor while you type, and when tsc runs (npm run build); never while the app runs in the browser
Why: Static checking: the editor's red underline and the compiler both read the types before the code runs; the browser only ever receives JavaScript (Lesson 1.1, three facts to keep).

1.1 · Why React + TypeScript: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run both.
const capacity = 18
const spotsTaken = '12'
console.log(capacity - spotsTaken)
node
6
npx tsc --noEmit
p1.ts:3:24 - error TS2363: The right-hand side of an arithmetic operation must be of type 'any', 'number', 'bigint' or an enum type.

3 console.log(capacity - spotsTaken)
                         ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in p1.ts:3
Why: JavaScript converts the text '12' to a number for -, so Node prints 6 and looks right. TypeScript refuses arithmetic on a string (TS2363) and points at the right-hand side. Item Q2 of the self-check.
P2
Predict, then run both.
const capacity = '18'
console.log(capacity + 2)
node
182
npx tsc --noEmit
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: + with a string means "join": '18' + 2 is '182', in JavaScript and in TypeScript. Nothing here contradicts a type you declared, so tsc is silent. The lesson's table: a string where a number belongs "works, or concatenates". With const capacity: number = '18' it would be TS2322.

1.2 · Your Workspace: self-check

Q7
npm run dev is running. You change const tagline: string to const tagline: number and save. What does the page at localhost:5173 show?
A) A blank page
B) Vite's error overlay with TS2322
C) The tagline, as before
D) NaN where the tagline was
Why: The dev server removes the types and serves the code without checking it; once the types are gone the line is ordinary, working JavaScript. Your editor and npx tsc -b still report TS2322, and npm run build refuses it (Lesson 1.2, Exercise 1).
A is wrong: The page goes blank when the code crashes while drawing (toUppercase), not on a wrong annotation.
B is wrong: Vite's dev server does not run tsc.
D is wrong: The value is still the text; only its annotation is wrong.
Q8
In shoreline-hq with no mistakes in it, what does this print?
npx tsc -b
Nothing at all: no output means no errors
Why: tsc speaks only when something is wrong. The silence is the success message; npm run build then goes on to vite build.
Q9
While you work on Shoreline Crew HQ, which two programs run, each in its own terminal?
A) The API (node server.ts, port 4000) and the dev server (npm run dev, port 5173)
B) VS Code and tsc -b
C) npm install and npm run build
D) The API and npm run preview (port 4173)
Why: The API answers with the data; the dev server serves the app; the browser talks to both. Start the API first (Step 5's start-of-session card).
B is wrong: VS Code is the editor; tsc is a check you run when you want it.
C is wrong: Both finish and stop; neither keeps running.
D is wrong: preview serves a finished build; while you work, it is the dev server.
Q10
A learner's node server.ts stops with ERR_UNKNOWN_FILE_EXTENSION and Node.js v20.20.2. What do you tell them?
A) Install the current LTS (24 or newer), then open a NEW terminal
B) Run npm install in shoreline-api first
C) Rename server.ts to server.js
D) Start it with npx tsc server.ts
Why: Node 20 cannot run TypeScript files; the API needs Node to strip the types itself (Lesson 1.2, An old Node.js). A terminal opened before the install still finds the old Node.
B is wrong: The API has no dependencies: there is nothing to install.
C is wrong: It is TypeScript; renaming does not remove the types.
D is wrong: tsc checks types; it does not run a server.
Q11
npm run build prints error TS2551 and Found 1 error. What happened to vite build?
A) It built the site anyway, with the error inside
B) It printed a warning and continued
C) It built only the files without errors
D) It never ran: the build script is tsc -b && vite build
Why: && runs the second command only when the first one succeeded, so a type error stops the build before anything broken is written to dist/ (Lesson 1.2, The scripts).
A is wrong: Nothing is built when tsc fails.
B is wrong: A type error is an error here, not a warning.
C is wrong: There is no partial build: vite build does not start.
Q12
Which tool printed this, does it make the command fail, and what does tsc say about the same line?
  ⚠ eslint(no-unused-vars): Variable 'motto' is declared but never used. Unused variables should start with a '_'.
    ╭─[banner.ts:9:7]
  8 │ export const crewSize: string = 24
  9 │ const motto = 'Many hands, cleaner sands.'
    ·       ──┬──
    ·         ╰── 'motto' is declared here
 10 │
    ╰────
  help: Consider removing this declaration.
oxlint (npm run lint); a warning, so the command still succeeds; tsc reports it as an error, TS6133
Why: The two tools overlap here and disagree on severity: the course app's tsconfig sets noUnusedLocals, so tsc fails the build, while oxlint warns. The course aims for 0 of both (Lesson 1.2, Exercise 2; Lab 1.2, Task 3).

1.2 · Your Workspace: predict, then check

P3
Predict the status code and the answer, then open it.
/api/sites/seal-rock
HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "not_found",
  "message": "No site with that id."
}
Why: 404 with a JSON body: not_found and a sentence a person can read. Not an empty answer and not a crash: an API says precisely what is wrong, and the app shows it (Lesson 4.1).
P4
Predict the status code and the answer, then open it.
/api/events?_status=500
HTTP/1.1 500 Internal Server Error
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "server_error",
  "message": "Simulated 500 response (the _status parameter)."
}
Why: The API's own switch: ?_status=500 makes it answer a server error on purpose, so you can build and test the error screen without breaking anything (Lesson 4.1; --fail-rate does it at random).

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the labs run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them (Node v24.22.2, in a terminal like a learner's, in a fresh copy of the lab folder; the environment check on a course folder copied from the course downloads, with a private copy of the API). Test-runner durations are left out; everything else is as printed.

Environment check: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs

A tool, not an exercise. Run from a ready course folder (the finished Lesson 1.2 code) with the API running, it prints (on a learner's machine the versions differ, and without VS Code's code command the line is a CHECK and the verdict READY WITH NOTES):

Environment check for React + TypeScript: Build a Modern Web App
Course folder: shoreline
OK    Node.js v24.22.2 (24 or newer)
OK    npm 11.19.1
OK    VS Code 1.146.0
OK    shoreline-api is in the course folder
OK    shoreline-hq: React 19.3.1, TypeScript 7.0.2, Vite 8.3.8, oxlint 1.93.0
OK    the API answers at http://localhost:4000/api: "Many hands, cleaner sands.", 15 public cleanups
OK    tsc -b: no type errors
OK    oxlint (npm run lint): Found 0 warnings and 0 errors.
OK    the dev server starts (npm run dev) and serves the app
READY for React + TypeScript: Build a Modern Web App

Three broken machines, as the checker builds them: nothing answering at the address, a course folder with the API but no shoreline-hq (the Lesson 1.2 starting code), and the ready folder with the lesson's typo in App.tsx. Each ends with NOT READY: fix the FIX lines above, then run this check again and exit code 1:

FIX   nothing answers at http://localhost:4299 (no answer (connection timed out)): start the API in its own terminal: cd shoreline-api, then node server.ts

FIX   no shoreline-hq here: in the course folder run  npm create vite@latest shoreline-hq -- --template react-ts  (Lesson 1.2)

FIX   tsc -b found type errors; the first: src/App.tsx(7,21): error TS2551: Property 'toUppercase' does not exist on type 'string'. Did you mean 'toUpperCase'?

The typo case is the lesson in miniature: tsc -b fails while the dev server still starts (its OK line is in the same run). On the instructor's machine a closed port timed out (connection timed out); on most machines it says ECONNREFUSED. Node older than 24 gives a FIX line naming the version (and node server.ts would fail with ERR_UNKNOWN_FILE_EXTENSION); lint warnings, a missing code command or a synced folder give CHECK lines (READY WITH NOTES).

Diagnostic practice: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.mjs → diagnostic_solution.mjs

As shipped the starter prints JavaScript's answers with the three mistakes in place (the second site instead of the first, the site object instead of its name, the wrong comparison):

D1: 3 North Jetty
D2: { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' }
D3: [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]

The solution prints (the starter's "Expected when done" comment quotes it):

D1: 3 Driftwood Cove
D2: Driftwood Cove
D3: [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', 'Family Beach Morning' ]

Common sticking points: sites[1] for the first item (arrays count from 0); cleanup.name (the name is in site); flipping >= to > instead of <.

Lab 1.1 (guided): labs/module-01/l1_1-why-react-typescript/starter → solution

As shipped, npm test in starter/ (the summary) and npm run check (the first line of each error):

ℹ tests 5
ℹ pass 0
ℹ fail 5
bags.ts:13:30 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.
bags.ts:14:3 - error TS2741: Property 'weightKg' is missing in type '{ siteName: string; bags: number; }' but required in type 'BagCount'.
bags.ts:20:20 - error TS2551: Property 'wieghtKg' does not exist on type 'BagCount'. Did you mean 'weightKg'?
lab.test.ts:38:1 - error TS2578: Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive.
Found 4 errors in 2 files.

The solution: npm test, and npm run check prints nothing after its two > lines:

✔ Task 1: the summary of the first cleanup
✔ Task 1: a full cleanup has 0 spots left
✔ Task 2: every count has a number of bags and a weight
✔ Task 2: totalWeight adds the weights
✔ Task 3: a label for each status
ℹ tests 5
ℹ pass 5
ℹ fail 0

What each task shows: (1) the interface comes from the real JSON, field by field (text in quotes is string, bare numbers are number), and the editor's suggestions start the moment it exists; (2) three error shapes on new code: TS2322 points at bags with the interface line as the reason, TS2741 at the object missing weightKg, TS2551 at the typo with a Did you mean (any weight for Pelican Point Beach is fine); (3) a union of four words, which makes the test file's @ts-expect-error line correct: while EventStatus is string, tsc reports the directive as unused (TS2578), a neat way to TEST a type. Accept an if chain or a switch in statusLabel.

As shipped: the tests (spec view), the type check and the linter:

✖ the banner names the crew in capitals and counts the volunteers

The solution: the tests, and npm run lint (npm run check prints nothing after its two > lines):

✔ the crew size is a number
ℹ tests 2
ℹ pass 2
ℹ fail 0

Reading it: (1) the typo crashes while running (the terminal's blank page) and tsc names it first, with the fix; (2) crewSize: string = 24 runs perfectly, because the annotation is gone when the code runs, so only tsc complains: the fix is number (changing the value to '24' would pass the check and fail the second test); (3) the unused motto is a tsc ERROR (TS6133, noUnusedLocals) and an oxlint WARNING: remove it, or export it. Watch for learners who silence TS6133 by prefixing _: oxlint accepts that, tsc does not.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_2-your-workspace/debug_count_events.ts (deliberately broken)

As shipped, with the API running, Node stops at once:

[TypeError: Failed to parse URL from http://localhost:4000api/events] {
  [cause]: TypeError: Invalid URL
      at debug_count_events.ts:6:24
    code: 'ERR_INVALID_URL',
    input: 'http://localhost:4000api/events'
  }
}

Node.js v24.22.2
  1. Bug 1, the address: api + 'api/events' glues the port to the path (http://localhost:4000api/events] {). Fix: ${api}/api/events . The course writes addresses without a slash at the end and adds /api/... in code.
  2. Bug 2, a missing await: with bug 1 fixed, Node prints 200: undefined cleanups on the first page and then crashes on events[0].title. response.json() returns a Promise; events.length is read from the Promise. Fix: const events = await response.json().

TypeScript names bug 2 before anything runs: the bug-1-fixed handout, copied into solution/ as count_events.ts, then npm run check:

count_events.ts:8:43 - error TS2339: Property 'length' does not exist on type 'Promise<any>'.
count_events.ts:9:31 - error TS7053: Element implicitly has an 'any' type because expression of type '0' can't be used to index type 'Promise<any>'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: count_events.ts:8

Fixed, it is solution/count_events.ts, which prints (against the course API):

200: 10 cleanups on the first page
The first one: Storm Debris Sweep

The lesson in it: the first message is not always the only bug, and Node only finds the second one by running into it; tsc finds it by reading (diagnostic D4, self-check Q1).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.1 · Why React + TypeScript
  1. C The script runs and prints NaN, with no warning: Node only strips the types and runs the JavaScript that is left. The misspelled property is undefined, undefined minus a number is NaN, and nothing warns you. Only the TypeScript compiler (or your editor) checks the types.
  2. A Updating the screen when a cleanup's number of sign-ups changes: React draws the screen from your data and updates it when the data changes. TypeScript checks that your code uses the data the way its types describe; it never touches the screen.
  3. D Never, unless your code checks the data itself: TypeScript only checks code before it runs and believes whatever type you declare for incoming data. Data from the outside must be checked while the app runs, which Lesson 2.4 and Module 8 teach.
  4. B At the line that uses the component with the string: Props are a contract. The error appears at the line that breaks it, where the component is used with a string, and it points back to the props type that declares a number.
  5. C It is a local back end with sample data that both course apps talk to: shoreline-api is a small mock back end that runs on your computer with sample data around today's date. Shoreline Crew HQ talks to it over HTTP, and the React Native course's phone app uses the same API and types.
Lesson 1.2 · Your Workspace
  1. B The page goes blank, because the browser fails while running code Vite never checked: Vite strips the types and serves the file unchecked. The browser fails when it calls a method that does not exist, React has nothing to draw, and the page is blank, with the error only in the developer tools console. The editor and tsc would have caught it before.
  2. D npm run build: The build script is tsc -b and then vite build, joined by and-and: vite build only runs when the type check passes, so a type error stops the build.
  3. A The API is already running in another terminal: Only one program can listen on a port. Another copy of the API is almost certainly running in a terminal you opened earlier: use it, or stop it with Ctrl+C first.
  4. C Changing into shoreline-hq first, then running the command: npm looks for package.json in the current folder. The app's package.json is in shoreline-hq, so cd shoreline-hq and run the command there.
  5. B In src/main.tsx, which calls createRoot on the root element and renders App: index.html loads src/main.tsx, which calls createRoot on the element with the id root and renders the App component into it. The exclamation mark tells TypeScript that element is not null.

Opening: Reading the Room

There is no retrieval warm-up in Session 1. The opening's two checks have their answers above: the environment check output (what READY looks like) and the diagnostic D1 to D6. Learners who end the clinic still NOT READY leave with a numbered plan in their workbook's Start Here box.

Exit Ticket

  1. Who does what: React draws the screen from the app's data and keeps it up to date (components, declarative); TypeScript checks before the code runs that the code treats the data the way its types describe.
  2. NaN versus the error: Node removes the types and runs the JavaScript, so undefined - 12 quietly becomes NaN; tsc reads the types and reports TS2551 with a suggestion. Running is not checking.
  3. Two terminals: shoreline-api running node server.ts (the API, http://localhost:4000) and shoreline-hq running npm run dev (the app, http://localhost:5173). The API first.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("checks them, then runs"). Believes running includes checking. Rerun the NaN demo: who would have printed an error?
  • Q2 "Node prints NaN" or "612". Mixes - and + with strings. Run P1 and P2 side by side: - converts, + joins.
  • Q3 A (delete it from the interface). Fixes the description to match broken data. Ask which screens need spots taken.
  • Q4 A, B or D. Has not separated type errors from logic errors. Write capacity + spotsTaken on the board: which rule does it break? None.
  • Q7 A or B. Expects the dev server to check. Do Lesson 1.2's Exercise 1 live: the tagline stays.
  • Q11 A or C. Does not know what && does in the build script. Read "build": "tsc -b && vite build" together.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 2: Types, Inference and Shaping Data

Answers and reasoning for every Session 2 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 2 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Session 1 Retrieval

  1. W1 node spots.ts printed NaN; npx tsc --noEmit spots.ts printed TS2551. Why the difference? Answer: Node removes the types and runs what is left; tsc reads the types and checks them. Running is not checking.
  2. W2 npx tsc -b prints nothing at all in shoreline-hq. Good or bad? Answer: Good: tsc speaks only when something is wrong.
  3. W3 npm run build printed error TS2322. Did vite build run? Answer: No: the script is tsc -b && vite build, and && stops at the first failure.
  4. W4 Which tool flags an unused constant as a warning, and which one makes it an error? Answer: oxlint (npm run lint) warns; tsc reports TS6133 because the app's tsconfig sets noUnusedLocals.

2.1 · Types and Inference: self-check

Q1
In a fresh scratch.ts you write let volunteers = 21 and const capacity = 18. What do the two hovers show?
A) let volunteers: number and const capacity: number
B) let volunteers: number and const capacity: 18
C) let volunteers: 21 and const capacity: 18
D) let volunteers: any and const capacity: any
Why: let may change later, so TypeScript infers the general type; a const never changes, so it gets the literal type 18 (Lesson 2.1, Inference). Literal types become the building blocks of unions in Lesson 2.2.
A is wrong: That is what const capacity: number = 18 would show: the annotation throws the literal away.
C is wrong: A let can be reassigned, so its type cannot be one exact value.
D is wrong: Inference always finds a type for a value you wrote; any comes from outside data (JSON.parse) or a parameter without a type.
Q2
Predict both: does npm run check accept this file, and what does node scratch.ts print?
const saved = JSON.parse('{"capacity": "18"}')
const left: number = saved.capacity - 12
console.log(left)
The check is silent; Node prints 6
Why: JSON.parse returns any, and everything read from an any is any too, so saved.capacity - 12 passes the check. At run time JavaScript turns the text '18' into a number for -. It looks right by luck: with "twelve" it would be NaN. Typed unknown, the check would ask first (TS18046).
Q3
Which of these lines needs a type annotation to pass npm run check in ts-lab?
A) const crewName = 'Shoreline Crew'
B) const bags = [14, 9, 22]
C) let volunteers = 21
D) function spotsLeft(capacity, taken) { ... }
Why: Parameters are an edge: TypeScript does not guess them from the callers and reports TS7006 (Parameter 'capacity' implicitly has an 'any' type). The other three have values to infer from (Lesson 2.1, When to Write a Type).
A is wrong: Inferred as the literal "Shoreline Crew".
B is wrong: Inferred as number[] from the items.
C is wrong: Inferred as number.
Q4
npm run check prints this. What is TypeScript asking you to do?
scratch.ts:4:13 - error TS18046: 'meta' is of type 'unknown'.
A) Annotate the parameter meta
B) Prove what meta is (for example with typeof) before you use it
C) Change unknown to any so the property can be read
D) Add tagLine to the JSON text
Why: TS18046: an unknown value accepts anything but allows nothing until you narrow it. Here the check also finds the typo (tagLine vs tagline) that any let through to a crash (Lesson 2.1, any and unknown).
A is wrong: meta is a variable, not a parameter (that would be TS7006).
C is wrong: That silences the question and brings the crash back: the smoke alarm without batteries.
D is wrong: The data is right; the code misspells the property.
Q5
npm run check reports this for the last line. Which fix answers the question?
const sizes = ['S', 'M', 'L']
const big = sizes.find((size) => size === 'XL')
console.log(big.length)
A) console.log(big!.length)
B) const big: any = sizes.find(...)
C) if (big !== undefined) { console.log(big.length) }
D) Set "strict": false in tsconfig.json
Why: find returns undefined when nothing matches, so big is string | undefined and the check says <code class="inline">error TS18048: 'big' is possibly 'undefined'.</code>. Here nothing matches at all: ! or any would turn the question into a crash (Lesson 2.1, Exercise 2; Lesson 2.2's null toolkit).
A is wrong: ! promises the value is there; with no 'XL' in the list the program crashes.
B is wrong: any switches checking off for big and everything read from it.
D is wrong: Turning strict checks off hides every null question in the project, not just this one.
Q6
In ts-lab you type npx tsc types.ts. What happens, and what do you run instead?
tsc stops with error TS5112 (tsconfig.json is present but will not be loaded if files are specified on the command line); run npm run check (tsc --noEmit with no file name)
Why: Naming a file would ignore the project's settings, so TypeScript 7 refuses. npm run check checks every .ts file in the folder with the tsconfig (Lesson 2.1, Common Mistakes).

2.1 · Types and Inference: predict, then check

P1
Predict what npm run check says, then check.
const meetTime = '09:00'
let startTime = meetTime
startTime = 9
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:3:1 - error TS2322: Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.

3 startTime = 9
  ~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:3
Why: meetTime is the literal "09:00", but let startTime = meetTime gets the general type string (a let may change), so assigning 9 is TS2322. Lesson 2.1's logbook: the first entry decided the column.
P2
Predict what node scratch.ts prints and what npm run check says, then run both.
const saved: unknown = JSON.parse('18')
console.log(saved + 1)
node scratch.ts
19
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:2:13 - error TS18046: 'saved' is of type 'unknown'.

2 console.log(saved + 1)
              ~~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:2
Why: Node runs it (JSON gave a number, and 18 + 1 is 19). The check refuses: an unknown must be narrowed before you use it, even when today's value happens to be right.

2.2 · Shaping Data: self-check

Q7
Which statement about interface and type is true?
A) Only interface can describe an object shape
B) Only type can name a union such as 'draft' | 'scheduled'
C) A type alias can be reopened to add properties later
D) They catch different mistakes in object literals
Why: An interface always describes an object; a type alias names any type, unions included. The course convention (and the API's types.ts): interface for object shapes, type for unions and everything else (Lesson 2.2, interface or type?).
A is wrong: type Site = { ... } describes the same shape and catches the same mistakes.
C is wrong: It is the other way round: two interfaces with one name merge; a second type Site is an error.
D is wrong: Both report TS2561, TS2353 and TS2741 the same way.
Q8
Does npm run check accept return state.message after only the loading check, and what does node scratch.ts print?
type LoadState =
  | { kind: 'loading' }
  | { kind: 'failed'; message: string }

function report(state: LoadState) {
  if (state.kind === 'loading') {
    return 'Loading...'
  }
  return state.message
}
console.log(report({ kind: 'failed', message: 'offline' }))
Yes, the check is silent; it prints offline
Why: Each check removes a member of the union. After if (state.kind === 'loading') return, only the failed member is left, and it has a message. Add a third member without message and the same line becomes TS2339 (Lesson 2.2, A discriminant property).
Q9
What does node scratch.ts print?
const left: number = 0
console.log(left || 'unknown', left ?? 'unknown')
A) 0 0
B) unknown unknown
C) 0 unknown
D) unknown 0
Why: || falls back on any falsy value, and 0 is falsy; ?? falls back only on null and undefined. For "missing", use ?? (Lesson 2.2, Why ?? and not ||).
A is wrong: || replaces the 0.
B is wrong: ?? keeps the 0: it is not null or undefined.
C is wrong: The two operators are the other way round.
Q10
Which use of the non-null assertion ! is honest?
A) document.getElementById('root')! in main.tsx, where index.html in the same project has the element
B) sites.find((site) => site.id === siteId)!.name
C) savedName!.trim() for a name read from sessionStorage
D) event.checkInCode!.length on a cleanup a volunteer opened
Why: ! is a promise TypeScript cannot check. It is fair only for what your own project guarantees: the root element is in your own index.html. Anything from a search, the browser's storage or the API gets an if, ?? or ?. (Lesson 2.2, The ! assertion).
B is wrong: A search can find nothing: the lesson's Seal Rock Pier crash.
C is wrong: A first visit has nothing saved: null.
D is wrong: The API leaves the code out for volunteers: undefined.
Q11
In type LoadState = { kind: 'loading' } | { kind: 'failed'; message: string } | { kind: 'ready'; events: CleanupEvent[] }, what is kind?
A) An optional property
B) A type parameter
C) The discriminant: a shared property with a different literal in each member
D) A readonly property
Why: A discriminated union tells its members apart by one shared literal property; checking it narrows to one member. The course always calls it kind for screen states (Lessons 2.2, 3.3, 4.1) and type for reducer actions (5.1).
A is wrong: Every member has it; nothing is optional.
B is wrong: Type parameters are Lesson 2.3's <T>.
D is wrong: Nothing here says readonly.
Q12
tsc prints this for capacity.toFixed(0). What do you write before the call?
scratch.ts:3:19 - error TS2339: Property 'toFixed' does not exist on type 'string | number'.
A) if (typeof capacity === 'number'), and call it inside
B) capacity: any in the parameter list
C) capacity!.toFixed(0)
D) Turn the union into an interface
Why: With a union you may only use what every member has; numbers have toFixed, strings do not. Narrow with typeof and TypeScript lets you call it inside the if (Lesson 2.2, Narrowing: typeof).
B is wrong: any hides the question; the string case would crash.
C is wrong: ! removes null and undefined, not string.
D is wrong: An interface describes an object, not a string or a number.

2.2 · Shaping Data: predict, then check

P3
Predict what npm run check says, then check.
type Size = 'S' | 'M' | 'L'
const order = { name: 'Volunteer shirt', size: 'M' }
const size: Size = order.size
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:3:7 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'Size'.

3 const size: Size = order.size
        ~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:3
Why: The widening trap again: order was built without a type, so order.size is string, and a string is not a Size. const order: { name: string; size: Size } = … (or an interface) fixes it where it is created.
P4
Predict the two lines node scratch.ts prints, then run it.
function spotsText(spotsTaken: number | null) {
  return `${spotsTaken ?? 'not counted'} | ${spotsTaken || 'not counted'}`
}
console.log(spotsText(0))
console.log(spotsText(null))
node scratch.ts
0 | not counted
not counted | not counted
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit
Why: ?? falls back only on null (and undefined), so 0 stays 0; || falls back on 0 too. With null both fall back. The check is silent: both are valid code, and this is a logic choice only you can make.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 2.1 · Types and Inference
  1. B const meetTime: "09:00": A const can never change, so TypeScript infers the literal type, the exact text 09:00. A let with the same value would be inferred as string.
  2. C The parameters in function spotsLeft(capacity, taken): TypeScript does not guess parameter types from the calls, so both parameters would implicitly be any, which strict mode reports as TS7006. Annotate them, for example capacity: number.
  3. A npm run check passes, and the program crashes when it runs: JSON.parse returns any, and everything read from an any is unchecked too. The check passes, and the program crashes when it runs because meta.tagLine is undefined. Typing meta as unknown would have turned this into TS18046.
  4. D TS2345: Argument of type 'number' is not assignable to parameter of type 'string': push is a function call, so the message is about an argument: Argument of type number is not assignable to parameter of type string. TS2345 is the call version of TS2322.
  5. C Use it inside if (session !== null) { … }: getItem returns string or null. Checking session !== null handles the nothing-saved case, and inside the if TypeScript knows session is a string.
Lesson 2.2 · Shaping Data
  1. B type Terrain = 'sand' | 'rocky' | 'marsh' | 'mixed': A union of literal types allows exactly these four strings. Anything else, including a typo, is TS2322 (or TS2820 with a Did you mean suggestion), and the editor suggests the four words as you type.
  2. D TS18048: 'event.checkInCode' is possibly 'undefined': An optional property reads as string or undefined, so TS18048 asks you to handle the missing case, for example with event.checkInCode?.length ?? 0 or an if.
  3. A The property was inferred as string; annotate the object: const flats: Site = …: Object properties can change later, so TypeScript infers the general type string for terrain. Writing const flats: Site = ... checks marsh against Terrain where the object is created.
  4. C Inside if (state.kind === 'failed') { … }: Checking the discriminant narrows state to the member with kind failed, which is the only one that has message.
  5. A unknown: 0 is falsy, so the OR operator falls back to the right side. For missing values use ??, which only falls back on null and undefined and prints 0 here.

Exit Ticket

  1. Annotations: function parameters, a variable that gets its value later, an empty array; data from outside as unknown.
  2. unknown: the check asks what the value is before you use it (TS18046 until you narrow it); any waves every mistake through, and it spreads to everything read from it.
  3. LoadState: type LoadState = { kind: 'loading' } | { kind: 'failed'; message: string } | { kind: 'ready'; events: CleanupEvent[] } (any discriminant name is fine; the course uses kind).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Has not seen const give a literal type. Hover const meetTime = '09:00' together.
  • Q2 "NaN" or "an error". Thinks the check reads JSON values, or that - refuses text at run time. Run it; then type saved as unknown.
  • Q3 A, B or C. Annotates values TypeScript can see. Back to the decision diagram: does it have its value already?
  • Q5 A. Reaches for !. Run it: the list has no XL.
  • Q9 A or C. Mixes up ?? and ||. Write the falsy values on the board: false, 0, '', null, undefined, NaN.
  • Q10 B, C or D. Trusts data with !. Ask who guarantees each value.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 3: Functions, Generics and Typing Real Data

Answers and reasoning for every Session 3 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 3 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Session 2 Retrieval

  1. W1 const flats = { name: 'Sandpiper Flats', terrain: 'marsh' } passed where a Site is expected gives TS2345 ending in "Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'Terrain'". Why, and the fix? Answer: The property was inferred as string (a const object's properties can change); annotate where it is created: const flats: Site = ….
  2. W2 spotsTaken || 'unknown' with spotsTaken = 0: what prints, and what should it be? Answer: unknown; use ??, which falls back only on null and undefined.
  3. W3 const meta = JSON.parse(text): what is its type, and what does the course write instead? Answer: any; the course writes const meta: unknown = JSON.parse(text) and narrows it before use.
  4. W4 In LoadState, which property tells the members apart, and what is it called? Answer: kind: the discriminant of a discriminated union.

2.3 · Functions and Generics: self-check

Q1
What does node scratch.ts print?
function spotsText(left: number, unit = 'spots'): string {
  return `${left} ${unit} left`
}
console.log(spotsText(1, 'spot'), '/', spotsText(4))
A) 1 spots left / 4 spots left
B) 1 spot left / 4 undefined left
C) 1 spot left / 4 spots left
D) Nothing: npm run check reports TS2554 first
Why: A default parameter is used only when the caller leaves the argument out; spotsText(4) gets 'spots'. From the caller's side it is simply optional: the hover shows unit?: string (Lesson 2.3, Optional, Default and Rest Parameters).
A is wrong: The first call passes 'spot', which replaces the default.
B is wrong: The default fills the gap: never undefined inside the function.
D is wrong: One or two arguments are both allowed (Expected 1-2 arguments).
Q2
Why does npm run check reject function eventLine(town?: string, title: string)?
A required parameter cannot follow an optional one (TS1016): with one argument, TypeScript could not tell which one you meant. Put the optional parameter last, give it a default, or use an options object
Why: The lesson's rule for optional parameters, with its real error TS1016. An options object ({ showStatus = false, prefix = '-' }: LineOptions = {}) removes the ordering problem altogether, and it is exactly how React props work (Lesson 3.2).
Q3
With type EventFormatter = (event: CleanupEvent) => string and printAll(list, format: EventFormatter), which call does npm run check accept?
A) printAll(events, (event) => event.capacity)
B) printAll(events, () => 'one more cleanup')
C) printAll(events, (title: string) => title)
D) printAll(events, (event, page: number) => event.title)
Why: A function that takes FEWER parameters fits a function type (it simply ignores the cleanup); the return type must still be a string. The lesson's two failing calls are A (returns a number) and C (the wrong parameter type).
A is wrong: Returns a number: TS2322, "the expected type comes from the return type of this signature".
C is wrong: TS2345: Types of parameters 'title' and 'event' are incompatible.
D is wrong: A second REQUIRED parameter that printAll never passes: not assignable.
Q4
groupBy<T, K>(items: T[], keyOf: (item: T) => K): Map<K, T[]> is called as groupBy(events, (event) => event.status) with events: CleanupEvent[]. What does the hover show it returns?
A) Map<string, CleanupEvent[]>
B) Map<K, T[]>
C) Map<any, any[]>
D) Map<EventStatus, CleanupEvent[]>
Why: TypeScript infers both type parameters from the arguments: T from the array, K from what the callback returns. The labeled bin reads its label from what you put in (Lesson 2.3, project Step 4 and its screenshot).
A is wrong: status is an EventStatus, not any string.
B is wrong: The type parameters are filled in at the call.
C is wrong: Generics keep the checks: that is the point over any.
Q5
findById<T extends { id: string }> is called with a list of bag counts. tsc prints this. What does it mean?
scratch.ts:22:22 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'number[]' is not assignable to parameter of type '{ id: string; }[]'.
A) The constraint is doing its job: numbers have no id, so they cannot be searched by id
B) findById needs an explicit type argument: findById<number>(...)
C) The constraint must be removed for numbers
D) bagCounts needs an annotation
Why: extends reads "has at least". The constraint lets the body use item.id AND stops calls with items that have none, at the call, where the mistake is (Lesson 2.3, Constraints).
B is wrong: T would still have no id: the call stays wrong.
C is wrong: Then the body's item.id is TS2339 again.
D is wrong: Its type, number[], is inferred correctly; it just is not a list of things with ids.
Q6
This statusLabel gets TS2366 with its : string. What happens if you delete : string?
type EventStatus = 'draft' | 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'

function statusLabel(status: EventStatus): string {
  if (status === 'scheduled') {
    return 'Scheduled'
  }
  if (status === 'completed') {
    return 'Completed'
  }
}

console.log(statusLabel('draft'))
The check goes silent (the inferred return type becomes "Scheduled" | "Completed" | undefined), and statusLabel('draft') quietly returns undefined
Why: A written return type makes TypeScript check every path against the promise, inside the function. That is the course's reason to write return types on helpers other code relies on (Lesson 2.3, Common Mistakes; Lesson 2.2's bonus question).

2.3 · Functions and Generics: predict, then check

P1
Predict what npm run check says about the last line; then predict the first two lines Node prints.
function bagsLine(bags: number, label = 'bags', note?: string): string {
  return note === undefined ? `${bags} ${label}` : `${bags} ${label} (${note})`
}
console.log(bagsLine(3))
console.log(bagsLine(1, 'bag', 'heavy'))
console.log(bagsLine())
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:6:13 - error TS2554: Expected 1-3 arguments, but got 0.

6 console.log(bagsLine())
              ~~~~~~~~

  scratch.ts:1:19 - An argument for 'bags' was not provided.
    1 function bagsLine(bags: number, label = 'bags', note?: string): string {
                        ~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:6
node scratch.ts (without the last line)
3 bags
1 bag (heavy)
Why: bags is required; label has a default and note is optional, so the count says 1-3 and the follow-up points at the parameter that was not provided. bagsLine(3) uses the default; the second call passes everything.
P2
Predict what npm run check says, then check.
function middle<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined {
  return items[Math.floor(items.length / 2)]
}
const sites = ['pelican-point', 'driftwood-cove', 'north-jetty']
const pick = middle(sites)
console.log(pick.toUpperCase())
npm run check
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:6:13 - error TS18048: 'pick' is possibly 'undefined'.

6 console.log(pick.toUpperCase())
              ~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:6
Why: middle is honest: it returns T | undefined (an empty list has no middle), and T is string here, so pick is string | undefined: TS18048. pick?.toUpperCase() or an if answers it.

2.4 · Typing Real Data: self-check

Q7
In ts-lab you change import type { CleanupEvent, Volunteer } from './shapes.ts' to import { CleanupEvent, Volunteer } from './shapes.ts'. What happens?
A) npm run check reports TS1484 (and TS5097), and node functions.ts stops with does not provide an export named 'CleanupEvent'
B) Nothing: both forms do the same
C) Only the editor complains; Node runs it
D) node functions.ts first runs shapes.ts and prints its eight lines
Why: Types are stripped before Node runs the files, so at run time there is no CleanupEvent to import. verbatimModuleSyntax makes the check ask for import type, so the tools can delete the line (Lesson 2.4, What happens without type).
B is wrong: They differ exactly at run time.
C is wrong: Node fails with a SyntaxError.
D is wrong: With import type, shapes.ts never runs; without it, the import fails before anything prints.
Q8
What does node scratch.ts print, and is npm run check silent?
function isText(value: unknown): value is string {
  return typeof value === 'string'
}
const words: unknown[] = ['gloves', 3, 'bags', null]
const texts = words.filter(isText)
console.log(texts.map((word) => word.toUpperCase()))
[ 'GLOVES', 'BAGS' ], and yes, the check is silent
Why: filter has an overload for type guards: passing isText makes the result string[], so word.toUpperCase() is allowed. With a plain boolean return type, texts would be unknown[] and the check would refuse (Lesson 2.4, A type predicate says what "true" means).
Q9
A guard promises value is number but its body is return value !== undefined. What does TypeScript do?
A) Reports TS2322 in the guard's body
B) Checks the value at run time and throws
C) Compiles without a word, and treats every value that passes (even the text 'twelve') as a number
D) Refuses to narrow with a guard it cannot verify
Why: A type predicate is a promise TypeScript cannot check: it trusts the body completely. So a guard checks every property its type promises and is tested with good and bad data; Module 8 hands the job to Zod (Lesson 2.4, the stop sign).
A is wrong: The body returns a boolean, which is all TypeScript asks.
B is wrong: Types never run.
D is wrong: It narrows exactly as the predicate says.
Q10
type StatusLabels = Record<EventStatus, string>, and const labels: StatusLabels = { draft: 'Draft', scheduled: 'Scheduled', completed: 'Completed' }. What does the check say?
A) Nothing: Record allows any keys
B) TS2353: 'cancelled' is an extra property
C) Nothing at check time; reading labels.cancelled crashes later
D) An error: property 'cancelled' is missing
Why: A Record over a union insists on EVERY key, which is why the lesson calls it a favorite for lookup tables: add a fifth status and TypeScript lists every table that needs it (Lesson 2.4, Utility Types; Lesson 3.3 uses one).
A is wrong: Record<string, string> would; a union of keys is exact.
B is wrong: Nothing extra is there; one is missing.
C is wrong: The check catches it before anything runs.
Q11
With noUncheckedIndexedAccess on and const siteIds: string[], which line is NOT affected?
A) const firstId = siteIds[0] (its type)
B) for (const id of siteIds) { console.log(id.toUpperCase()) }
C) siteIds[2].toUpperCase()
D) return items[0] in a function promising T
Why: Reading by index may find nothing, so it gives string | undefined; a for...of loop only visits items that exist, so id stays a string (Lesson 2.4, The setting that makes items[0] honest).
A is wrong: It becomes string | undefined.
C is wrong: TS18048 (or TS2532): possibly undefined.
D is wrong: TS2322: Type 'T | undefined' is not assignable to type 'T'.
Q12
You put API_URL=http://localhost:4000 (no prefix) in .env.local and read import.meta.env.API_URL in the HQ. What happens?
A) It works: every variable in .env.local reaches the code
B) The check reports TS2339 (only VITE_API_URL is declared), and at run time the value is undefined: Vite passes only VITE_ names
C) Vite keeps it secret on the server
D) The build fails with a Vite error
Why: Only VITE_-prefixed variables reach browser code, and with strictImportMetaEnv every name must be declared in vite-env.d.ts. And whatever does reach the code is public: nothing in the front end is secret (Lesson 2.4, Environment Variables).
A is wrong: Vite filters by the prefix, on purpose.
C is wrong: There is no server here: everything ends up in the browser or nowhere.
D is wrong: Vite builds; the value is simply missing (and the type check catches the name first).

2.4 · Typing Real Data: predict, then check

                                                                ~~~~

Why: Pick keeps only the listed properties, and an object literal with an extra one is TS2353 (Lesson 2.2's excess property check). The edit form sends only the id and the phone.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 2.3 · Functions and Generics
  1. C TS2554: Expected 2-3 arguments, but got 1: count and word are required and plural has a default, so a call needs two or three arguments. The follow-up line says an argument for word was not provided.
  2. A (event: CleanupEvent) => string: A function type looks like an arrow function with types and no body: the parameters in parentheses, then the arrow and the return type. Give it a name with a type alias, such as EventFormatter.
  3. C string | undefined: TypeScript infers T from the argument: an array of strings, so T is string and the result is string or undefined (the array might have been empty).
  4. B Add a constraint: findById<T extends { id: string }>: A constraint tells TypeScript that every T has a string id, so item.id is allowed inside, lists without ids are rejected at the call, and each call still returns its own type.
  5. D Mistakes are reported inside the function, where the bug is, and callers get a clear promise: A written return type is a promise the function is checked against, so a mistake is reported on the line inside the function (as with TS2741 in paginate) instead of at callers in other files, and the signature documents what callers can rely on.
Lesson 2.4 · Typing Real Data
  1. C Node removes it with the other types, so shapes.ts never runs: A type-only import is removed with the other types, so shapes.ts never runs. With a plain import, Node would ask shapes.ts for a CleanupEvent at run time and fail, which is what TS1484 warns about.
  2. B When the function returns true, TypeScript treats the argument as a Volunteer: The function still returns a boolean. The predicate is a promise: after a true answer, TypeScript treats the argument as a Volunteer. TypeScript trusts it, so the body must really check.
  3. D CleanupEvent | undefined: Nothing guarantees an item at a given position, so indexing adds undefined. That is the TS2532 on events[0].title, fixed with ?. and ??.
  4. A On a server that calls the map service for the app, never in the front end: Vite copies every VITE_ variable into the JavaScript that every visitor downloads, so the front end cannot keep a secret. A server (like shoreline-api) keeps the key and calls the map service on the app's behalf.
  5. C Partial<Omit<CleanupEvent, 'id'>>: Omit removes the id, and Partial makes everything that is left optional. The API's EventChanges type is built the same way from NewEvent.

Exit Ticket

  1. Function type: (event: CleanupEvent) => string (usually named: type EventFormatter = …).
  2. The constraint: the body reads item.id; extends { id: string } guarantees every T has one and refuses lists without ids (TS2345 at the call).
  3. getJSON: throws Unexpected data from GET /api… and never returns unchecked data; everything past the door can trust its type.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or B. Mixes up default and passed values. Run both calls, hover the function.
  • Q3 A or C. Believes any function fits a function type. Show the TS2322 "expected type comes from the return type of this signature".
  • Q4 A or C. Thinks generics widen. Hover the call in the lesson's screenshot.
  • Q7 B or C. Has not seen that types vanish at run time. Run the plain import: SyntaxError.
  • Q9 A or D. Expects TypeScript to verify guards. Write the lying guard and run it with 'twelve'.
  • Q12 A or C. Thinks .env files are private to the app. Grep the built file for the address.

Session 4: Components and TSX

Session 4 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Session 3 Retrieval

  1. W1 What happens to an import type line when Node runs the file? Answer: It is removed with the other types; the imported file never runs.
  2. W2 What does value is EventStatus add to a function that returns a boolean? Answer: When it returns true, TypeScript treats the argument as an EventStatus (a type guard).
  3. W3 With noUncheckedIndexedAccess on, what is the type of events[0] when events: CleanupEvent[]? Answer: CleanupEvent | undefined.
  4. W4 What does the browser console of your HQ show on the dev server since Lesson 2.4, with the API running? Answer: Loaded 10 cleanups. First: Storm Debris Sweep

3.1 · Components and TSX: self-check

Q1
You switched App to a named export, and tsc prints this for main.tsx. What is the fix?
src/main.tsx:4:8 - error TS2613: Module '"/Users/you/shoreline/shoreline-hq/src/App"' has no default export. Did you mean to use 'import { App } from "/Users/you/shoreline/shoreline-hq/src/App"' instead?
A) Add export default App back to App.tsx as well
B) Change the import to import { App } from './App.tsx'
C) Rename App.tsx to App.ts
D) Remove the .tsx extension from the import
Why: TS2613: import App from asks for a DEFAULT export, and App.tsx now has a NAMED one. The message's "Did you mean" is the fix. The course uses named exports for everything it adds: the import must use the real name (Lesson 3.1, Step 5).
A is wrong: Two exports for one component is exactly the mix-up the convention avoids.
C is wrong: A component file holds TSX: .tsx.
D is wrong: The HQ imports keep their extensions since Lesson 1.2.
Q2
On the dev server, a console.log('HomePage rendered') at the top of HomePage prints twice for one page load. Why?
A) HomePage is rendered by two parents
B) A bug: the component re-renders forever
C) <StrictMode> calls each component twice in development to expose impure ones; the build does not
D) The browser loaded main.tsx twice
Why: Strict Mode, development only, calls each component twice and throws one answer away, so an impure component shows its problem early. A pure component gives the same TSX both times (Lesson 3.1, Rendering must be pure).
A is wrong: AppLayout places it once.
B is wrong: It would print forever, not twice.
D is wrong: One module load; two calls.
Q3
Why does npx tsc -b reject this component, and what is the fix that adds nothing to the page?
export function SiteHeading() {
  return (
    <h2>Pelican Point Beach</h2>
    <p>Port Marisol</p>
  )
}
TS2657: JSX expressions must have one parent element (a function returns one value); wrap the two in a fragment, <>…</>
Why: Tags are values: two side-by-side tags would be two return values. A fragment groups them without adding a box to the page (Lesson 3.1, Rule 2).
Q4
tsc prints this for <p>Today's lead: {leader}</p>. What do you write instead?
src/Scratch.tsx:4:27 - error TS2322: Type '{ name: string; phone: string; }' is not assignable to type 'ReactNode'.
A) {leader.toString()}
B) {JSON.stringify(leader)}
C) {leader as ReactNode}
D) {leader.name}: the property you want to show
Why: React can draw text, numbers, elements and arrays of these, never a plain object. Say which property to show. Without TypeScript the browser shows a blank page and "Objects are not valid as a React child" (Lesson 3.1, Rule 4; Lab 3.1 Task 2).
A is wrong: Draws [object Object].
B is wrong: Draws raw JSON: valid, but not what a user should read.
C is wrong: An assertion silences the check and brings back the crash.
Q5
In Header.tsx, import logoUrl from '../assets/logo.svg'. What is the type of logoUrl, and where does it come from?
A) string, from Vite's declare module '*.svg' (loaded by "types": ["vite/client"])
B) SVGElement, because the file is an SVG
C) any: TypeScript cannot read SVG files
D) HTMLImageElement, from the DOM library
Why: A declaration file says that any import ending in .svg has a default export that is a string: the file's address after the build. <img src={logoUrl} /> always points at the right place (Lesson 3.1, Images and Their Types).
B is wrong: An import gives an address, not an element.
C is wrong: The declaration gives it a precise type; there is no any.
D is wrong: That is what querySelector('img') returns (Lesson 2.3).
Q6
The crew's logo sits right next to the words "Shoreline Crew HQ" in the header. Which alt is right?
A) alt="logo"
B) alt="Shoreline Crew HQ logo"
C) No alt at all, since the text is there
D) alt="", so screen readers skip a decorative picture
Why: Describe what the picture tells the user, or write alt="" when the text next to it already says it: otherwise a screen reader announces the name twice. A missing alt is not the same as an empty one (Lesson 3.1, Step 3; the checks count img[alt='']).
A is wrong: Says nothing useful and repeats.
B is wrong: Reads the name twice.
C is wrong: Without alt, screen readers may read the file name.

3.1 · Components and TSX: predict, then check

P1
Predict what npx tsc -b says about this component (in src/Scratch.tsx), then check.
export function TidesLink() {
  return <a href="/tides" tabindex="0">Tide tables</a>
}
npx tsc -b
src/Scratch.tsx:2:27 - error TS2322: Type '{ href: string; tabindex: string; children: string; }' is not assignable to type 'DetailedHTMLProps<AnchorHTMLAttributes<HTMLAnchorElement>, HTMLAnchorElement>'.
  Property 'tabindex' does not exist on type 'DetailedHTMLProps<AnchorHTMLAttributes<HTMLAnchorElement>, HTMLAnchorElement>'. Did you mean 'tabIndex'?

2   return <a href="/tides" tabindex="0">Tide tables</a>
                            ~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:2
Why: Attributes use JavaScript's camelCase names; TypeScript knows every attribute an <a> accepts and suggests the fix. tabIndex={0}: a number in braces.
P2
A component that returns text instead of an element: predict what npx tsc -b says, then check.
function Tagline() {
  return 'Many hands, cleaner sands.'
}

export function Banner() {
  return (
    <p className="lead">
      <Tagline />
    </p>
  )
}
npx tsc -b
(no output: no errors)
Why: Nothing: a string is a ReactNode, so a component may return text (or a number, or null). Most components return one element, but React can draw anything in the ReactNode list.

  )
}

With class and for put back into the solution's TideRules (the kit's run html-habits), React renders it and warns, and the test counts the warnings:

+   "Invalid DOM property `for`. Did you mean `htmlFor`?",
+   "Invalid DOM property `class`. Did you mean `className`?",
Test Files  1 failed (1)
Tests  1 failed | 2 passed (3)

What each task shows: (1) every HTML habit has a TSX name, and React's warnings are the runtime echo of tsc's TS2322; accept tabIndex={0} (preferred) or tabIndex="0". (2) An object in braces is TS2322 in the check and "Objects are not valid as a React child" when it runs. (3) TS2613 and the lowercase component (TS2339 + TS6133); PageFooter.tsx with a named export.

Checkpoint 1: model answer

Verified like a lab (labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/lab.json): the starter's five tests fail with TODO and its check reports 10 errors; the model answer passes the five tests and a silent check.

// Checkpoint 1: model answer (instructor copy)

export type Zone = 'high' | 'middle' | 'low'

export interface Survey {
  id: string
  site: string
  animal: string
  zone: Zone
  count: number
  note?: string
  verifiedBy: string | null
}

export function surveyLine(survey: Survey): string {
  const verified = survey.verifiedBy === null ? 'not verified yet' : `verified by ${survey.verifiedBy}`
  const note = survey.note === undefined ? '' : ` (${survey.note})`
  return `${survey.animal} at ${survey.site} (${survey.zone} zone): ${survey.count}, ${verified}${note}`
}

export type SurveyFormatter = (survey: Survey) => string

export function formatAll(surveys: Survey[], format: SurveyFormatter = surveyLine): string[] {
  return surveys.map(format)
}

export function maxBy<T>(items: T[], valueOf: (item: T) => number): T | undefined {
  let best: T | undefined
  for (const item of items) {
    if (best === undefined || valueOf(item) > valueOf(best)) {
      best = item
    }
  }
  return best
}

const zones: Zone[] = ['high', 'middle', 'low']

function isRecord(value: unknown): value is Record<string, unknown> {
  return typeof value === 'object' && value !== null && !Array.isArray(value)
}

export function isSurvey(value: unknown): value is Survey {
  return (
    isRecord(value) &&
    typeof value.id === 'string' &&
    typeof value.site === 'string' &&
    typeof value.animal === 'string' &&
    zones.some((zone) => zone === value.zone) &&
    typeof value.count === 'number' &&
    (value.note === undefined || typeof value.note === 'string') &&
    (value.verifiedBy === null || typeof value.verifiedBy === 'string')
  )
}

export function readSurveys(json: string): Survey[] {
  const data: unknown = JSON.parse(json)
  if (Array.isArray(data) && data.every(isSurvey)) {
    return data
  }
  throw new Error('Unexpected survey data')
}

export type SurveyEdit = Partial<Omit<Survey, 'id'>>

Scoring notes: Task 1 with ?? (survey.note ? … : '' is fine too) or a template with two small ifs both meet the row; || for the note is fine (an empty note adds nothing), ! on verifiedBy is "Partly". Task 2: items.reduce or a loop in maxBy; returning T (with !) fails the honest-undefined line: "Partly". Task 3: a guard written without isRecord but with typeof value === 'object' && value !== null meets the row; data as Survey[] is "Not yet" even when the tests pass on good data (the bad-data tests catch it).

Rubric (the guide's, for reference)
CriterionMeets (2)Partly (1)Not yet (0)
Types and unions (Task 1)Zone is a union of the three words; Survey has the optional note and verifiedBy: string | null; the Task 1 @ts-expect-error lines are acceptedTypes written, but one is too loose (zone: string, note: string | undefined required, verifiedBy?:)Types missing, any, or copied from a neighbor
Null and narrowing (Tasks 1 and 3)surveyLine handles the missing note and the null verifiedBy with if, ?? or ?.; no !; the guard narrows the zone with an equality checkWorks, but with !, || or an asCrashes on the null or the missing note, or never narrows
Functions and generics (Task 2)SurveyFormatter is a function type; formatAll has the default; maxBy<T> takes (item: T) => number and returns T | undefinedWorks for surveys only, or returns T (the honest-undefined line fails)any parameters or no types
Outside data (Task 3)JSON.parse result typed unknown; isSurvey is a type predicate checking every property; readSurveys throws on bad data; SurveyEdit = Partial<Omit<Survey, 'id'>>The guard checks only some properties, or SurveyEdit written by handas Survey[] instead of a check, or no guard
ProcessRuns npm test and npm run check after each change, reads the first error top to bottom, can explain one decisionRuns only the tests, or reads only the last line of an errorDoes not run the checks; guesses

Module 2 review: self-check

Q7
Module 2 review. const zones = ['high', 'middle', 'low']. What is the inferred type of zones?
A) string[]
B) ('high' | 'middle' | 'low')[]
C) readonly ['high', 'middle', 'low']
D) any[]
Why: The array of a const can still change (push), so its items are inferred as string. To get the union, write the type: const zones: Zone[] = [...], as the checkpoint's model answer does (Lessons 2.1, Exercise 1, and 2.2).
B is wrong: Only with an annotation (Zone[]).
C is wrong: That needs as const, which the course does not use.
D is wrong: Inference sees the strings.
Q8
Module 2 review. With note?: string and verifiedBy: string | null in Survey, which object literal is NOT a valid Survey?
A) One with a note and verifiedBy: null
B) One without a note and verifiedBy: 'Maya Okafor'
C) One without a note and without verifiedBy
D) One with a note and verifiedBy: 'Theo Nakamura'
Why: An optional property may be left out; a string | null property must be there, with null meaning "deliberately empty" (TS2741 when it is missing). The checkpoint's type test checks exactly this (Lesson 2.2, FAQ: optional vs null).
A is wrong: Valid: null is allowed.
B is wrong: Valid: the note is optional.
D is wrong: Valid.
Q9
Module 2 review. Write the signature (no body) of maxBy: it takes any list and a function that gives a number for each item, and returns the item with the largest number, or undefined for an empty list.
function maxBy<T>(items: T[], valueOf: (item: T) => number): T | undefined
Why: A type parameter for the items, a function type for the callback (its parameter is T, so callers' arrow functions are typed by context), and an honest return type: an empty list has no largest item (Lesson 2.3).
Q10
Module 2 review. isSurvey(value: unknown): boolean checks every property correctly. Why does if (isSurvey(data)) { data.zone } still fail the check?
A) unknown can never be narrowed
B) A boolean return type does not tell TypeScript what true means; it needs value is Survey
C) The guard must use as Survey inside
D) Guards only work with filter
Why: TypeScript does not read function bodies to find out what true means: the type predicate says it (Lesson 2.4).
A is wrong: typeof, equality and guards all narrow it.
C is wrong: as is a claim, not a check.
D is wrong: They narrow in an if as well.
Q11
Module 2 review. Which type means "any property of a survey except the id, all optional"?
A) Partial<Survey>
B) Omit<Survey, 'id'>
C) Pick<Survey, 'id'>
D) Partial<Omit<Survey, 'id'>>
Why: Omit removes the id, Partial makes the rest optional; utility types compose (Lesson 2.4; the API's EventChanges is built the same way).
A is wrong: Still allows the id.
B is wrong: Every other property is still required.
C is wrong: Only the id.
Q12
Module 2 review. A phone sends JSON text. Which first line does the course write?
A) const data = JSON.parse(json) as Survey[]
B) const data: unknown = JSON.parse(json)
C) const data: any = JSON.parse(json)
D) const data: Survey[] = JSON.parse(json)
Why: Outside data is unknown until checked; then a guard (Array.isArray(data) && data.every(isSurvey)) turns it into Survey[] (Lessons 2.1 and 2.4).
A is wrong: A claim, not a check: bad data passes.
C is wrong: Checks nothing, and spreads.
D is wrong: The same claim as A, written as an annotation: JSON.parse returns any, so it is accepted unchecked.

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 3.1 · Components and TSX
  1. D A call to React's jsx functions that returns a plain object describing the h1: The compiler turns the tag into a jsx call with the tag name, the attributes as an object and the contents as children. The call returns a plain object describing the h1, and React later makes the page match it.
  2. A TSX attributes use JavaScript's names, and React's types for label call this one htmlFor: TSX attributes become properties of an object, and for is a reserved word in JavaScript, so React uses the DOM property name htmlFor. React's types list every attribute a label accepts, which is how TypeScript can suggest the right one.
  3. C The tag is the HTML footer element, so the page gets an empty footer and tsc reports the function as unused: Lowercase tags are HTML elements, and footer is a real one, so the page gets an empty HTML footer. The function is never used, which noUnusedLocals reports as TS6133. Components are always capitalized.
  4. B One that returns a heading built from crewName, a constant defined at the top of its file: It only reads values that do not change and returns TSX built from them, so every call gives the same result and changes nothing else. That is all purity asks.
  5. A Its return is alone on a line, so the TSX below it never gets returned: With return alone on its line, the function returns nothing, so its inferred type is a function returning void. Starting the TSX on the return line with return ( fixes it, and the linter points at the unreachable TSX.

Exit Ticket

  1. render to the page: React calls the component functions, gets elements (plain objects) and creates or changes the real DOM to match; after data changes it renders again and changes only what differs.
  2. Two TSX rules: any two of className/htmlFor and camelCase attributes, one parent or a fragment, every tag closed, braces for expressions, comments in braces.
  3. Named, capitalized, one per file: the import uses the real name (no TS2613 mix-ups; renames reach every file); lowercase tags are HTML; one component per file is easy to find.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Wants both kinds of export. Explain the one-rule habit: named exports for everything the course adds.
  • Q2 A or B. Reads Strict Mode as a bug. Show that the build logs once.
  • Q4 A or B. Draws an object as text. Show [object Object] on the page.
  • Q7 B. Expects inference to keep literals in arrays. Hover zones together.
  • Q8 A or B. Mixes optional and nullable. Write note?: string and verifiedBy: string | null side by side.
  • Q12 A or D. Writes a claim instead of a check. Feed it {"surveys": []}.
