3.2 · Props, Children and Composition: self-check

Q1
NextCleanup draws <SiteName name="Driftwood Cove" town="Port Marisol" />. What does React pass to SiteName?
A) Two arguments: 'Driftwood Cove' and 'Port Marisol'
B) Nothing: the component reads the attributes from the page
C) One object: { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' }
D) A string of HTML it has to parse
Why: Every attribute goes into ONE props object, the second argument of _jsx(SiteName, { name, town }) in the compiled code; the parameter destructures it (Lesson 3.2, Props: a Component's Options Object).
A is wrong: That is how a plain function with two parameters is called; components get one object.
B is wrong: Components never read the page; the parent hands them their data.
D is wrong: TSX compiles to function calls with objects, not to HTML strings.
Q2
A teammate made the step-free badge orange for sites without a step-free route, and tsc prints this. What is the honest fix?
src/components/SiteCard.tsx:19:11 - error TS2322: Type '"success" | "warning"' is not assignable to type '"info" | "success" | undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/components/SiteCard.tsx:19
A) Decide whether the design needs a new tone: add 'warning' to Badge's union (and a .badge-warning style), or keep 'info'
B) Write tone={... as any}
C) Change Badge's tone to tone?: string
D) Delete the tone prop from Badge
Why: The union is the list of looks Badge can draw (a class per word in index.css). Either the design grows by one word, in the union and in the CSS together, or the card uses a tone that exists. Every other answer makes a badge with no style possible (Lesson 3.2, Step 4; Exercise 2).
B is wrong: any switches checking off; the badge would get a class nothing styles.
C is wrong: Then any typo becomes a class name and nothing warns you.
D is wrong: Every badge loses its colors to silence one error.
Q3
Predict what npx tsc -b says about this line in src/Scratch.tsx.
import type { Site } from './api/types.ts'

type SiteImage = Site['picture']
error TS2339: Property 'picture' does not exist on type 'Site'. (the property is called image)
Why: An indexed access type names a property's type, so the property must exist: the same check as reading site.picture in code, only at the type level (Lesson 3.2, Reuse the domain types).
Q4
TideTime declares label?: string. Its first line was changed to function TideTime({ time, label, highlight = false }: TideTimeProps) and tsc prints this. Which change fixes it without changing any caller?
src/Scratch.tsx:10:8 - error TS18048: 'label' is possibly 'undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:10
A) Make label required in TideTimeProps
B) Type the props as any
C) Write label?.toUpperCase()
D) Give label a default in the destructuring: label = 'Low tide'
Why: An optional prop is string | undefined inside the component; a default removes the undefined, so label is a plain string and every caller keeps working (Lesson 3.2, Optional Props and Defaults).
A is wrong: Then every caller that leaves it out gets TS2741: you changed the callers.
B is wrong: any switches the checks off for every prop.
C is wrong: It compiles, but the first TideTime draws ': 06:40' with no label at all.
Q5
Exercise 1's ContactLine takes volunteer: Pick<Volunteer, 'name' | 'phone'> (phone is string | null). Which object literal can a caller pass?
A) { name: 'Hannah Becker' }
B) { name: 'Hannah Becker', phone: null }
C) { name: 'Hannah Becker', phone: 5550102 }
D) { name: 'Hannah Becker', phone: null, role: 'organizer' }
Why: Pick keeps exactly name and phone, both required; null is a real value ("we know there is no phone"). Leaving phone out is TS2741, a number is TS2322 and an extra property in an object literal is TS2353 (Lesson 3.2, Exercise 1; workbook P3).
A is wrong: Missing is not null: TS2741.
C is wrong: phone is text or null: TS2322.
D is wrong: Object literals may not carry properties the type does not list: TS2353.
Q6
A component does site.name = site.name.toUpperCase() before it returns. What reports it?
A) tsc, with TS2540 (read-only property)
B) oxlint's react(immutability) warning; tsc says nothing
C) React, with an error in the console
D) Nothing at all: it is fine to fix up a prop
Why: The props object is not marked readonly, so npx tsc -b is silent; npm run lint warns react(immutability): This value cannot be modified. Work out a new value instead: <h2>{site.name.toUpperCase()}</h2> (Lesson 3.2, Props are read-only).
A is wrong: Only properties typed readonly give TS2540; Site's are not.
C is wrong: React does not watch your objects; the parent's data simply changes behind its back.
D is wrong: It changes the parent's data and breaks the same-inputs-same-output rule from Lesson 3.1.
Q7
tsc prints this for the Note component. Explain the message and name the two-line fix.
src/Scratch.tsx:11:6 - error TS2322: Type '{ title: string; children: Element; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & NoteProps'.
  Property 'children' does not exist on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & NoteProps'.
The paragraph between the tags arrived as a children prop and NoteProps has none: add children: ReactNode to NoteProps and draw {children}
Why: Content between a component's tags becomes props.children. A component that does not declare it does not accept any; without TypeScript the paragraph would silently disappear (Lesson 3.2, Children and Composition).
Q8
function SiteName(name: string) is used as <SiteName name="Driftwood Cove" />, and tsc prints this. What is wrong?
src/Scratch.tsx:6:11 - error TS2322: Type '{ name: string; }' is not assignable to type 'string'.
A) The name must be passed in braces: name={'Driftwood Cove'}
B) SiteName must return a string
C) The parameter is the whole props object: destructure it, ({ name }: { name: string })
D) Components cannot take strings, only objects
Why: React passes { name: string; } (one object); the function expects a string. Destructure the props object in the parameter list (Lesson 3.2, Forgetting the braces around the props).
A is wrong: A string in quotes and in braces is the same value.
B is wrong: A component returns TSX; the problem is its parameter.
D is wrong: Props can hold strings; they arrive inside the props object.
Q9
A Badge should show one short word on any page (a town, a terrain, a status). Which props fit best?
A) label: string and tone?: 'info' | 'success' with a default
B) site: Site, so it can pick the word itself
C) children: ReactNode, so it can hold anything
D) One boolean per color: info, success, warning, danger
Why: A general-purpose component takes plain values so any page can use it; variants are a union with a default, not several booleans; a badge holds one short label, so children would invite paragraphs (Lesson 3.2, Designing a Component's Props).
B is wrong: That ties a general component to one kind of data: the home page could not use it for a status.
C is wrong: Children are for frames like Card; a badge is one word.
D is wrong: Booleans allow impossible combinations (info AND danger); a union allows exactly one.
Q10
Someone deletes the line {children} from AppLayout and saves. What tells them before the browser does?
A) Nothing: only the empty page shows it
B) React prints a warning in the console
C) oxlint's react(jsx-key)
D) npx tsc -b: TS6133, 'children' is declared but its value is never read
Why: noUnusedParameters (Lesson 1.2) catches the destructured children that is never used, which here means an empty layout. Only because it is unused: use it somewhere else and nothing warns (Lesson 3.2, Step 7).
A is wrong: The compiler does catch it, through the unused parameter.
B is wrong: React has nothing to warn about: the component returns valid TSX.
C is wrong: jsx-key is about lists without keys.
Q11
Old code: TideTime.defaultProps = { label: 'Low tide' } on a function component that destructures { time, label }. With React 19.3, what does <TideTime time="06:40" /> render, and do tsc or oxlint complain?
<p>: 06:40</p> (no label); neither tool says a word
Why: React 19 ignores defaultProps on function components written in TSX, while tsc and oxlint accept the assignment silently. Move each default into the destructuring (Lesson 3.2, Common Questions).
Q12
After pasting the sites into src/data/sites.ts, someone changes one terrain to beach. Where does the second location in this message point, and why?
src/data/sites.ts:10:5 - error TS2322: Type '"beach"' is not assignable to type 'Terrain'.
Found 1 error in src/data/sites.ts:10
src/api/types.ts:44:3 - The expected type comes from property 'terrain' which is declared here on type 'Site'
A) To the API's own types (src/api/types.ts): pasted JSON is checked against Site like any other code
B) To node_modules: TypeScript's description of strings
C) To the API server, which rejected the value
D) Nowhere: data files are not checked
Why: The annotation Site[] checks all six objects, nested image included; the follow-up line shows where the rule comes from, the Terrain union in the copied API types (Lesson 3.2, Step 2).
B is wrong: That follow-up appears for string methods (Lesson 1.2); here the rule is the API's Terrain type.
C is wrong: Nothing is sent anywhere: this is tsc reading a file.
D is wrong: A .ts data file is code: every value is checked.

The solution's three files:

// Task 1: a small rounded label with a tone. Two tones only: 'info' (the default) and 'warning'.
interface TagProps {
  label: string
  tone?: 'info' | 'warning'
}

export function Tag({ label, tone = 'info' }: TagProps) {
  return <span className={`tag tag-${tone}`}>{label}</span>
}
import type { ReactNode } from 'react'

// Task 2: a frame with a heading. Whatever sits between <Panel> and </Panel> goes under the heading.
interface PanelProps {
  title: string
  children: ReactNode
}

export function Panel({ title, children }: PanelProps) {
  return (
    <section className="panel">
      <h2>{title}</h2>
      {children}
    </section>
  )
}
import type { Site } from './api/types.ts'
import { Panel } from './Panel.tsx'
import { Tag } from './Tag.tsx'

// Task 3a: a site's picture. It needs the picture object only, not the whole site.
export function SitePhoto({ image }: { image: Site['image'] }) {
  return (
    <img
      className="site-photo"
      src={image.url}
      alt={image.alt}
      width={image.width}
      height={image.height}
    />
  )
}

// Task 3b: small pieces composed: the Panel knows nothing about sites, the tile does
export function SiteTile({ site }: { site: Site }) {
  return (
    <Panel title={site.name}>
      <SitePhoto image={site.image} />
      <p>
        <Tag label={site.town} />{' '}
        <Tag
          label={site.accessible ? 'Step-free route' : 'No step-free route'}
          tone={site.accessible ? 'info' : 'warning'}
        />
      </p>
    </Panel>
  )
}

What each task shows: (1) a union prop with a default; the test file's @ts-expect-error lines TEST the type: with tone?: string the first one is "unused" (TS2578) because tone="danger" was accepted; (2) content between the tags arrives as children, and until PanelProps declares it, both the test file and SiteTile get TS2322 (the props TypeScript received included children); (3) Site['image'] for the part, and a tile composed from pieces that know nothing about sites. Accept <Tag ... /> pairs in any order and any wrapper element; the tests look for the texts and classes only. Watch for tone = 'info' written in the interface (tone?: 'info' is a type, not a default).

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 3.2 · Props, Children and Composition
  1. C Badge({ label: 'sand' }), with one object holding every attribute: All attributes are collected into one props object, and the component receives it as its single argument. Destructuring in the parameter list unpacks it, just like an options object in Lesson 2.3.
  2. A A <Card> was used with content inside it but no title attribute: A Card was used with content between its tags (which became children) but without the required title attribute. The fix is in the place that uses Card: add a title.
  3. D 'info' | 'success': The default fills in the value when the prop is left out, so undefined is gone inside the component, and the union of the two words stays. That is why the template literal can only produce badge-info or badge-success.
  4. B ReactNode: ReactNode is React's union of everything it can draw: elements, strings, numbers, arrays of nodes and the values that draw nothing. It is the usual type for children.
  5. A The type of the image property of Site: an object with url, alt, width and height: An indexed access type names the type of one property of another type. Here it is the object with url, alt, width and height, and it follows the API's type if that ever changes.

Exit Ticket

  1. Banner: interface BannerProps { title: string; tone?: 'info' | 'warning' } and function Banner({ title, tone = 'info' }: BannerProps).
  2. children: the content between the component's tags, passed as a prop; typed ReactNode (anything React can draw).
  3. SiteCard versus Badge: SiteCard shows a site, so the domain object is its clearest contract (and keeps working when the card shows one more property); Badge is general-purpose and takes plain values so any page can use it.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (two arguments). Still thinks of components as functions with positional parameters. Show the compiled _jsx(SiteName, { ... }) again.
  • Q4 C (label?.toUpperCase()). Makes the error go away instead of deciding what the screen should show. Ask: what does the first TideTime draw now?
  • Q6 A (TS2540). Expects TypeScript to guard every rule. Name the division of labor: types for shapes, the linter for React's rules, tests for behavior.
  • Q9 B (site: Site). Has not separated domain components from general ones; walk through the design table with Badge on the home page.
  • Q10 A (nothing catches it). Did not connect noUnusedParameters with the empty layout; rerun Step 7's second break.
  • Lab Task 1: tone?: string kept, @ts-expect-error deleted. Edited the test instead of the type: the test file is the contract; put it back and read TS2578 again.

3.3 · Lists and Conditional Rendering: self-check

Q1
Where does the key go in events.map((event) => <li><EventCard event={event} /></li>)?
A) Inside EventCard, on its article
B) On the <li>, the element the map callback returns
C) On the <ul> around the list
D) Anywhere inside the item: React finds it
Why: React compares the elements map returns, so the key belongs on the <li> (key={event.id}). A key inside EventCard is on the wrong element, and EventCard never receives a key prop anyway (Lesson 3.3, Putting the key inside the item component).
A is wrong: EventCard draws one item; the list's items are the <li> elements.
C is wrong: The <ul> is one element, not an item of the list.
D is wrong: React reads the key only on the elements in the array.
Q2
Two lookup tables are typed Record<EventStatus, string>. tsc prints this. What are the two fixes?
src/Scratch.tsx:3:7 - error TS2741: Property 'draft' is missing in type '{ scheduled: string; completed: string; cancelled: string; }' but required in type 'Record<EventStatus, string>'.
src/Scratch.tsx:13:3 - error TS2561: Object literal may only specify known properties, but 'canceled' does not exist in type 'Record<EventStatus, string>'. Did you mean to write 'cancelled'?
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:3
Add a draft entry to the first table; spell the key cancelled (the API's word) in the second
Why: TS2741 names the missing key; TS2561 names the unknown one and suggests the right spelling. The table must match the API, not the dictionary: American English writes canceled, the API's EventStatus says cancelled (Lesson 3.3, Lookup Tables).
Q3
WaitingList renders <div>{waiting.length && <p>{waiting.length} people are waiting for a spot.</p>}</div>. With nobody waiting, what is on the page?
A) An empty div
B) A TypeScript error, because a number is not an element
C) <div>0</div>
D) An oxlint warning, and an empty div
Why: && returns its left side when it is falsy, and 0 is a number React draws. tsc and oxlint both accept it (real output: <div>0</div>). Write waiting.length > 0 && ... (Lesson 3.3, The 0 trap).
A is wrong: false, null and undefined draw nothing; 0 draws.
B is wrong: A number is a valid ReactNode.
D is wrong: oxlint 1.93 has no rule for it: no tool warns you.
Q4
SpotsLine has a switch on availability.kind. A teammate adds a 'few' kind to the Availability union but no case for it. What happens?
A) npx tsc -b reports the missing case
B) React throws while drawing the card
C) oxlint warns about a non-exhaustive switch
D) It compiles, and cards of that kind silently lose their spots line
Why: A component may return undefined, so a switch with a missing case compiles (the kit ran it: npx tsc -b printed nothing). A lookup table would complain; a switch does not until Lesson 5.1's never check (Lesson 3.3, Exercise 3).
A is wrong: That is exactly what tsc does NOT do yet.
B is wrong: undefined draws nothing; nothing throws.
C is wrong: No such rule is on in the course's oxlint.
Q5
Predict what npx tsc -b says about this table, and how to fix the table itself.
const loose: Record<string, string> = {
  draft: 'Draft',
  scheduled: 'Scheduled',
}

export function LooseLabel({ status }: { status: string }) {
  return <span>{loose[status].toUpperCase()}</span>
}
error TS2532: Object is possibly 'undefined'.: with any string as a key nothing guarantees the entry exists; key the table by the union (Record<EventStatus, string>)
Why: noUncheckedIndexedAccess (Lesson 2.4) makes every lookup in a Record<string, T> possibly undefined, and nothing checks the table is complete. Keyed by the union, a lookup is a plain string (Lesson 3.3).
Q6
A map callback was written with braces, and tsc prints this first line. What is the fix?
src/Scratch.tsx:6:7 - error TS2322: Type 'void[]' is not assignable to type 'ReactNode'.
A) Parentheses instead of braces, (item) => (<li ...>), or a return inside the braces
B) Annotate the result as JSX.Element[]
C) Move the key to the <ul>
D) Wrap the <li> in a fragment
Why: An arrow function with braces needs a return; without it every item is undefined (void), and void[] is not something React can draw (Lesson 3.3, Common Mistakes).
B is wrong: The annotation would only add a second error: the callback still returns nothing.
C is wrong: Keys are another topic; the list is empty because nothing is returned.
D is wrong: A fragment that is not returned is still not returned.

3.4 · State and Events: self-check

Q7
tsc prints this for StatusPicker. Which line made TypeScript reject 'finished'?
src/Scratch.tsx:10:52 - error TS2345: Argument of type '"finished"' is not assignable to parameter of type 'SetStateAction<StatusFilter>'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:10
A) setStatus('finished') itself: setters only take string literals
B) useState<StatusFilter>('all'): the type argument holds every update to the union
C) type StatusFilter = EventStatus | 'all', because it has five words
D) The button's type="button"
Why: useState('all') would widen the state to string and accept any word; the type argument makes the setter take a SetStateAction<StatusFilter> (Lesson 3.4, When useState Needs a Type).
A is wrong: Without the type argument the same call compiles.
C is wrong: The union is the list of allowed words; it is fine.
D is wrong: The button's type has nothing to do with state.
Q8
const [savedIds, setSavedIds] = useState([]) and the first button that adds an id gives this. What did TypeScript infer, and what is the fix?
src/Scratch.tsx:7:55 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'never'.
never[] (an empty array tells TypeScript nothing); useState<string[]>([])
Why: An array that can hold nothing at all: every element you add is 'not assignable to never'. Say what it will hold with a type argument (Lesson 3.4, The state starts as an empty array).
Q9
A named handler, function handleChange(event) { setQuery(event.target.value) }, gives this. What does the parameter need?
src/Scratch.tsx:6:25 - error TS7006: Parameter 'event' implicitly has an 'any' type.
A) event: any
B) event: Event
C) event: ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>, imported with type from react
D) Nothing: move it into a useState call
Why: A handler written in place (onChange={(event) => ...}) gets its type from the prop; a named function has no context, so annotate it with React's event type for the element (Lesson 3.4, Typed Event Handlers).
A is wrong: any silences TS7006 and switches the checks off.
B is wrong: React passes its own synthetic ChangeEvent, not the DOM's Event: the types do not match.
D is wrong: Hooks hold state; they do not type handlers.
Q10
A terrain menu and the list of sites it filters sit side by side inside SiteFinder. Where does the chosen terrain live?
A) In the menu component, where the choice happens
B) In a module-level variable both can read
C) In both components, kept in sync with effects
D) In SiteFinder, the closest common parent, passed down as props
Why: Siblings cannot reach into each other; data flows down. The parent holds the state, the menu becomes a controlled component with a value and an onChange callback, and the list is derived (Lesson 3.4, Where State Lives; Lab 3.4, Task 2).
A is wrong: Then the list never hears about it (the lab's starter).
B is wrong: A plain variable does not make React draw again.
C is wrong: Two copies of one fact fall out of step; keep one source of truth.
Q11
npm run lint prints this (an error, not a warning). What does it ask you to do?
× react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "useState" is called conditionally. React Hooks must be called in the exact same order in every component render.
A) Move the useState call above the early return, so it runs on every render
B) Rename the Hook so it starts with use
C) Wrap the useState call in a useEffect
D) Add a key to the textarea
Why: React tells a component's state apart by the order of its Hook calls; a Hook after an early return runs on some renders and not others (Lesson 3.4, The Rules of Hooks).
B is wrong: useState already starts with use.
C is wrong: Hooks may not be called inside other Hooks' callbacks either.
D is wrong: Keys are for lists.
Q12
A search box stores both query and the filtered list, and handleChange does setQuery(value) then setVisible(events.filter(... includes(query) ...)). Why is the count always one keystroke behind, and what is the fix?
query in the handler is this render's snapshot, the text BEFORE the keystroke; keep only query in state and derive the list during render
Why: The setter asks for a new render; it does not change the variable you already have. Less state is the fix: one source of truth, nothing to fall out of date (Lesson 3.4, Derive, don't store; Lab 3.4, Task 3).

The solution's list and banner:

import type { DebrisCount } from './api/types.ts'
import { debrisLabels } from './labels.ts'

interface DebrisListProps {
  items: DebrisCount[]
}

// Task 1: the counts as a list, biggest first, each with its label (Task 2's table).
export function DebrisList({ items }: DebrisListProps) {
  if (items.length === 0) {
    return <p className="empty-state">Nothing counted yet.</p>
  }

  const sorted = items.toSorted((a, b) => b.count - a.count)
  return (
    <ol className="debris-list">
      {sorted.map((item) => (
        <li key={item.category}>
          {debrisLabels[item.category]}: {item.count}
        </li>
      ))}
    </ol>
  )
}
// Task 3: what the crew hears on the morning of a cleanup, and who is waiting for a spot.
export type TideCall =
  | { kind: 'go' }
  | { kind: 'delayed'; minutes: number }
  | { kind: 'cancelled'; reason: string }

interface TideBannerProps {
  call: TideCall
  waiting: string[]
}

export function TideBanner({ call, waiting }: TideBannerProps) {
  return (
    <section aria-label="Tide call">
      <CallLine call={call} />
      {waiting.length > 0 && <p>{waiting.length} people are waiting for a spot.</p>}
    </section>
  )
}

function CallLine({ call }: { call: TideCall }) {
  switch (call.kind) {
    case 'go':
      return <p>On as planned.</p>
    case 'delayed':
      return <p>Starts {call.minutes} minutes late: the tide is still high.</p>
    case 'cancelled':
      return <p role="alert">Cancelled: {call.reason}</p>
  }
}

What each task shows: (1) toSorted returns a new array, sort changed the report the test checks afterwards; the key stays on the <li>; the empty list returns a sentence early. Ties keep the API's order (glass before foam: both 76); (2) once the table is Record<DebrisCategory, string>, tsc lists the seven missing categories by name and debrisLabels.straws becomes an error, so the test's @ts-expect-error is used; the labels are the API's own (GET /api/meta), so a learner who writes "Fishing gear" fails the test, not the type check; (3) the starter drew a 0 for nobody waiting and NOTHING for a cancelled call, and neither tsc nor oxlint said a word: tests catch behavior.

The solution's picker update, the controlled menu's handler and the parent:

import { useState } from 'react'
import type { Site } from './api/types.ts'
import { TerrainFilter, type TerrainChoice } from './TerrainFilter.tsx'

// Task 2: the state lives in the closest common parent; the visible list is derived, never stored
export function SiteFinder({ sites }: { sites: Site[] }) {
  const [terrain, setTerrain] = useState<TerrainChoice>('all')
  const visible = sites.filter((site) => terrain === 'all' || site.terrain === terrain)

  return (
    <>
      <TerrainFilter value={terrain} onChange={setTerrain} />
      <p role="status">
        Showing {visible.length} of {sites.length} sites.
      </p>
      <ul>
        {visible.map((site) => (
          <li key={site.id}>{site.name}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </>
  )
}

What each task shows: (1) push + set hands React the same array (no redraw) and useState([]) is never[]; the fix is useState<string[]>([]) and setJoined((current) => current.includes(id) ? current.filter(...) : [...current, id]); (2) the menu becomes controlled and narrows with isTerrain (no as), the parent holds useState<TerrainChoice>('all') and derives visible; (3) the stored list filtered with the previous keystroke's text (after g, then gl: it shows the matches for g, four); deriving removes the bug and a piece of state. Accept onChange={(event) => onChange(isTerrain(event.target.value) ? event.target.value : 'all')} inline: it narrows the same way.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.3 · Lists and Conditional Rendering
  1. B To match each item with the one it drew last time, so it can keep, update, move, add or remove items correctly: React compares the new list with the last one by key: same key means the same item, kept and updated; a new key is created; a missing key is removed. That is why keys must be stable and unique among siblings.
  2. D A div containing the character 0: The && expression evaluates to its falsy left side, the number 0, and React draws numbers. Write signups.length greater than 0 so the left side is a real boolean.
  3. C npx tsc -b reports TS2741: property 'draft' is missing: Record with a union of keys requires every key. tsc reports TS2741, Property draft is missing, so the table cannot fall out of date with the type.
  4. A { kind: 'failed'; message: string }: Checking the discriminant narrows the union to the one member whose kind is failed, so state.message is a string you can read safely.
  5. D key={event.id}: The API's id is unique, comes from the data and never changes for the same event, which is exactly what a key needs to be.
Lesson 3.4 · State and Events
  1. C 1: All three calls read the snapshot of this render, where bags is 0, so each asks for 1. Updater functions that add one to the current value would chain and give 3.
  2. A string: The DOM gives every select value as a string, whatever the options say. That is why EventFilters checks it with isEventStatus before passing it up as a StatusFilter.
  3. D In EventsPage, the closest common parent: EventsPage is the closest common parent of everything that reads or changes query. It passes the value down as props and a callback for changes, and derives the lists from it.
  4. B never[]: An empty array literal gives TypeScript nothing to go on, so the state is never[] and adding any id is a TS2322 error. Write useState with a type argument, such as an array of string.
  5. A npx tsc -b reports TS2322, and in the browser React stops with "Too many re-renders": setOpen(true) runs while the button is drawn and returns void, which is not a MouseEventHandler (TS2322). Run anyway, it sets state during render, again and again, until React stops with Too many re-renders.

Exit Ticket

  1. The table: const terrainLabels: Record<Terrain, string> = { sand: 'Sandy beach', rocky: 'Rocky shore', marsh: 'Tidal marsh', mixed: 'Sand and rocks' } (any labels; the four keys are what TypeScript checks).
  2. useState('all'): the state widens to string, so any word is accepted (and the errors appear where the wider type is used); useState<StatusFilter>('all').
  3. The two questions: can it be worked out from what you already have (then derive it, it is not state)? Who reads it and who changes it (put it in the closest component above all of them, no higher)?

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (key inside EventCard). Thinks of the key as the item's own data; show that React reads keys only on the array map returns.
  • Q3 A (an empty div). Treats 0 like false. Rerun the lesson's two renders: <div>0</div>.
  • Q4 A (tsc reports it). Over-trusts the compiler with switches; contrast with the table's TS2741.
  • Q7 A or C. Has not connected the type argument with what the setter accepts; hover the setter with and without it.
  • Q10 A or C. Keeps state where the event happens; draw the round trip.
  • Q12 "React is slow" or "setState is asynchronous". Close, but the model is the snapshot: the handler reads this render's value.

3.5 · Forms and Actions: self-check

Q1
Which field should be a CONTROLLED input (value + onChange)?
A) The email on a one-time sign-up form
B) A note field that is only read when the form is sent
C) The description, when a live "N of 2000 characters" count sits under it
D) Every field in every form: it is the React way
Why: Controlled inputs are for values the screen needs while someone types (a count, a live search, fields that depend on each other); fill-in-and-submit fields are less code as uncontrolled fields read through FormData (Lesson 3.5, Controlled and Uncontrolled Inputs).
A is wrong: Read once at submit: uncontrolled with a name is enough.
B is wrong: Same: nothing needs it before the submit.
D is wrong: Neither kind is "the React way"; each fits a job.
Q2
readTitle declares const title: string = formData.get('title'), and tsc prints this. Which fix is safe?
src/Scratch.tsx:2:9 - error TS2322: Type 'FormDataEntryValue | null' is not assignable to type 'string'.
  Type 'null' is not assignable to type 'string'.
A) typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : '' (narrow it)
B) formData.get('title') as string
C) formData.get('title')!
D) String(formData.get('title'))
Why: get returns FormDataEntryValue | null: a string, a File or null. typeof narrows it, and a missing field becomes '' that your checks report (Lesson 3.5, What formData.get really returns).
B is wrong: It compiles and crashes on a misspelled name: Cannot read properties of null.
C is wrong: The ! is the same promise as as string, with less to read.
D is wrong: String(null) is the text "null": a title nobody typed.
Q3
A teammate writes const value = formData.get('titel') as string and then value.trim() in an action. What do npx tsc -b and npm run lint say, and what happens when someone presses Save?
Both tools pass it; the action crashes in the browser: Uncaught TypeError: Cannot read properties of null (reading 'trim')
Why: There is no field named titel, so get returns null, and as string told TypeScript not to worry. One typeof check turns the crash into a message (Lesson 3.5; Lab 3.5 Task 1 is the same bug).
Q4
The waiting-list action was written as joinWaitingList(formData: FormData, _previous: JoinState) and tsc prints this first line. What is wrong?
src/Scratch.tsx:18:46 - error TS2769: No overload matches this call.
A) useActionState needs a type argument
B) The action's parameters are in the wrong order: previous state first, then the FormData
C) JoinState must be an interface, not a union
D) The form needs onSubmit as well as action
Why: An action for useActionState gets (previousState, formData); a plain form action gets (formData). Read the long message from the bottom: a { kind: "idle" } object is not a FormData (Lesson 3.5, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: The state type is inferred from the action's return type.
C is wrong: A union is exactly right for the state.
D is wrong: With an action React does the submit handling.
Q5
NewEventForm's fieldProps sets defaultValue: values[field]. What would happen without it, after a Publish with mistakes?
A) Nothing: React keeps what was typed in uncontrolled fields
B) tsc reports that defaultValue is missing
C) The messages would not appear
D) The messages appear, and every field is empty again: React resets the form after every action
Why: React resets the form after each action; a field goes back to its defaultValue. The state remembered the values; without defaultValue nothing puts them back. Neither tool catches it (Lesson 3.5, Exercise 2; the kit ran tsc and lint on that change: both silent).
A is wrong: That was true for onSubmit forms; actions reset the form.
B is wrong: defaultValue is optional: tsc has no reason to complain.
C is wrong: The messages come from the state, which still has them.
Q6
Both submit buttons have name="status" (value draft and value scheduled). How does planCleanup know which was pressed?
A) formData.get('status') holds the value of the button that submitted the form
B) React passes the button as a third argument
C) It cannot: two buttons need two forms
D) From event.submitter in an onSubmit handler
Why: The submitting button's name and value are part of the FormData. Enter in a text field presses the FIRST submit button, so Save as draft (the safe choice) comes first (Lesson 3.5, Step 3).
B is wrong: An action gets only the previous state and the FormData.
C is wrong: One form, two submits is the lesson's design.
D is wrong: There is no onSubmit: the form uses action.
Q7
Someone changes fieldProps to return 'aria-invalid': 'yes'. tsc reports 8 errors; this is the end of the first. What does it tell you about spread props?
Types of property '"aria-invalid"' are incompatible.
Type 'string' is not assignable to type '"false" | "grammar" | "spelling" | "true" | boolean | undefined'.
Every spread property is still type-checked against what the element accepts: aria-invalid takes a boolean or "true"/"false" (and grammar, spelling), not 'yes'
Why: {...fieldProps('title')} is not a way around the checks: TypeScript compares the returned object with the element's props, once per field that uses it (Lesson 3.5, Step 3).
Q8
The form has NO noValidate, the capacity input has max={200}, and an organizer types 500 and presses Publish. What happens?
A) The action runs and shows the form's capacity message
B) Chrome blocks the submit and shows its own bubble; the action never runs
C) React throws a validation error
D) The API rejects it
Why: The browser's own checks run first and stop the submit ("Value must be less than or equal to 200." in Chrome). noValidate turns the bubbles off so every message comes from one place and stays on the page (Lesson 3.5, Three places that check).
A is wrong: The action never sees it.
C is wrong: React does not validate fields.
D is wrong: Nothing is sent: the browser stopped it before the action.
Q9
shoreline-api answers a draft with these mistakes like this. Why does the form keep its errors in exactly this shape?
{
  "fieldErrors": {
    "title": [
      "Use at least 5 characters for the title."
    ],
    "date": [
      "Enter a date as YYYY-MM-DD."
    ],
    "endTime": [
      "The end time must be after the start time."
    ],
    "meetingPoint": [
      "Say where volunteers meet (at least 3 characters)."
    ]
  }
}
A) JSON requires arrays
B) So that in Lesson 7.2 the API's answer can be shown with the same FieldError component, field by field
C) TypeScript cannot type objects of strings
D) So the browser can show the messages in its bubbles
Why: FieldErrors = Partial<Record<EventField, string[]>> is the API's fieldErrors with checked keys. The kit ran the form's validateEvent and the API on the same mistakes: the same four fields, most messages word for word (the empty date is reworded for people).
A is wrong: Arrays are a choice: a field can have several messages.
C is wrong: It can; that is what Record does.
D is wrong: noValidate turns the bubbles off on purpose.
Q10
What connects the capacity field to its message for a screen reader?
A) The message's red color
B) Putting the message right under the field
C) A title attribute on the field
D) aria-invalid="true" on the field and aria-describedby pointing at the message's id
Why: aria-invalid announces the field as invalid; aria-describedby makes the reader read the message with the field's name. Color and position help people who see them (Lesson 3.5, Messages that assistive technology can find).
A is wrong: Color alone tells a screen reader nothing.
B is wrong: Position is visual; the markup must connect them.
C is wrong: title is not read reliably and is not the message.
Q11
In NewEventForm, {...fieldProps('title')} became {...fieldProps('titel')}. Why is this a compile error instead of a field that is always empty?
src/components/NewEventForm.tsx:66:45 - error TS2345: Argument of type '"titel"' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EventField'.
fieldProps takes an EventField, a union of the eight field names, so a misspelled name is not a valid argument (TS2345)
Why: Without the union the field would be named titel, readValues would read title (missing, so ''), and the form would say "Add a title." for a title you typed (Lesson 3.5, Step 1 and Step 5).
Q12
What disables Save as draft and Publish while an async action is still running?
A) A useState(false) you set to true in the action
B) The browser, automatically
C) isPending, the third value of useActionState: true until the action's promise settles
D) noValidate
Why: isPending is React's: no extra state to forget to reset. Today's checks finish instantly; in Lesson 7.2 the action waits for the API and the disabled={isPending} line is already there (Lesson 3.5, isPending).
A is wrong: Possible, but more code and easy to get wrong; the Hook gives it to you.
B is wrong: Browsers do not disable buttons for you.
D is wrong: noValidate only turns off the browser's own checks.

The solution's reading and checking:

// formData.get()returns a string, a File (from a file input) or null (no such field)
function readText(formData: FormData, name: SignupField): string {
  const value = formData.get(name)
  return typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : ''
}

// Task 1: every field as trimmed text; a field that is missing (or a file) reads as ''.
export function readSignup(formData: FormData): SignupValues {
  return {
    name: readText(formData, 'name'),
    email: readText(formData, 'email'),
    shirt: readText(formData, 'shirt'),
    note: readText(formData, 'note'),
  }
}

// Task 2: a type guard for the four sizes (the <select> hands over any string)
export function isShirtSize(value: string): value is ShirtSize {
  return value === 'S' || value === 'M' || value === 'L' || value === 'XL'
}

// Task 2: one message per problem, under the field it belongs to. An empty object = nothing to fix.
export function validateSignup(values: SignupValues): SignupErrors {
  const errors: SignupErrors = {}
  function add(field: SignupField, message: string) {
    errors[field] = [...(errors[field] ?? []), message]
  }

  if (values.name === '') add('name', 'Add your name.')
  if (!values.email.includes('@')) add('email', 'Enter an email address, such as you@example.com.')
  if (!isShirtSize(values.shirt)) add('shirt', 'Choose a shirt size.')
  if (values.note.length > 200) add('note', 'Keep the note to 200 characters or fewer.')
  return errors
}

and the form's field helper (the full file is in solution/src/SignupForm.tsx):

: its name, what to show after the reset, and a link to its message
  function fieldProps(field: SignupField) {
    const invalid = errors[field] !== undefined
    return {
      id: field,
      name: field,
      defaultValue: values[field],
      'aria-invalid': invalid,
      'aria-describedby': invalid ? `${field}-error` : undefined,
    }
  }

What each task shows: (1) get returns FormDataEntryValue | null; a missing field is null and as string turns that into the lesson's crash; the typeof check makes it ''; (2) the fieldErrors shape, a list per field, an empty object for "nothing to fix", the guard instead of as for the size; (3) after the action React resets the form: defaultValue from the state puts the typed values back, the select needs a key (React 19.3), and aria-invalid with aria-describedby pointing at email-error gives the field its accessible description (the test checks it with toHaveAccessibleDescription). Accept a separate FieldMessage component or inline paragraphs, as long as the ids match.

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 3.5 · Forms and Actions
  1. B Nothing: React reset the form to its default values: React resets the form after an action, and an uncontrolled field goes back to its defaultValue, here an empty string. That is why the project passes the submitted values back as defaultValue.
  2. D FormDataEntryValue | null: FormDataEntryValue is File or string, and get returns null when there is no such field. Narrow it with typeof value === string before using it.
  3. A The previous state, then the FormData: The previous state comes first, then the FormData. Swapping them gives TS2769, No overload matches this call, because the state is not a FormData.
  4. C So that the chosen site survives the form reset: a new key makes a new select that starts with the newest value: In React 19.3 a select's defaultValue is applied only when the select is created, so after the reset it went back to Choose a site. A new key makes a new select with the newest defaultValue.
  5. B Because anyone can send it a request without the form, so the server must never trust what arrives: Anyone can send the API a request without your form, with curl or a script. Front-end checks are for people; the server is the authority and must check everything it receives.

Exit Ticket

  1. The reset: React resets the form after every action, so uncontrolled fields go back to their defaultValue; giving each field defaultValue from the submitted values (kept in the action's state) puts the text back.
  2. Reading a field: const value = formData.get('title') then typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : '' (no as).
  3. The two attributes: aria-invalid on the field and aria-describedby pointing at the message's id.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 D ("every field controlled"). Learned controlled inputs as the only React way; revisit the table and the sign-up sheet.
  • Q2 B (as string). Fixes the message, not the problem: run the TitleForm demo with the misspelled name.
  • Q5 A (React keeps the text). Still thinks in onSubmit terms; Exercise 2 live shows the reset.
  • Q8 A (the action shows its message). Does not know the browser checks first; try it without noValidate.
  • Q9 C or D. Has not linked the error shape to the API (Lesson 7.2 will show the same messages from the API).
  • Lab Task 3: value= instead of defaultValue=. Makes the fields controlled with no onChange: read-only fields and React's console message about a value without onChange (Lesson 3.4).

Session 8 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 3.5 Retrieval

  1. FormDataEntryValue | null (a string, a File, or null when there is no such field); narrow with typeof value === 'string'.
  2. The previous state first, then the FormData (swapped, tsc says TS2769: No overload matches this call).
  3. aria-invalid on the field, aria-describedby with the message's id.

3.6 · Styling and Accessible Components: self-check

Q1
Scratch.module.css has a .note rule. Which class does the browser see on the element that uses styles.note?
A) note
B) styles.note
C) _note_ followed by a short code made from the file and the line number
D) Scratch-module-note
Why: Vite renames every class in a module: an underscore, your name, a code from the file, the line (_note_1pozh_1 in the lesson). The same name in another module gets another code, so they cannot clash (the radio's "Driftwood lead").
A is wrong: That is global CSS: one namespace for the whole app.
B is wrong: That is the JavaScript expression; its VALUE is the generated name.
D is wrong: Not Vite's format; and you never type the generated name yourself.
Q2
A helper function noteClass(): string { return styles.note } gives this. Why is styles.note not a string?
src/Scratch.tsx:5:3 - error TS2322: Type 'string | undefined' is not assignable to type 'string'.
  Type 'undefined' is not assignable to type 'string'.

5   return styles.note
    ~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:5
vite/client types every module as CSSModuleClasses (any string key), and noUncheckedIndexedAccess adds undefined to every read: TypeScript cannot know the class exists
Why: className accepts string | undefined, so JSX is fine; a function that promises a string is not. It is the honest answer, not a bug (Lesson 3.6, What TypeScript knows about styles).
Q3
You change className={styles.note} to className={styles.ntoe}. What do npx tsc -b and npm run lint say, and what does the page show?
A) tsc reports TS2551 with a Did you mean
B) oxlint warns about an unknown class
C) The build fails, so the page keeps its old look
D) Both are silent; the note loses its border and its element has no class attribute
Why: Any key is allowed, so the typo is a valid string | undefined: undefined, which React leaves out. Run for real: tsc prints nothing and lint says Found 0 warnings and 0 errors. CSS Modules make clashes impossible, not typos.
A is wrong: Only with a declaration file (Exercise 3).
B is wrong: oxlint does not read CSS.
C is wrong: Nothing fails: that is the trap.
Q4
In Badge.tsx, className={${styles.badeg} ${styles[tone]}} (a typo). What does the Elements panel show as the badge's class?
A) class="_badge_1nzko_1 _info_1nzko_9"
B) class="undefined _info_1nzko_9"
C) No class attribute at all
D) Nothing: the badge is not rendered
Why: A template literal turns undefined into the text "undefined". The tone class still works, so the badge keeps its color and loses its pill shape. If you ever see undefined in a class, look for a misspelled module class (Exercise 2).
A is wrong: That is the correct name: the typo reads a key that does not exist.
C is wrong: That happens with className={styles.ntoe} alone: React drops an undefined className, but not a string.
D is wrong: It renders; only its styles change.
Q5
Style object or CSS Module class? (a) a bar as wide as the share of spots taken; (b) a darker color on hover; (c) the card's padding and border.
(a) a style object (the value comes from data); (b) and (c) a class in the component's module
Why: Inline styles are for values computed at run time. A style object cannot express :hover, :focus-visible, dark mode or a media query, and a page full of them is as hard to change as one big stylesheet.
Q6
tsc prints this for a typed style object. Which change fixes it?
src/Scratch.tsx:4:3 - error TS2353: Object literal may only specify known properties, and ''background-color'' does not exist in type 'Properties<string | number, string & {}>'.

4   'background-color': 'var(--kelp)',
    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:4
A) Write backgroundColor
B) Add as CSSProperties after the object
C) Remove the : CSSProperties annotation
D) Put the value in quotes twice
Why: TS2353 is the excess property check from Lesson 2.2: CSSProperties lists every property in camelCase. The annotation is what made TypeScript check the object before it reached style.
B is wrong: An assertion silences the check; the browser would still ignore the property.
C is wrong: Then nothing checks it until the style prop, or never.
D is wrong: The key is the problem, not the value.
Q7
className={styles.skip-link} gives four errors from one line. Why?
A) The module has no skip-link class
B) JavaScript reads it as styles.skip - link: a subtraction
C) CSS Modules forbid dashes
D) The import is missing
Why: styles.skip is possibly undefined, link is not a name, a subtraction gives a number, and className wants a string: four errors. Use camelCase (.skipLink); 'skip-link' in brackets would work but loses the dot.
A is wrong: Even with the class there, the dot syntax cannot read a name with a dash.
C is wrong: Dashes are valid CSS; only the dot access fails.
D is wrong: The import is there; the expression is the problem.
Q8
After a rename, tsc prints this. What is wrong?
src/components/Card.tsx:11:32 - error TS2339: Property 'card' does not exist on type 'typeof import("*.css")'.
Found 1 error in src/components/Card.tsx:11
A) The class is called something else in the CSS file
B) The component must import React
C) styles needs a type annotation
D) The file is Card.css: only *.module.css files are CSS Modules
Why: A plain .css import is global CSS with no class names to hand back, so vite/client types it as an empty module. Rename it Card.module.css and fix the import (Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: The CSS is never read by TypeScript.
B is wrong: The JSX transform needs no import.
C is wrong: An annotation would only hide the real problem.
Q9
With the jsx-a11y plugin on, npm run lint prints this for a site tile. What is the fix?
⚠ jsx-a11y(click-events-have-key-events): Enforce a clickable non-interactive element has at least one keyboard event listener.  src/Scratch.tsx:10:5
⚠ jsx-a11y(no-static-element-interactions): Static HTML elements with event handlers require a role.  src/Scratch.tsx:10:6
⚠ jsx-a11y(alt-text): Missing `alt` attribute.  src/Scratch.tsx:11:7
Found 3 warnings and 0 errors.
The right element: a <button type="button"> instead of the div, and alt="" on the image (the name is already there in text)
Why: Not three attributes on the div: a button is focusable, works with Enter and Space and is announced as a button. tsc said nothing: the types were fine. After the fix: Found 0 warnings and 0 errors.
Q10
The events page has fifteen buttons, each named "Show details". What does the lesson do?
A) Adds <VisuallyHidden> for {event.title}</VisuallyHidden> after the visible words
B) Adds aria-label="Show details" to each button
C) Adds a <span style={{ display: "none" }}> with the title
D) Adds a title attribute with the event's title
Why: Text that screen readers read but that takes no room: every name becomes unique ("Show details for Tide Pool Morning Cleanup") and the visible words still come first, so voice control can say "click Show details". No tool flagged the problem: each button had a name, just not a useful one.
B is wrong: Same name fifteen times again.
C is wrong: display: none hides the text from screen readers too.
D is wrong: A tooltip is not reliably part of the name and is invisible to keyboard users.
Q11
Inside the dark header the focus ring must be orange. How does the lesson do it?
A) A second :focus-visible rule inside Header.module.css
B) outline: none and a box-shadow
C) --focus: var(--sunrise) on .header: the token changes for everything inside, and the one global rule uses it
D) An inline style on every header link
Why: Custom properties are inherited, not scoped by modules: a module can SET a token for its subtree. The global ring reads var(--focus), so one rule serves the whole app.
A is wrong: Two focus rules to keep in step instead of one.
B is wrong: outline: none is the mistake the lesson warns about.
D is wrong: Inline styles cannot express :focus-visible at all.
Q12
Name the layers the lesson checks accessibility with, and one problem only a person finds.
The linter (jsx-a11y), the browser's audits (Lighthouse, axe DevTools), you (a keyboard pass, a screen reader), and later automated tests (Lesson 9.3). A person finds e.g. fifteen identical button names, a wrong alt text, a confusing order
Why: Automated tools are a floor, not a ceiling: they find what a rule can decide, and only some of it.

// Juniper Hill Community Garden: the plot board's data, described once (TODO A)

/** Where a plot is: a raised bed, a plot in the ground, or a bench in the greenhouse */
export type BedKind = 'raised' | 'ground' | 'greenhouse'

/** open = anyone can request it; assigned = a gardener has it this season; resting = left fallow this season */
export type PlotStatus = 'open' | 'assigned' | 'resting'

export interface Plot {
  id: string
  number: number
  bed: BedKind
  sizeSqFt: number
  sunHours: number
  status: PlotStatus
  /** Only assigned plots have a gardener */
  gardener?: string
  notes: string
}

/** What a valid request holds once the form's text has been checked */
export interface PlotRequest {
  name: string
  email: string
  bed: BedKind
  householdSize: number
}

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 3.6 · Styling and Accessible Components
  1. D A generated name that starts with _card_, unique to that file: Vite renames each class to an underscore, the name you wrote, a short code made from the file and the line number. No other file can produce the same name, so the rule cannot clash.
  2. B string | undefined: vite/client types every CSS Module as an index signature of strings, and noUncheckedIndexedAccess adds undefined to every read, because TypeScript cannot know whether the class exists.
  3. C An empty string, alt="": The text next to it already names the link, so the logo is decorative here. An empty alt tells screen readers to skip it, and the link is announced once as Shoreline Crew HQ.
  4. A Style the ring with :focus-visible instead of :focus: focus-visible shows the ring for keyboard focus and usually not after a mouse click, so the designer gets what they asked for and keyboard users keep the ring they navigate by.
  5. B The problems a rule can find are fixed; a keyboard pass and a screen reader check are still needed: Automated tools catch problems a rule can decide, and only some of all problems. Fifteen buttons named Show details passed both; a keyboard pass and a screen reader find what rules cannot judge.

Exit Ticket

  1. Modules: class names scoped to their file, so no clashes and no leftovers when a component goes; a misspelled class still compiles (string | undefined), so look at the page.
  2. Button vs div: reachable with Tab, works with Enter and Space, announced as a button with its name, shows the focus ring (any two).
  3. Record: the type has one key per member of the union, so npm run typecheck reports every table that lacks the new status until it has one.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("note"). Still thinks in global CSS. Open the Elements panel on their own page.
  • Q3 A (TS2551). Expects TypeScript to read CSS. Show the vite/client line: any key is allowed.
  • Q4 C (no class attribute). Mixes the two typos: React drops an undefined className, but a template literal writes the word.
  • Q6 B (as CSSProperties). Reaches for assertions to silence errors: the property would still be ignored by the browser.
  • Q10 B or C. Thinks a name is enough. Ask them to imagine the screen reader's list of fifteen buttons.
  • Q11 A. Duplicates rules instead of setting a token: show that custom properties are inherited.

Warm-up: Part 1 on One Page

Any two ideas per module with a real place in the learner's project. Typical answers:

ModuleIdeasWhere in the project

Part 1 Assessment: Written Part, Model Answers and Marking

10 points. The two outputs are real: tsc on a copy of the project's model solution with the change the question describes.

W1
A teammate runs node plots.ts on a file that has a type error, and Node prints a result. Why does Node not complain, and which command finds the error? (1)
Node removes the types and runs the JavaScript that is left: it never checks them. npx tsc --noEmit (here npm run typecheck, or npm run build, which runs tsc first) reads the types and reports the error.
Marking: The point needs both halves: running is not checking, and a tsc command.
W2
A learner shows the gardener's name in capitals and tsc prints the error below. What does it mean, and how do you fix it? (2)
src/components/PlotCard.tsx:26:77 - error TS18048: 'plot.gardener' is possibly 'undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/components/PlotCard.tsx:26
gardener is optional in Plot (gardener?: string), so its type is string | undefined, and checking plot.status === 'assigned' does not tell TypeScript that the name is there. Fix: check the property itself, plot.gardener !== undefined && ... (or plot.gardener?.toUpperCase()).
Marking: 1 point for "possibly undefined because it is optional", 1 for a fix that narrows. A non-null assertion ! or as string scores 0 for the fix.
W3
The garden adds a fourth status, 'archived', to PlotStatus. What does npm run typecheck do next, and why is that useful? (1)
It reports every Record<PlotStatus, ...> table that has no entry for 'archived' (here statusLabels and statusTones in labels.ts, TS2741). The compiler lists every place the new status needs a decision, before anything runs.
src/utils/labels.ts:12:14 - error TS2741: Property 'archived' is missing in type '{ open: string; assigned: string; resting: string; }' but required in type 'Record<PlotStatus, string>'.
src/utils/labels.ts:18:14 - error TS2741: Property 'archived' is missing in type '{ open: "success"; assigned: "info"; resting: "neutral"; }' but required in type 'Record<PlotStatus, BadgeTone>'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/utils/labels.ts:12
Marking: The point needs "it lists the tables missing the new key".
W4
Why does the plot list use key={plot.id} and not the array index? (1)
A key tells React which item is which between renders. When the filters remove or reorder plots, an index key would point at a different plot, so state such as an open details toggle would jump to the wrong card. An id stays with its plot.
Marking: Accept any answer that ties the key to the item's identity when the list changes.
W5
A board keeps const [shown, setShown] = useState(0) and updates it in useEffect(() => setShown(visible.length), [visible]). What is wrong, and what do you write instead? (2)
The count can be worked out from state on every render: storing it adds a second render and a moment where the number and the list disagree (and visible is a new array each render, so the effect runs every time). Write const shown = visible.length (derived, never stored).
Marking: 1 point for "derived value kept in state", 1 for the one-line fix.
W6
What is the type of formData.get('email'), and how do you get a string from it without as? (1)
FormDataEntryValue | null (a string, a File, or null when there is no such field). Narrow it: typeof value === 'string' ? value : ''.
Marking: The point needs the narrowing.
W7
A card has className={${styles.card} ${styles.cardd}} (a typo). What does npx tsc -b say, and what does the browser's Elements panel show? (1)
Nothing: every key of a CSS Module is typed string | undefined, so the typo compiles. The class attribute shows the real card class followed by the word undefined, a class that matches no rule.
Marking: The point needs both: no error, and undefined in the class.
W8
<div onClick={toggle}>Show details</div> works with a mouse. Name two things <button type="button"> gives you that the div does not. (1)
Any two of: it can be reached with Tab; Enter and Space press it; screen readers announce it as a button with its name; it shows the focus ring; jsx-a11y stops warning.
Marking: Half a point each, rounded down.

spotsText, a switch with no default (tsc then knows every status returns):

import type { WorkDay } from './types.ts'

export function spotsText(day: WorkDay): string {
  switch (day.status) {
    case 'open':
      return day.spotsLeft === 1 ? '1 spot left' : `${day.spotsLeft} spots left`
    case 'full':
      return 'Full'
    case 'cancelled':
      return 'Cancelled'
  }
}

WorkDayList: one piece of state, the list and the count derived:

import { useState } from 'react'
import type { WorkDay } from './types.ts'
import { spotsText } from './workDays.ts'

interface WorkDayListProps {
  days: WorkDay[]
}

export function WorkDayList({ days }: WorkDayListProps) {
  const [openOnly, setOpenOnly] = useState(false)
  // Derived from the one piece of state: never stored
  const visible = openOnly ? days.filter((day) => day.status === 'open') : days

  return (
    <>
      <p>
        <input
          id="open-only"
          type="checkbox"
          checked={openOnly}
          onChange={(event) => setOpenOnly(event.target.checked)}
        />
        <label htmlFor="open-only">Hide full and cancelled days</label>
      </p>
      <p role="status">
        Showing {visible.length} of {days.length} work days
      </p>
      <ul>
        {visible.map((day) => (
          <li key={day.id}>
            {day.title}, {day.day}: {spotsText(day)}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </>
  )
}

SignUpForm: the action returns the next state; the message is linked to the field:

import { useActionState } from 'react'
import type { WorkDay } from './types.ts'

type SignUpState = { kind: 'editing'; error?: string } | { kind: 'done'; name: string }

// The form's action: the previous state and what was submitted give the next state
function signUp(_previous: SignUpState, formData: FormData): SignUpState {
  const value = formData.get('name')
  const name = typeof value === 'string' ? value.trim() : ''
  return name === '' ? { kind: 'editing', error: 'Enter your name.' } : { kind: 'done', name }
}

interface SignUpFormProps {
  day: WorkDay
}

export function SignUpForm({ day }: SignUpFormProps) {
  const [state, formAction] = useActionState(signUp, { kind: 'editing' })
  const error = state.kind === 'editing' ? state.error : undefined

  return (
    <>
      <p role="status">
        {state.kind === 'done' && `Thanks, ${state.name}: you are signed up for ${day.title}.`}
      </p>
      <form action={formAction} noValidate>
        <label htmlFor="name">Your name</label>
        <input
          id="name"
          name="name"
          aria-invalid={error !== undefined}
          aria-describedby={error !== undefined ? 'name-error' : undefined}
        />
        {error !== undefined && <p id="name-error">{error}</p>}
        <button type="submit">Sign up</button>
      </form>
    </>
  )
}
TaskPointsHow to score
Task 1: the spots line3Test 1 passes (2); one entry or branch per status, no default that hides a missing one (1).
Task 2: the list and its filter4Tests 2 and 3 pass (1 each); the visible list and the count derived from ONE piece of state (1); keys from day.id (1).
Task 3: the sign-up form3Tests 4 and 5 pass (1 each); the message is linked with aria-describedby and the field marked aria-invalid (1).

Accept a Record<WorkDayStatus, ...> or an if chain for Task 1 if every status is handled; a stored count (useState + an effect) loses Task 2's derived point; an error shown only with color or not linked loses Task 3's last point.

Self-check: Part 1 Review

Items 2, 8 and 10 show real tsc output (a strict project like the course app); item 4's code was checked and run: tsc is silent and Node prints the first plot's notes:

Next to the tool shed. Tomatoes grew well here last season.
Q1
Your page looks fine at localhost:5173, but the editor underlines a type error. Which command refuses to ship it?
A) npm run dev
B) npm run build
C) Reloading the browser
D) node server.ts
Why: npm run build is tsc -b && vite build: a type error stops it before anything is written. The dev server removes the types and serves the code without checking it (Lessons 1.1 and 1.2: running is not checking).
A is wrong: The dev server never checks types.
C is wrong: The browser only ever receives JavaScript.
D is wrong: That runs the API.
Q2
tsc prints this for a new line in the project. What happened, and what is the fix?
src/review.ts:3:14 - error TS2322: Type '"opne"' is not assignable to type 'PlotStatus'.
Found 1 error in src/review.ts:3
PlotStatus is a union of three words and 'opne' is not one of them; write 'open'
Why: A union of string literals allows exactly its words, so a misspelled status is an error before anything runs (Lessons 1.1 and 2.2; for a closer misspelling, such as Lesson 1.1's 'canceled', tsc adds a Did you mean). The Part 1 project's TODO A test checks exactly this with @ts-expect-error.
Q3
Plot declares gardener?: string. What is the type of plot.gardener?
A) string
B) string | null
C) string | undefined
D) any
Why: An optional property may be missing, and a missing property reads as undefined. Check it (plot.gardener !== undefined) before you use it as a string; the Part 1 written part asks about exactly this.
A is wrong: Only after narrowing.
B is wrong: null is a value someone sets on purpose; optional means it can be absent.
D is wrong: TypeScript never gives you any for a declared property.
Q4
With function first<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined, what does TypeScript infer for const plot = first(plots)?
import { plots } from './data/plots.ts'

function first<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined {
  return items[0]
}

const plot = first(plots)
console.log(plot?.notes)
A) Plot | undefined
B) Plot
C) unknown
D) T | undefined
Why: T is inferred from the argument (Plot[]), so the result is Plot | undefined (the list could be empty); hence plot?.notes. Run for real: tsc is silent and Node prints the first plot's notes.
B is wrong: The return type says it may be undefined.
C is wrong: Generics keep the type; unknown would throw it away.
D is wrong: T is replaced by the type of the argument at each call.
Q5
Data arrives from outside your code (a fetch, JSON.parse, localStorage). What do you do before treating it as a Plot?
A) Write as Plot
B) Nothing: the interface describes it
C) Annotate the variable as Plot
D) Keep it unknown and check it with a type guard
Why: Types are gone at run time and the data may not match. A guard (value is Plot) checks it once, at the edge, and narrows it for the rest of the code (Lesson 2.4; Module 8 does it with Zod).
A is wrong: An assertion is a promise nobody checks.
B is wrong: The interface describes what you expect, not what arrived.
C is wrong: An annotation on a JSON.parse result is the same unchecked promise.
Q6
A Card component should wrap any content between its tags. Which prop type does that?
A) children: string
B) children: ReactNode
C) content: JSX.Element[]
D) children: any
Why: ReactNode is anything React can render: text, numbers, elements, lists, null. Children are just a prop with a special syntax (Lesson 3.2).
A is wrong: Then no element could go inside.
C is wrong: Not how JSX passes what is between the tags.
D is wrong: any turns checking off.
Q7
Why key={plot.id} and not key={index} on the plot cards?
The key tells React which item is which between renders; when the filters change the list, an index points at a different plot (state such as an open toggle jumps cards), an id stays with its plot
Why: Keys come from the data's identity, stable and unique among siblings (Lesson 3.3).
Q8
A teammate adds a short-name table and tsc prints this. What does it tell you?
src/review.ts:3:14 - error TS2741: Property 'greenhouse' is missing in type '{ raised: string; ground: string; }' but required in type 'Record<BedKind, string>'.
Found 1 error in src/review.ts:3
A) The union BedKind has a typo
B) Records cannot hold strings
C) The table has no entry for 'greenhouse': a Record<BedKind, ...> needs one per member
D) The object must be declared with as const
Why: A Record over a union is a complete table: tsc lists the missing keys. Add a member to the union and every table that lacks it lights up (Lesson 3.3's statusBadges).
A is wrong: The union is fine; the table is incomplete.
B is wrong: The value type is string, which is what was given.
D is wrong: as const would not add the missing key.
Q9
The board shows "Showing 5 of 12 plots". Where should the 5 come from?
A) A useState updated in a useEffect when the filters change
B) A useState updated in each filter's onChange
C) visible.length, worked out on every render from the filter state
D) A global variable
Why: It can be derived from state, so it is never stored: the list and its count always agree, and there is nothing to keep in step (Lesson 3.4).
A is wrong: An extra render and a moment where list and count disagree.
B is wrong: Two places to update; one will be forgotten.
D is wrong: React does not re-render when a global changes.
Q10
A select passes event.target.value straight to setBed and tsc prints this. Which fix keeps the types honest?
src/review.tsx:7:53 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'string' is not assignable to parameter of type 'SetStateAction<BedFilter>'.
Found 1 error in src/review.tsx:7
A) Narrow it with a guard: isBedKind(value) ? value : 'all'
B) setBed(event.target.value as BedFilter)
C) Change the state to useState<string>
D) // @ts-ignore above the line
Why: A select hands over any string; the guard checks it is one of ours before it reaches state (the HQ's EventFilters does exactly this with isEventStatus).
B is wrong: An unchecked promise: a renamed option value would slip through.
C is wrong: Then every later use loses the union.
D is wrong: Silences the check and keeps the bug.
Q11
After a form action returns errors, why do the fields still show what the person typed?
React resets the form after every action, to each field's defaultValue; the action returns the typed values in its state and the fields use them as defaultValue (a select also needs a new key)
Why: Lesson 3.5: values + defaultValue keep the typing; without them every field empties after a failed submit.
Q12
In the HQ after Lesson 3.6, what lives in index.css and what lives in a CSS Module?
index.css: design tokens (with their dark-mode values), the base font, plain element rules and the :focus-visible ring. A module: each component's own classes (camelCase), or a shared look (Form, Button, Card)
Why: Page-wide rules where everyone looks for them; component rules next to their component, scoped. Custom properties cross module boundaries.

Exit Ticket

  1. Any idea with a place in their code. Weak answers name an idea without a file: ask "where?" at the hand-in.
  2. Their own; it should match the check-in's next step. If it does not, the check-in did not land: follow up in writing.
  3. Their own; collect them for the course feedback.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (npm run dev). Still trusts the dev server. Lesson 1.2's Exercise 1 again: the tagline as a number.
  • Q3 A (string). Has not connected ? with undefined. W2 of the written part tests exactly this.
  • Q5 A or C. Treats types as checks. Ask: what happens when the server sends "18"?
  • Q9 A or B. Stores derived values: the most common Part 1 project problem (rubric row 3).
  • Q10 B. Reaches for as: the rubric's row 1 scores it 0.
