Start Here · Part 1 Refresher

Not graded. Each reason says where the skill lives and where it comes back. R1, run for real in ts-lab:

> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

refresher.ts:4:16 - error TS2339: Property 'events' does not exist on type 'LoadState'.
  Property 'events' does not exist on type '{ kind: "loading"; }'.

4   return state.events.length
                 ~~~~~~


Found 1 error in refresher.ts:4
R1
npm run check in ts-lab reports an error for this function. What does TypeScript want?
import type { LoadState } from './shapes.ts'

export function eventCount(state: LoadState): number {
  return state.events.length
}
A) A narrowing check: state.kind === 'ready' before state.events
B) An annotation: state.events as CleanupEvent[]
C) The non-null operator: state.events!.length
D) Nothing: it is a bug in TypeScript
Why: Only the ready member has events. Check the discriminant first, and TypeScript narrows the state to that member. Skill: union narrowing. Where it comes back: Lesson 2.2; 4.1's LoadState<T> and every page that loads.
R2
What type does TypeScript give items here, and what should you write instead?
const [items, setItems] = useState([])
A) never[]: write useState<Site[]>([])
B) any[]: nothing to change
C) unknown[]: add as Site[]
D) Site[]: TypeScript reads the API
Why: An empty array tells TypeScript nothing about its items. Give the type argument when the start value is empty (or null). Skill: typing empty state. Where it comes back: Lesson 3.4; 4.1's useState<Site[]>([]) and useState<LoadState<…>>.
R3
Why does client.ts check API answers with functions like isCleanupEvent(value: unknown): value is CleanupEvent?
A) TypeScript cannot see data that arrives while the app runs, so a check must prove its shape
B) Because fetch returns a string
C) To make the code faster
D) Because TypeScript forbids any
Why: Types disappear before the code runs (Lesson 1.1). A type predicate is the runtime check that earns the type. Skill: data from outside is unknown. Where it comes back: Lesson 2.4; client.ts's isCleanupEvent and getJSON, which 4.1 extends.
R4
Where may a component call useState?
A) Anywhere, as long as it runs once
B) At the top level of the component, before any early return
C) Inside an if, when the value is needed
D) Only inside event handlers
Why: React matches Hook calls by their order on every render: same calls, same order. 4.1's events page returns early only after its three Hooks. Skill: Rules of Hooks. Where it comes back: Lesson 3.4; every effect and every custom Hook from 4.1 on.
R5
The events page shows "Showing 2 of 15". Where does the 2 come from?
A) A second useState updated in every handler
B) An effect that counts after each render
C) A calculation from state during render, never stored
D) A global variable
Why: Derived values are worked out on every render. Today's lesson adds the one place for code that is NOT pure: effects. Skill: pure rendering and derived values. Where it comes back: Lessons 3.1 and 3.4; 4.1's "You might not need an effect" table.
R6
Which markup lets a screen reader announce "Loading the beach sites…" without moving the focus?
A) <div className={styles.loading}>
B) <p role="status">
C) <p aria-hidden="true">
D) <h2 tabIndex={-1}>
Why: A status region is announced when its text changes. LoadStatus uses role="status" for loading and role="alert" for a failure. Skill: accessible status messages. Where it comes back: Lessons 3.5 and 3.6; 4.1's LoadStatus (role status / role alert).

4.1 · Effects and Fetching: self-check

Q1
Which of these jobs needs useEffect?
A) Working out how many cleanups match the search box
B) Hiding a card's details when Hide details is clicked
C) Keeping the browser tab's title in step while the sites page is open
D) Formatting a cleanup's date for its card
Why: The tab title is outside React: something to keep in step while the component is on the screen, with a cleanup that puts the old title back (Exercise 2). A value worked out from state is a plain calculation, and a click belongs in its handler: the lesson's "You might not need an effect" table.
A is wrong: Derived from state during render (Lesson 3.4); an effect would only add a second render.
B is wrong: That is the click's own handler.
D is wrong: A pure calculation: formatDate during render.
Q2
On the dev server, EffectLog logs in its effect and in its cleanup. What does the console show after a reload, and why?
import { useEffect } from 'react'

export function EffectLog() {
  useEffect(() => {
    console.log('effect: start')
    return () => console.log('effect: clean up')
  }, [])

  return <p>Open the console.</p>
}
effect: start, effect: clean up, effect: start: Strict Mode mounts, unmounts and mounts again, in development only
Why: The <StrictMode> in main.tsx proves that the cleanup really undoes the effect. The build runs each effect once. Fix a cleanup, never remove StrictMode.
Q3
npx tsc -b prints this for a component that writes useEffect(async () => { … }, []). What is the fix?
src/Scratch.tsx:8:13 - error TS2345: Argument of type '() => Promise<void>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EffectCallback'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:8
A) Annotate it as useEffect<Promise<void>>
B) Put an async function inside the effect and call it (or use .then)
C) Add getSites to the dependency array
D) Return the promise from a cleanup function
Why: An async function always returns a promise, and an effect may return only nothing or a cleanup function (void | Destructor). Keep the effect synchronous and start the async work inside it.
A is wrong: useEffect takes no type argument; the effect's type is EffectCallback.
C is wrong: Dependencies decide WHEN it runs, not what it may return.
D is wrong: A cleanup must be a function; React would call a promise.
Q4
oxlint warns about SiteEventCount. The parent switches siteId from Pelican Point to Long Strand. What does the page show?
  ⚠ react-hooks(exhaustive-deps): React Hook useEffect has a missing dependency: 'siteId'
    ╭─[src/Scratch.tsx:13:6]
 11 │   useEffect(() => {
 12 │     getEvents(`siteId=${siteId}`).then((found) => setCount(found.page.total))
    ·                         ───┬──
    ·                            ╰── useEffect uses `siteId` here
 13 │   }, [])
    ·      ──
 14 │
    ╰────
  help: Either include it or remove the dependency array.
A) Nothing: npx tsc -b refuses to build it
B) Long Strand's count, a moment later
C) An error message, because the request is canceled
D) Still Pelican Point's count: the effect never runs again
Why: The effect reads siteId but its array is empty, so it ran once, for the first site. Put siteId in the array: [siteId]. tsc has no opinion on dependencies; the linter does.
A is wrong: Types are fine; only the linter sees the dependency.
B is wrong: That is what [siteId] would do.
C is wrong: Nothing cancels it: nothing runs again at all.
Q5
With the API running, what does this print?
const response = await fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events?_status=500')
console.log(response.ok, response.status)
console.log(await response.json())
A) false 500, then the API's error object
B) It throws TypeError: Failed to fetch
C) true 500, then the error object
D) It throws an ApiError
Why: fetch rejects only when no answer arrives. An error status still resolves, with ok false: your code has to check it, which is why client.ts throws its own ApiError. Real output: false 500.
B is wrong: That is what happens with no answer at all (the API stopped).
C is wrong: ok is true only for statuses 200 to 299.
D is wrong: ApiError is the HQ's own class: fetch knows nothing about it.
Q6
Which type makes "show the data before it has arrived" impossible to write?
A) { loading: boolean; error?: string; data?: Site[] }
B) Site[] | null
C) { kind: string; data: Site[] }
D) { kind: 'loading' } | { kind: 'failed'; message: string } | { kind: 'ready'; data: Site[] }
Why: Only the ready member has data, so state.data is an error until state.kind === 'ready' narrows it. Lesson 2.2's LoadState, made generic: LoadState<T>.
A is wrong: Three independent fields allow loading AND an error AND data at once.
B is wrong: null cannot say whether it is loading or failed.
C is wrong: data is always there, even while loading.
Q7
Why does the project write .catch((error: unknown) => …) instead of .catch((error) => …)?
Without the annotation the rejection is any, so anything compiles (error.no.such.thing); unknown forces a check first
Why: describeError narrows it with instanceof ApiError (the API answered with an error status) and instanceof TypeError (no answer at all): two different messages for people, the details for the console.
Q8
In the sites page's .catch, why is if (controller.signal.aborted) return the first line?
A) Strict Mode needs it to run the effect twice
B) A request we canceled is not a failure to show anyone
C) abort() only throws in development
D) It retries the request once
Why: The cleanup aborts a request nobody is waiting for any more (the page left, or a dependency changed): the promise rejects with an AbortError, and that is expected, not an error message.
A is wrong: Strict Mode works either way; the check only keeps the canceled run quiet.
C is wrong: Aborting rejects the promise in development and in production.
D is wrong: Nothing retries; the next run of the effect asks again.
Q9
The Network tab on the DEV server shows two requests for /api/sites when /sites opens, the first one canceled. How many does npm run preview show, and why?
One: Strict Mode's start, clean up, start happens in development only
Why: The first request was aborted by Strict Mode's cleanup (net::ERR_ABORTED), which proves the cleanup works; the build runs the effect once.
Q10
With the API running, what does this print?
const response = await fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events?status=scheduled&when=upcoming&perPage=1')
const items = await response.json()
console.log(items.length, response.headers.get('X-Total-Count'))
1 8: one cleanup in the body, the total of 8 in the X-Total-Count header
Why: Lists come one page at a time; how many there are in all travels in the headers, which readPage reads into Page<T>. The home page shows it: Cleanups coming up: 8.
Q11
An effect fetches and sets state, and has NO dependency array at all. What do npx tsc -b and npm run lint say, and what happens?
A) The linter warns about the missing array
B) tsc reports that useEffect needs two arguments
C) Both pass; the Network tab fills with hundreds of requests
D) React stops the loop after two renders
Why: Render, effect, fetch, setState, render, effect… The lesson measured more than 700 requests in two seconds, with both tools quiet (the kit's run of the same file: 0 warnings, no type errors). Every effect gets an array, even [].
A is wrong: No rule fires for a missing array (the kit ran it: 0 warnings).
B is wrong: The second argument is optional.
D is wrong: Nothing stops it while the page is open.
Q12
A Try again button should show "Loading…" again. Where does setState({ kind: 'loading' }) belong?
A) In the click handler, next to the attempt + 1 that runs the effect again
B) As the first line of the effect
C) In the effect's cleanup
D) In the component body, before the return
Why: Setting state synchronously at the top of an effect renders twice for nothing, and the linter's react(set-state-in-effect) rule says so (Common Mistakes). The click that caused the change sets it (Exercise 1).
B is wrong: The set-state-in-effect warning.
C is wrong: The cleanup undoes the effect; it does not start the next one.
D is wrong: Setting state during render loops forever.

4.1 · Effects and Fetching: predict, then check

Run with Node against the course API (the kit's private copy; outputs show the learners' address).

P1
With the API running, what does Node print? (fetch works the same in Node and in the browser.)
const response = await fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events?_status=500')
console.log(response.ok, response.status)
console.log(await response.json())
false 500
{
  error: 'server_error',
  message: 'Simulated 500 response (the _status parameter).'
}
Why: fetch resolves on an error status: ok is false and the body is the API's error object. Only no answer at all rejects. That is why client.ts checks ok and throws its own ApiError.
P2
The request is canceled before its answer arrives. What does Node print?
const controller = new AbortController()
const answer = fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/sites?_delay=500', { signal: controller.signal })
controller.abort()
try {
  await answer
  console.log('arrived')
} catch (error) {
  console.log(error.name, controller.signal.aborted)
}
AbortError true
Why: abort() rejects the promise with an AbortError, and the signal says it was aborted. The sites page's catch checks exactly that first, and returns quietly.
P3
One cleanup per page. What does Node print?
const response = await fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events?status=scheduled&when=upcoming&perPage=1')
const items = await response.json()
console.log(items.length, response.headers.get('X-Total-Count'))
1 8
Why: The body holds one item (perPage=1); the X-Total-Count header holds how many there are in all (8 with the course's sample data). readPage reads both into Page<T>.

 ✓ src/lab.test.tsx (7 tests)

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 4.1 · Effects and Fetching
  1. B Once, after the page has first appeared on the screen: An effect runs after React has updated the screen, and an empty dependency array means only after the first render. The start, clean up, start sequence is Strict Mode, and only in development.
  2. D TS2345: Promise<void> is not assignable to void | Destructor: An async function returns a promise, and EffectCallback may only return nothing or a cleanup function. Put an async function inside the effect and call it, or use then and catch.
  3. A It resolves with a Response whose ok is false and status is 500: fetch rejects only when no answer arrives. A 500 is an answer, so the promise resolves with ok false and the error body. The HQ's request function checks ok and throws an ApiError.
  4. C Strict Mode ran the effect, its cleanup (which aborted the request), and the effect again: In development, StrictMode runs the effect, its cleanup and the effect again. The cleanup aborts the first request, the second one delivers the sites, and the production build sends only one.
  5. B Still Pelican Point's count, and the linter warned about the missing siteId: With an empty array the effect never runs again, so the count stays Pelican Point's. The linter's exhaustive-deps rule warns about exactly this; the fix is the array siteId.

Exit Ticket

  1. The three parts: the effect (runs after the screen is updated), the cleanup it returns (before the next run and when the component goes away), the array (run again when x changed).
  2. [] with siteId read: the effect never runs again, so a new site still shows the first site's data; oxlint's react-hooks(exhaustive-deps) warns; tsc is silent.
  3. Three states: loading ("Loading …", role status), failed (what could not be done and what to try, role alert; details in the console), ready (the data; an empty list is ready with zero items, with its own words).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or D. Reaches for an effect for derived values. Show the lesson's table: is there an outside system?
  • Q3 A or C. Treats the async error as a dependency or typing problem. Read void | Destructor aloud.
  • Q4 B. Believes React re-runs effects on any prop change. Rerun the missing-dependency demo with [siteId] and without.
  • Q5 B or D. Expects fetch to throw on an error status. P1 shows false 500.
  • Q11 A or B. Trusts the tools to catch the flood. Both were quiet in the kit's run; only the Network tab shows it.
  • Q12 B. The set-state-in-effect warning; the click handler owns "show Loading again".
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 11: Custom Hooks, Refs and the DOM

Answers and reasoning for every Session 11 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 11 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

W1
An effect reads siteId. What goes in its dependency array, and who tells you if you forget?
A) [siteId]; oxlint's exhaustive-deps
B) []; tsc
C) Nothing; React finds it
D) [siteId]; tsc
Why: Every value from the component that the effect reads. tsc is silent; the linter lists it (Lesson 4.1, Common Mistakes).
W2
The sites page's effect ends with return () => controller.abort(). When does React call that function?
A) Only when the page closes the browser tab
B) Before the effect runs again, and when the component goes away
C) Right after the request answers
D) Never in production
Why: The cleanup: before the next run and on unmount (and in Strict Mode's start, clean up, start in development).
W3
Four pages hold the same dozen lines of effect, with a different request each. What does today's first lesson do with them?
A) Copies them into App.tsx
B) Moves them into one custom Hook, useApi(key, load), with Try again built in
C) Replaces them with a library
D) Deletes the loading state
Why: Write it twice before extracting; the HQ has four copies. The Hook moves the code, not the safety: LoadState still forces the three states.

4.2 · Custom Hooks: self-check

Q1
Which of these should be a custom Hook?
A) formatDate(date): it turns a date string into words
B) LoadStatus: it returns the loading or error message
C) Code that calls useState and useEffect and returns a value to the component
D) The list of the six beach sites
Why: The lesson's table: calls no Hooks = a plain function; returns elements = a component; calls Hooks and returns values = a custom Hook, named use…
A is wrong: No Hooks inside: a plain function, like formatDate.
B is wrong: It returns elements: a component.
D is wrong: Data, not logic: it comes from the API.
Q2
npm run lint prints this (first lines). Which tool is it, how serious is it, and what is the fix?
  × react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "useEffect" is called in function "setTabTitle" that is neither a React function component nor a custom React Hook function. …
   ╭─[src/Scratch.tsx:5:3]
oxlint's rules-of-hooks, an ERROR (tsc is silent); rename setTabTitle to useTabTitle
Why: The linter finds Hooks by their names: only components and functions named use… may call Hooks. With the use name it also checks the rules INSIDE the Hook (the early-return mistake).
Q3
A toggle Hook ends with return [on, toggle]. tsc reports this. Which change fixes it?
src/Scratch.tsx:12:48 - error TS2322: Type 'boolean | (() => void) | undefined' is not assignable to type 'MouseEventHandler<HTMLButtonElement> | undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:12
A) return [on, toggle] as const
B) Annotate the caller: const [open, toggle]: boolean[] = useToggle(false)
C) Write onClick={toggle!}
D) Use useState<boolean>(initial) inside the Hook
Why: An array literal is inferred as an array of unions, so each destructured name is boolean | (() => void) | undefined. as const makes it a readonly tuple: a boolean first, a function second.
B is wrong: That would make toggle a boolean too.
C is wrong: ! removes undefined, not boolean.
D is wrong: The state was already boolean; the RETURN type is the problem.
Q4
The first try at useApi had load in its dependency array, and a page passed (signal) => getEvents(…). What happened?
A) npx tsc -b reported the function in the array
B) npm run lint reported an error
C) React warned in the console and stopped
D) Hundreds of requests, with both tools quiet: every render made a new function
Why: Two functions are never the same object (Object.is), so the effect ran after every render: render, new function, effect, answer, state, render… The lesson measured more than 700 requests in two seconds; the kit's run of the same file: 0 lint warnings, no type errors.
A is wrong: tsc has no opinion on dependencies.
B is wrong: load IS read by the effect, so the array is "right".
C is wrong: Nothing warns; only the Network tab shows it.
Q5
You type "pelican" in the events search with useDebouncedValue(query.trim(), 300). How many requests go out? And with the delay set to 0?
One (q=pelican) after the typing pauses; seven with delay 0, one per letter
Why: Each keystroke restarts the timer in the effect; only 300 ms of quiet lets the settled value change, and only a changed key makes useApi ask again (both measured in the lesson).
Q6
oxlint prints this for useApi. What do you change?
  ⚠ react-hooks(exhaustive-deps): Functions returned from `useEffectEvent` must not be included in the dependency array.
    ╭─[src/hooks/useApi.ts:32:21]
 31 │     return () => controller.abort()
 32 │   }, [key, attempt, loadLatest])
    ·                     ──────────
 33 │
    ╰────
A) Wrap loadLatest in useCallback
B) Remove loadLatest from the array: only the key and attempt decide when to load
C) Remove the array completely
D) Add load to the array as well
Why: A useEffectEvent function is new on every render and is not meant to trigger anything; in the array it brings back the endless requests. Values that should reload (the key, the attempt) go in the array.
A is wrong: useCallback is the older workaround; useEffectEvent replaces it here.
C is wrong: Then it runs after every render.
D is wrong: That is the flood again.

4.2 · Custom Hooks: predict, then check

P1
The toggle Hook now ends with return [on, toggle] as const. What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useState } from 'react'

function useToggle(initial: boolean) {
  const [on, setOn] = useState(initial)
  const toggle = () => setOn((current) => !current)
  return [on, toggle] as const
}

export function DetailsButton() {
  const [open, toggle] = useToggle(false)
  return (
    <button type="button" aria-expanded={open} onClick={toggle}>
      Details
    </button>
  )
}
(no output: no errors)
Why: as const makes the return a readonly tuple, [boolean, () => void]: open is a boolean, toggle a function, and onClick accepts it.
P2
A Hook returns early when the title is empty. What does npm run lint say?
import { useEffect } from 'react'

export function useTabTitle(title: string) {
  if (title === '') return
  useEffect(() => {
    document.title = title
  }, [title])
}
  × react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "useEffect" is called conditionally. React Hooks must be called in the exact same order in every component render.
   ╭─[src/Scratch.tsx:5:3]
 4 │       if (title === '') return
 5 │ ╭─▶   useEffect(() => {
 6 │ │       document.title = title
 7 │ ├─▶   }, [title])
   · ╰──── This Hook call is not reachable on every render path.
 8 │     }
   ╰────
  help: Move the Hook call before the condition, or call it unconditionally and branch inside the Hook/effect instead.

Found 0 warnings and 1 error.
Finished in …ms on 34 files with 150 rules using … threads.
Why: The name useTabTitle tells the linter it is a Hook, so it checks the Rules of Hooks inside it: an early return before a Hook is an ERROR. Call the Hook every time; put the condition inside the effect.

4.3 · Refs and the DOM: self-check

Q7
A Stop button needs the id of the running interval. Nothing on the screen shows it. Where do you keep it?
A) In useState, so React knows about it
B) In useRef: it survives renders and changing it renders nothing
C) In a module-level variable
D) In a prop from the parent
Why: The rule of thumb: if the screen should change when the value changes, it is state; otherwise it can be a ref (the pocket notebook, not the whiteboard). SweepTimer keeps its id in useRef<number | null>(null).
A is wrong: Works, but renders again for a value nobody sees.
C is wrong: Shared by every copy of the component.
D is wrong: The parent does not start the timer.
Q8
tsc reports this for a click handler. What is the smallest fix, and why is TypeScript right to ask?
src/Scratch.tsx:7:5 - error TS18047: 'noteRef.current' is possibly 'null'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:7
noteRef.current?.focus(): before the element is on the page (and after it leaves) current is null
Why: useRef<HTMLTextAreaElement>(null) is a RefObject<HTMLTextAreaElement | null>. In a click handler the element is there, but TypeScript cannot know; ?. costs nothing.
Q9
tsc rejects this callback ref (first lines). Why?
src/Scratch.tsx:18:11 - error TS2322: Type '(el: HTMLButtonElement | null) => () => boolean' is not assignable to type 'Ref<HTMLButtonElement> | undefined'.
  Type '(el: HTMLButtonElement | null) => () => boolean' is not assignable to type '(instance: HTMLButtonElement | null) => void | (() => VoidOrUndefinedOnly)'.
    Type '() => boolean' is not assignable to type 'void | (() => VoidOrUndefinedOnly)'.
      Type '() => boolean' is not assignable to type '() => VoidOrUndefinedOnly'.
        Type 'boolean' is not assignable to type 'VoidOrUndefinedOnly'.
A) A Map cannot hold buttons
B) Callback refs were removed in React 19
C) The ref must be created with useRef
D) The cleanup arrow returns delete's boolean; a ref cleanup must return nothing
Why: React 19 lets a callback ref return a cleanup function, which must return nothing (VoidOrUndefinedOnly). Without braces, () => map.delete(name) returns a boolean. Braces fix it; the same rule rejects the old ref={(el) => (refs.current[i] = el)}.
A is wrong: The Map is fine.
B is wrong: React 19 added the cleanup.
C is wrong: Callback refs are functions, not ref objects.
Q10
TextField renders a div, a label and an input, and takes a ref prop. Which element should the ref reach, who decides, and how is the prop typed?
The input (what a parent wants to focus); the component decides by putting ref on it; ref?: Ref<HTMLInputElement>
Why: In React 19, ref is an ordinary prop for function components (no forwardRef). Which element a parent may reach is part of the component's API, like its other props.
Q11
On /events (before this lesson's fix), you type pelican, Tab to Clear filters and press Enter. The button disappears. Where is the focus?
A) On the page's body: the next Tab starts again at the skip link
B) On the search box
C) On the first cleanup card
D) Still on the button, which React keeps hidden
Why: A focused element that disappears drops the focus to body (measured in the lesson). clearFilters now focuses the search box first: the search box is already there, only the button goes.
B is wrong: Only after the project's one-line fix.
C is wrong: Nothing moves it there.
D is wrong: The button is removed from the page.
Q12
The HQ's Dialog calls showModal() in an effect and focuses the opener in the cleanup. Which statement is true?
A) The cleanup is only for Strict Mode
B) The portal is what makes the page behind inert
C) Escape: the browser returns the focus itself; a button that removes the dialog: only the cleanup returns it
D) showModal() can be called during rendering
Why: Measured in the lesson: Escape closes the dialog natively and the browser restores the focus; unmounting does not, and without the cleanup the focus fell to body. The !dialog.open check is what Strict Mode needs.
A is wrong: Strict Mode needs the !dialog.open guard; the cleanup is for every close.
B is wrong: showModal() makes the page inert; the portal only places the element in body.
D is wrong: It is a DOM method on an element that exists only after rendering: an effect.

4.3 · Refs and the DOM: predict, then check

P3
Older code writes a ref without a starting value. What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useRef } from 'react'

export function OldTimer() {
  const timerRef = useRef<number>()
  return <p>{typeof timerRef}</p>
}
src/Scratch.tsx:4:20 - error TS2554: Expected 1 arguments, but got 0.

4   const timerRef = useRef<number>()
                     ~~~~~~

  node_modules/@types/react/index.d.ts:1744:24 - An argument for 'initialValue' was not provided.
    1744     function useRef<T>(initialValue: T): RefObject<T>;
                                ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:4
Why: React 19's useRef needs its starting value: write useRef<number | null>(null) (or undefined) and say what it starts as.
P4
A component takes ref as an ordinary prop (React 19). What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useRef, type Ref } from 'react'

interface TextFieldProps {
  id: string
  label: string
  /** React 19: ref is an ordinary prop */
  ref?: Ref<HTMLInputElement>
}

export function TextField({ id, label, ref }: TextFieldProps) {
  return (
    <div>
      <label htmlFor={id}>{label}</label>
      <input id={id} ref={ref} />
    </div>
  )
}

export function MeetingPointForm() {
  const meetingRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null)

  return (
    <>
      <TextField id="meeting" label="Meeting point" ref={meetingRef} />
      <button type="button" onClick={() => meetingRef.current?.focus()}>
        Edit the meeting point
      </button>
    </>
  )
}
(no output: no errors)
Why: ref?: Ref<HTMLInputElement> in the props, put on the input: no forwardRef needed. The parent writes ref={meetingRef} as with any element.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 4.2 · Custom Hooks
  1. A Each component sends its own request and keeps its own state: A custom Hook shares logic, not state. Each call has its own useState and its own effect, so each component sends its own request and keeps its own result. Sharing one answer needs a cache, which TanStack Query adds in Lesson 7.1.
  2. C npm run lint, with a rules-of-hooks error that says Hook names must start with use: oxlint's rules-of-hooks check sees useEffect in a function that is neither a component nor a Hook, and reports an error. Renaming it useTabTitle fixes it, and makes the linter check the rules inside it too.
  3. D boolean | (() => void) | undefined: Without as const the return type is an array of boolean or function, so each destructured name gets the union, plus undefined because noUncheckedIndexedAccess is on. as const makes it a readonly tuple: a boolean first, then a function.
  4. B Every render created a new function, so the dependency changed every time: Each render creates a new arrow function, and Object.is says two functions are never equal. So every render changed a dependency, the effect ran, set state, and rendered again. useApi now reloads only when the key string changes, and reads the function through useEffectEvent.
  5. C One, for q=pelican, after the typing pauses: Each new value clears the previous timer in the effect's cleanup, so the settled value only changes once the typing pauses for 300 ms. The key then becomes perPage=50 and q=pelican, and useApi sends one request.
Lesson 4.3 · Refs and the DOM
  1. C Nothing changes: the ref holds the new value, but React does not render again: Changing a ref does not render the component again. The new value is there for any code that reads it later, but the screen does not change. If the screen should show it, it belongs in state.
  2. A HTMLInputElement | null: Passing null picks the overload for DOM refs: RefObject of HTMLInputElement or null. Before React attaches the input, and after it is removed, there is no element, so TypeScript makes you check, for example with searchRef.current?.focus().
  3. D Declare ref?: Ref<HTMLInputElement> in its props and put it on the input: React 19 passes ref to function components as an ordinary prop. EventFilters declares ref as an optional Ref of HTMLInputElement, destructures it and puts it on the input; the page writes ref equals searchRef.
  4. B The arrow returns the assigned value, and a callback ref may only return nothing or a cleanup function: An arrow without braces returns its expression, and an assignment has a value: the element or null. A React 19 callback ref may only return nothing or a cleanup function. Braces around the body fix it.
  5. A The components above it in React's tree, such as EventCard, even though the element is in body: React events bubble through React's tree, where the dialog is still inside EventCard. The DOM position only decides where the element sits in the HTML. The PortalBubble demo measured exactly this.

Exit Ticket

  1. Two pages, one Hook: two requests (four on the dev server, half of them aborted by Strict Mode), two separate states. Sharing one answer needs lifting state, context (5.2) or a cache (7.1).
  2. Key and load: the key says what is loaded, so a new key loads again; load is a new function on every render and would run the effect every time, so the effect reads it through useEffectEvent.
  3. Ref vs state: refs: the dialog element, its opener, the search input, a timer id; state: the search text, confirming, the LoadState.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or B. Thinks "reusable" means Hook. Back to the table: does it call Hooks?
  • Q3 B. Tries to fix the caller; the Hook's return type is the problem. Hover the Hook's name.
  • Q4 A or B. Trusts the tools for the flood. Both are quiet; only the Network tab (or a test that counts requests) shows it.
  • Q7 A. Puts everything in state. The pocket notebook: nobody needs to see the timer id.
  • Q11 B. Believes the browser finds a sensible place for the focus. It does not: body, then the skip link.
  • Q12 A or B. Mixes up the jobs: the portal places, showModal makes inert, the cleanup returns the focus, the guard handles Strict Mode.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 12: Suspense and Error Boundaries, and useReducer

Answers and reasoning for every Session 12 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 12 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

W1
useApi(key, load): what does the key decide?
A) When the data is loaded again
B) How the data is loaded
C) Which component may call the Hook
D) The URL of the API
Why: WHAT is loaded, so a new key loads again; load (HOW) is read through useEffectEvent and never triggers a load.
W2
Where does the focus go after a dialog that a button removed, unless your code moves it?
A) Back to the opener, always
B) To the page's body
C) To the dialog's first button
D) To the address bar
Why: Escape lets the browser restore the focus; removing the dialog does not: the HQ's Dialog cleanup gives it back to the opener.
W3
What do useApi's pages and today's cleanup page have in common, and what is new?
A) Nothing: they are unrelated
B) Both load data; today the component reads it with use() and the waiting and failing move to boundaries
C) Both use refs
D) Today's page needs no API
Why: useApi pages ask LoadState which of three cases they are in; with Suspense, the component only handles the happy path.

4.4 · Suspense, use() and Error Boundaries: self-check

Q1
A page has the header, a back link, then <Suspense> around EventDetails, which calls use(eventPromise(id)). The API is slow. What is on the screen?
A) Nothing at all until the cleanup arrives
B) The header and the back link, and the Suspense fallback below them
C) The header only; the back link waits too
D) An error message, until the promise resolves
Why: Everything outside the nearest boundary stays; only the part inside it shows the fallback. With no boundary at all the whole app waits: #root stayed empty (measured in the lesson).
A is wrong: That is the app with no Suspense anywhere.
C is wrong: The back link sits outside the boundary.
D is wrong: Waiting is not failing: errors go to the error boundary.
Q2
A teammate writes const event = use(getEvent(eventId)). tsc and the linter are quiet. What do people see, and what is the fix?
"Loading…" forever, with requests going out non-stop; keep ONE promise per id (eventPromise(id)) instead of a new one every render
Why: A component that suspends is rendered again from the top; a new promise is pending again, so it suspends again. The lesson measured well over a thousand requests in three seconds; the kit's run of the same file: no type errors, 0 lint warnings.
Q3
Which tool printed this (first lines), and why is the code wrong?
  ⚠ react(error-boundaries): Avoid constructing JSX within try/catch
  × react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "use" cannot be called in a try/catch block.
A) tsc: use() returns undefined in a try
B) The browser: use() cannot be imported here
C) oxlint: use() must not sit in try/catch; its errors belong to an error boundary
D) oxlint, but it is only style
Why: use() suspends by throwing, so the catch would catch the WAITING and show the error at once (measured). Remove the try; put an error boundary around the component.
A is wrong: tsc has nothing to say about it.
B is wrong: The import is fine.
D is wrong: The rules-of-hooks part is an error, not style.
Q4
Which error does the HQ's ErrorBoundary catch?
A) An error thrown in an onClick handler inside it
B) A rejected promise in a setTimeout callback
C) An error thrown inside the boundary's own render
D) A component inside it that throws while rendering
Why: Boundaries catch errors thrown while rendering the components INSIDE them, including a rejected promise read with use(). Not handlers, not async code, not themselves (the next boundary up does). Lab 4.4's last test proves the click case.
A is wrong: The screen does not depend on it; React leaves the page as it is.
B is wrong: Async code handles its own errors.
C is wrong: The next boundary up catches that.
Q5
tsc reports this for the ErrorBoundary class (first lines). What is missing?
src/components/ErrorBoundary.tsx:21:3 - error TS2416: Property 'state' in type 'ErrorBoundary' is not assignable to the same property in base type 'Component<ErrorBoundaryProps, ErrorBoundaryState, any>'.
  Type '{ failed: boolean; }' is not assignable to type 'Readonly<ErrorBoundaryState>'.
    Property 'error' is missing in type '{ failed: boolean; }' but required in type 'Readonly<{ failed: true; error: unknown; }>'.
A) A type annotation on the field: state: ErrorBoundaryState = { failed: false }
B) The word static
C) A constructor that calls super(props)
D) as const after the object
Why: Without the annotation TypeScript infers { failed: boolean } for the field, which no longer fits the class's declared union state. Annotate it, and only failed: true has an error.
B is wrong: static belongs on getDerivedStateFromError (and is silent when missing).
C is wrong: Field initializers need no constructor.
D is wrong: as const would make it readonly, still not the union.
Q6
The API was down; the page shows Could not load this cleanup with Try again; the API is back. Why does EventPage pass onRetry={() => forgetEvent(eventId)}?
A rejected promise stays rejected: without forgetting it, the retry reads the same failure and sends no request
Why: Measured in the lesson: with onRetry removed, pressing Try again sent nothing and showed the same error. Forgetting makes the next render ask the API again.

4.4 · Suspense, use() and Error Boundaries: predict, then check

P1
A boundary class without static on getDerivedStateFromError. What do npx tsc -b and npm run lint print?
  getDerivedStateFromError(error: unknown): ErrorBoundaryState {
    return { failed: true, error }
  }
Found 0 warnings and 0 errors.
Finished in …ms on 38 files with 150 rules using … threads.
Why: Both quiet, and the boundary catches nothing: React only looks for the method on the class. The console says getDerivedStateFromError() is defined as an instance method and will be ignored.

5.1 · useReducer with Discriminated Unions: self-check

Q7
Which state is a good fit for useReducer rather than several useState calls?
A) A search box's text
B) Whether a card's details are open
C) Marks and an undo history that must change together, by rules
D) The list of cleanups from the API
Why: Several values that change together, rules for each change, and a next state that depends on the history: one reducer keeps the rules in one place. Server data stays out of reducers.
A is wrong: One value, one obvious setter: useState.
B is wrong: A toggle: useState.
D is wrong: Server data: useApi now, TanStack Query in 7.1.
Q8
tsc reports these two errors for the tally's buttons. Which actions were dispatched?
src/Scratch.tsx:27:53 - error TS2345: Argument of type '{ type: "added"; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'TallyAction'.
src/Scratch.tsx:30:55 - error TS2322: Type '"clear"' is not assignable to type '"added" | "reset"'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:27
A) { type: 'added' } without a weight, and { type: 'clear' }, which is no TallyAction
B) { type: 'added', weightKg: '4' } and { type: 'reset' }
C) Two correct actions in the wrong order
D) { type: 'reset', weightKg: 4 } twice
Why: The first message names the member you meant and the field it misses (and where weightKg is declared); the second lists the types that exist. Typed actions catch both before the browser sees them.
B is wrong: A string weight would be a different message.
C is wrong: Order is not a type error.
D is wrong: Not these messages.
Q9
A new member was added to TallyAction and the reducer ends with assertNever. What does this tell you, and what do you do?
src/Scratch.tsx:21:26 - error TS2345: Argument of type '{ type: "removed"; weightKg: number; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'never'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:21
No case handles removed: add a 'removed' case to that switch
Why: assertNever only accepts a value narrowed to never. Adding a member to a union and running npx tsc -b gives a to-do list: one error per switch that needs the new case.
Q10
A reducer ends with default: return state, and a teammate adds an action with no case for it. What happens?
A) tsc reports the missing case
B) The app throws when the action is dispatched
C) Nothing anywhere: it compiles, and the action silently does nothing
D) React warns in the console
Why: The kit ran it: npx tsc -b printed nothing. A default that returns the state swallows every action nobody handles; return assertNever(action) instead.
A is wrong: Only with assertNever (or an annotated return type and no default).
B is wrong: It returns the state.
D is wrong: Nothing warns.
Q11
Inside the reducer someone writes state.marks.set(id, 'attended') then return state. With marks typed as a plain Map, what do people see after a click, and why?
Nothing changes on screen: the same state object came back, so React saw no change and did not render
Why: React compares old and new state with Object.is. The project types marks as ReadonlyMap so this is TS2339 instead of a silent bug: copy first (new Map(old)), then change the copy.
Q12
In development, a console.log as the first line of rosterReducer prints twice per click. Why, and is it a problem?
A) A bug: dispatch was called twice
B) Strict Mode runs the reducer twice to flush out impure reducers; a pure reducer does not care
C) React always runs reducers twice, also in production
D) The reducer is in the dependency array
Why: Measured in the lesson: two logs, one check-in counted. A reducer that sent a request would send it twice: side effects go in handlers or effects.
A is wrong: One click, one dispatch.
C is wrong: Development only.
D is wrong: Reducers are not effects.

5.1 · useReducer with Discriminated Unions: predict, then check

P2
Predict each line: the action, Diego's mark, the history's length, and whether the same state object came back. Then node ex2-predict.ts.
import { initialRoster, rosterReducer, type RosterAction } from './src/reducers/rosterReducer.ts'

const actions: RosterAction[] = [
  { type: 'checkIn', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'checkIn', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'markNoShow', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'undo' },
  { type: 'undo' },
  { type: 'undo' },
]

let state = initialRoster
for (const action of actions) {
  const next = rosterReducer(state, action)
  console.log(action.type, next.marks.get('s088') ?? 'expected', next.history.length, next === state)
  state = next
}
checkIn attended 1 false
checkIn attended 1 true
markNoShow no-show 2 false
undo attended 1 false
undo expected 0 false
undo expected 0 true
Why: The second checkIn changes nothing (same state, no history); markNoShow remembers attended; the third undo finds an empty history and returns the same state.
P3
A reducer ends with default: return state and the union has a removed member with no case. What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useReducer } from 'react'

interface Tally {
  bags: number
  weightKg: number
}

type TallyAction =
  | { type: 'added'; weightKg: number }
  | { type: 'removed'; weightKg: number }
  | { type: 'reset' }

function tallyReducer(tally: Tally, action: TallyAction): Tally {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'added':
      return { bags: tally.bags + 1, weightKg: tally.weightKg + action.weightKg }
    case 'reset':
      return { bags: 0, weightKg: 0 }
    default:
      return tally
  }
}

export function BagTally() {
  const [tally, dispatch] = useReducer(tallyReducer, { bags: 0, weightKg: 0 })

  return (
    <section>
      <p role="status">
        {tally.bags} bags, {tally.weightKg} kg
      </p>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => dispatch({ type: 'added', weightKg: 4 })}>
        Add a 4 kg bag
      </button>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => dispatch({ type: 'reset' })}>
        Reset
      </button>
    </section>
  )
}
(no output: no errors)
Why: The default swallows the missing case: no error, and the action silently does nothing. return assertNever(action) turns it into an error.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 4.4 · Suspense, use() and Error Boundaries
  1. B The component suspends and the nearest Suspense boundary shows its fallback: The component suspends: React stops rendering it and shows the fallback of the nearest Suspense boundary above. When the promise resolves, React renders the component again and use returns the cleanup.
  2. D The loading fallback forever, while requests keep going out: Every render creates a new pending promise, so every render suspends, and each resolved promise starts another render with yet another promise. The measured demo sent well over a thousand requests in three seconds and never showed the title. The promise has to come from a cache or be started outside rendering.
  3. A A rejected promise read with use() inside it: use throws the rejection's error while the component renders, and errors thrown during rendering are what error boundaries catch. Errors in event handlers and in async code such as timers are not caught; handle those where they happen.
  4. C Catching rendering errors needs getDerivedStateFromError, and there is no Hook for it yet: Catching a child's rendering error needs the static getDerivedStateFromError method (or componentDidCatch), and React 19.3 has no Hook that does the same. So boundaries are classes, written by you or by a library such as react-error-boundary.
  5. B So the next render gets a new request instead of the same rejected promise: The cache still holds the rejected promise, and a rejected promise stays rejected: use would throw the same error at once. Forgetting it makes eventPromise start a new request on the next render. Measured: without onRetry, Try again sent no request and showed the error again.
Lesson 5.1 · useReducer with Discriminated Unions
  1. D It dispatches { type: 'checkIn', signupId: 's088' }, and rosterReducer returns the next state: The button only reports what happened. React calls rosterReducer with the current state and that action; the reducer returns a new state with the mark and one more history entry, and the desk renders again.
  2. B An error: property weightKg is missing in that object but required by the added action: TS2345: the object is not assignable to TallyAction, because the added member requires weightKg. TypeScript even points at where weightKg is declared. Each action carries exactly the data its kind needs.
  3. C npx tsc -b fails: the startOver action is not assignable to a parameter of type never: In the default branch, action is narrowed to whatever no case handled: the startOver member. That is not assignable to never, so tsc reports TS2345 and names the missing action, at the switch that needs the new case.
  4. A Nothing changes on screen: React got the same state object back, so it did not render: The reducer returned the same object, so React's Object.is comparison saw no change and did not render. Measured: the counts and the badge stayed as they were. Typing the marks as ReadonlyMap makes the set call a compile error instead.
  5. C The rows are server data that later lessons load differently; the reducer keeps only today's marks and history, so it never has to change: The rows are server data: today a saved copy, then useApi after sign-in (5.2), then TanStack Query (7.1). The reducer holds only what the desk itself decides, the marks and their history, so it survives both of those changes untouched. (Lesson 7.2 later changes it on purpose, when saved marks become server data too.)

Exit Ticket

  1. Suspense: the waiting (its fallback while something inside suspends). Error boundary: the failing (its fallback when something inside throws while rendering, including a rejected use()).
  2. Stable promise: a suspended component renders again from the top; a new promise would be pending again and suspend forever, sending a request each time.
  3. assertNever: a compile error naming each action no case handles, and a loud failure if an unexpected value arrives at run time.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Thinks Suspense blanks the page. Show the 16-loading screenshot: header and back link stay.
  • Q4 A. Expects boundaries to catch handler errors. Lab 4.4's last test.
  • Q5 B. Mixes up the two class traps; static is the silent one.
  • Q7 A or B. Reaches for a reducer for one value. Back to the useState-or-useReducer table.
  • Q10 A. Trusts the default. The kit's run printed nothing.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 13: Typed Context

Answers and reasoning for every Session 13 item: the warm-up, both halves of the self-check, the predict-then-check requests, the Lab 5.2 reference outputs and code, the Checkpoint 2 model answer and rubric, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 13 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Session 12 Retrieval

  1. EventDetails reads the cleanup with use(). Why does it go through eventPromises.ts instead of calling getEvent(id) itself? use() needs the SAME promise on every render; a new getEvent() call would suspend again and start over. The module keeps one promise per cleanup.
  2. On the event page, what does the ErrorBoundary catch that the Suspense boundary does not? A rejected promise (or any error while rendering): Suspense only shows a fallback while the promise is pending; the boundary shows EventProblem when it fails (404 → Cleanup not found).
  3. The desk dispatches { type: 'checkIn', signupId } for a row that is already marked attended. What should the reducer return, and why? The SAME state object: nothing changed, so React can skip the render, and Undo gets no empty step.
  4. What does assertNever(value: never) in a switch's default give you? A compile error the day someone adds an action or a union member without a case, and a loud error if an unexpected value arrives at run time.

5.2 · Typed Context: self-check

Q1
Which of these values belongs in a context rather than a prop?
A) The showDrafts flag EventsPage hands to EventGroups, its direct child
B) The text in the events search box, which changes on every keystroke
C) Who is signed in, which the header, the event cards and the desk all need
D) The list of cleanups the events page loaded from the API
Why: Context is for values many components at different depths need, and that change rarely: the signed-in person is the lesson's example. A direct child gets a plain prop (it is explicit); server data stays with useApi (TanStack Query in Lesson 7.1); fast-changing values stay local state (Lesson 5.2, the beach radio table).
A is wrong: One level down: a prop is clearer, and the lesson keeps it a prop on purpose.
B is wrong: It changes many times a second and only the events page uses it: local state.
D is wrong: Server data: useApi now, a data library later, not a context.
Q2
A component in src/Scratch.tsx reads the HQ's context directly and tsc prints this. What does the HQ do instead?
src/Scratch.tsx:5:11 - error TS2339: Property 'isOrganizer' does not exist on type 'AuthValue | undefined'.

5   const { isOrganizer } = useContext(AuthContext)
            ~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:5
A) Write useContext(AuthContext) as AuthValue so the error goes away
B) Call useAuth(), which checks for undefined once, throws outside the provider and returns an AuthValue
C) Give createContext a default object, so the value is never undefined
D) Turn off strict in tsconfig.app.json for this file
Why: The type tells the truth: outside a provider the value is undefined. useAuth() narrows it once with an if that throws, so every caller gets an AuthValue and a missing provider is a loud error (Lesson 5.2, The Undefined Context Trap).
A is wrong: A cast silences the check and keeps the bug: outside a provider the destructure crashes.
C is wrong: That is the trap itself: it compiles, and a missing provider becomes a silent bug.
D is wrong: Switching checks off is never the fix; the error is right.
Q3
The lesson's "harmless" default: what do npx tsc -b and npm run lint say about this file, and what happens when the button is clicked outside any provider?
import { createContext, useContext } from 'react'
import type { AuthValue } from './auth/AuthContext.ts'

// A "harmless" default so that nobody has to deal with undefined...

  session: null,
  isOrganizer: false,
  signIn: async () => {},
  signOut: () => {},

  return (

Why: The kit ran both: npx tsc -b printed nothing and oxlint said Found 0 warnings and 0 errors.. The default's signIn is an empty function, so the app is signed out forever and nothing says why: a setup mistake turned into a silent bug (Lesson 5.2).
Q4
Someone puts the context, its Hook and the provider in one AuthProvider.tsx. npm run lint prints this. What is the fix?
  ⚠ react(only-export-components): Fast refresh only works when a file only exports components. Use a new file to share constants or functions between components.
    ╭─[src/auth/AuthProvider.tsx:20:17]
 19 │ /** The sign-in state. Only works inside <AuthProvider>. */
 20 │ export function useAuth(): AuthValue {
    ·                 ───────
 21 │   const auth = useContext(AuthContext)
    ╰────
A) Turn off react(only-export-components) in .oxlintrc.json
B) Rename AuthProvider.tsx to AuthProvider.ts
C) Stop exporting useAuth and copy it into every component
D) Keep the context, its type and useAuth in AuthContext.ts and only the provider component in AuthProvider.tsx
Why: Vite's Fast Refresh can only keep a component's state when its file exports components and nothing else, so oxlint warns about the Hook and the context. The project splits them: AuthContext.ts (no JSX) and AuthProvider.tsx (Lesson 5.2, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: That hides a real problem: Fast Refresh would reload the whole page on every save of that file.
B is wrong: The provider returns JSX, which a .ts file cannot hold.
C is wrong: Then every component repeats the undefined check: the opposite of the pattern.
Q5
Step 1 changed request(path, signal?) to request(path, init: RequestInit = {}). Then npx tsc -b prints this. What does it mean?
src/api/client.ts:217:40 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'AbortSignal | undefined' is not assignable to parameter of type 'RequestInit | undefined'.
  Type 'AbortSignal' has no properties in common with type 'RequestInit'.
src/api/client.ts:246:40 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'AbortSignal | undefined' is not assignable to parameter of type 'RequestInit | undefined'.
  Type 'AbortSignal' has no properties in common with type 'RequestInit'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/api/client.ts:217
A) An AbortSignal shares no property with RequestInit, so it cannot be the options object: pass { signal }
B) fetch no longer accepts a signal: remove it from both calls
C) RequestInit needs every property: add method, headers and body
D) The callers must cast it: signal as RequestInit
Why: Every property of RequestInit is optional, so TypeScript checks options objects for at least one property in common (a weak type). Wrapping the signal, request(path, { signal }), puts it where fetch reads it (Lesson 5.2, Step 1).
B is wrong: The signal still matters (aborting stale requests, Lesson 4.1): it moves into the object.
C is wrong: All of RequestInit's properties are optional; that is why it is a weak type.
D is wrong: A cast hides the bug: fetch would ignore the signal.
Q6
The same request to /api/events?perPage=1, first as a guest, then with an organizer's token. Why does useApi now list the viewer in its dependencies?
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
x-total-count: 15

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
x-total-count: 17
A) To send the token with the request
B) Because the API answers differently per person, so a new person must mean a new request
C) So that guests cannot see drafts
D) Because React requires every Hook to read the context
Why: Who asks is part of the question: 15 public cleanups for a guest, 17 with the two drafts for an organizer. With the viewer in [key, viewer, attempt], signing in or out runs the effect again and the page follows without a reload. TanStack Query's keys do the same in Lesson 7.1.
A is wrong: request() adds the token from the session; the dependency only decides WHEN to ask again.
C is wrong: The API decides that (it sends drafts only to organizers); hiding is not security.
D is wrong: No such rule: useApi calls useAuth() because it needs the viewer.

5.2 · Typed Context: predict, then check

Real answers of the course API (the kit's private copy; outputs show the learners' address). P2 uses the API's documented test token for Theo (a volunteer); P3 a made-up token in the API's format, which the API treats exactly like one it has forgotten. Nothing here prints a real token.

P1
Predict the status code and what the answer says. (Maya's email with the wrong password.)

P2
Predict the status code and what the answer says. (Today's roster, asked for by a volunteer.)

P3

content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "unauthorized",
  "message": "Your session has ended. Sign in again."
}
Why: The API checks a token BEFORE routing, so an old token gets 401 everywhere, public lists too. The HQ's describeError turns it into "Your sign-in has ended. Sign out, then sign in again.", and Sign out always works.

import { useContext } from 'react'
import { HeightUnitContext } from './heightUnit.ts'

const feetPerMeter = 3.28

interface TideHeightProps {
  meters: number
}

// Reads the unit from the nearest provider above it (or the default, 'm')
export function TideHeight({ meters }: TideHeightProps) {
  const unit = useContext(HeightUnitContext)
  const text = unit === 'm' ? `${meters.toFixed(1)} m` : `${(meters * feetPerMeter).toFixed(1)} ft`
  return <strong>{text}</strong>
}
import { useState } from 'react'
import { HeightUnitContext, type HeightUnit } from './heightUnit.ts'
import { TideRow } from './TideRow.tsx'

// The board owns the unit; every TideHeight inside the provider hears it, whatever sits in between
export function TideBoard() {
  const [unit, setUnit] = useState<HeightUnit>('m')
  return (
    <section aria-label="Tide board">
      <HeightUnitContext value={unit}>
        <button type="button" onClick={() => setUnit(unit === 'm' ? 'ft' : 'm')}>
          Show {unit === 'm' ? 'feet' : 'meters'}
        </button>
        <TideRow label="High tide" meters={1.5} />
        <TideRow label="Low tide" meters={0.3} />
      </HeightUnitContext>
    </section>
  )
}
import { createContext, useContext } from 'react'

/** What every component can ask about today's shift at the supply shed */
export interface ShiftValue {
  /** null while nobody leads the shift */
  lead: string | null
  startShift: (name: string) => void
  endShift: () => void
}

// undefined is the value outside a ShiftProvider: a mistake that useShift() reports at once
export const ShiftContext = createContext<ShiftValue | undefined>(undefined)

/** Today's shift. Only works inside <ShiftProvider>. */
export function useShift(): ShiftValue {
  const shift = useContext(ShiftContext)
  if (shift === undefined) {
    throw new Error('useShift() must be used inside <ShiftProvider>')
  }
  return shift
}

/** "Lead: NAME" or "No lead yet" (moved here from ShiftProvider.tsx: a .tsx file exports components only) */
export function leadLabel(lead: string | null): string {
  return lead === null ? 'No lead yet' : `Lead: ${lead}`
}
import { useState, type ReactNode } from 'react'
import { ShiftContext } from './ShiftContext.ts'

interface ShiftProviderProps {
  children: ReactNode
}

// Owns the shift: the only place that changes it
export function ShiftProvider({ children }: ShiftProviderProps) {
  const [lead, setLead] = useState<string | null>(null)

  function startShift(name: string) {
    setLead(name)
  }

  function endShift() {
    setLead(null)
  }

  return <ShiftContext value={{ lead, startShift, endShift }}>{children}</ShiftContext>
}

What each task shows: (1) the three parts, with the default doing real work (the test renders TideHeight with no provider) and TideRow untouched; (2) the trap fixed twice over: the behavior (a thrown sentence instead of a silent no-op) and the TYPE (the test file's @ts-expect-error is only used once reading the context directly fails with TS2339); (3) the provider as the only writer of its state, and oxlint's only-export-components on a helper exported from a .tsx (the same message the lesson's one-file mistake shows for the Hook). Accept the helper in its own shift/labels.ts; accept use(ShiftContext) inside useShift().

import { useEffect, useState } from 'react'
import { isSite } from './isSite.ts'
import type { LoadState } from './loadState.ts'
import type { Site } from './types.ts'

/** One site from the API, loaded when the component appears and again whenever siteId changes */
export function useSite(apiUrl: string, siteId: string): LoadState<Site> {
  const [state, setState] = useState<LoadState<Site>>({ kind: 'loading' })

  useEffect(() => {
    const controller = new AbortController()
    fetch(`${apiUrl}/api/sites/${encodeURIComponent(siteId)}`, { signal: controller.signal })
      .then(async (response) => {
        if (!response.ok) {
          setState({ kind: 'failed', message: `Could not load the site (${response.status}).` })
          return
        }
        const data: unknown = await response.json()
        if (!isSite(data)) {
          setState({ kind: 'failed', message: 'The API sent something that is not a site.' })
          return
        }
        setState({ kind: 'ready', data })
      })
      .catch((error: unknown) => {
        if (controller.signal.aborted) return     // we cancelled it: nothing went wrong
        console.error(error)
        setState({ kind: 'failed', message: 'Could not reach the server.' })
      })
    return () => controller.abort()
  }, [apiUrl, siteId])

  return state
}
import { assertNever } from './assertNever.ts'

export type BagKind = 'trash' | 'recycling'

/** The bag tally of one cleanup */
export interface TallyState {
  trash: number
  recycling: number
  /** the kinds in the order they were added, for undo */
  history: readonly BagKind[]
}

export const initialTally: TallyState = { trash: 0, recycling: 0, history: [] }

export type TallyAction = { type: 'add'; kind: BagKind } | { type: 'undo' } | { type: 'reset' }

// One more (or one fewer) bag of a kind
function counted(state: TallyState, kind: BagKind, change: 1 | -1): TallyState {
  return kind === 'trash'
    ? { ...state, trash: state.trash + change }
    : { ...state, recycling: state.recycling + change }
}

export function tallyReducer(state: TallyState, action: TallyAction): TallyState {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'add':
      return { ...counted(state, action.kind, 1), history: [...state.history, action.kind] }
    case 'undo': {
      const last = state.history.at(-1)
      if (last === undefined) return state       // nothing to undo: the same state, so nothing renders
      return { ...counted(state, last, -1), history: state.history.slice(0, -1) }
    }
    case 'reset':
      return initialTally
    default:
      return assertNever(action)
  }
}
import { createContext, useContext } from 'react'
import type { TallyAction, TallyState } from './tally.ts'

/** What the tally's components share */
export interface TallyValue {
  state: TallyState
  dispatch: (action: TallyAction) => void
}

// No provider above = no tally: the type says so
export const TallyContext = createContext<TallyValue | undefined>(undefined)

/** The tally. Only works inside <TallyProvider>. */
export function useTally(): TallyValue {
  const tally = useContext(TallyContext)
  if (tally === undefined) {
    throw new Error('useTally() must be used inside <TallyProvider>')
  }
  return tally
}
import { useReducer, type ReactNode } from 'react'
import { initialTally, tallyReducer } from './tally.ts'
import { TallyContext } from './TallyContext.ts'

interface TallyProviderProps {
  children: ReactNode
}

// Owns the tally; every component below reads it through useTally()
export function TallyProvider({ children }: TallyProviderProps) {
  const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(tallyReducer, initialTally)
  return <TallyContext value={{ state, dispatch }}>{children}</TallyContext>
}

Scoring notes. Task 1: resetting to loading when the id changes (a setState in the effect body) is fine here; keeping the previous site until the new one arrives is what the HQ's useApi does; async/await inside the effect is equally right; a missing cleanup is "Partly" on row 1 even when the tests pass (they cannot see it: look at the code); data as Site instead of isSite is "Partly" on row 2. Task 2: a computed key ([action.kind]: …) instead of the helper is fine; an if chain without assertNever is "Partly". Task 3: use(TallyContext) instead of useContext is fine; a Hook that returns TallyValue | undefined and leaves the check to the callers is "Partly".

Modules 4 and 5 review: self-check

Q7
In development, the events page's request shows twice in the Network tab, the first one canceled. Why?
A) The effect has no dependency array, so it runs after every render
B) fetch retries a request once when the API is slow
C) Strict Mode runs effect, cleanup, effect in development; the cleanup's abort() cancels the first request
D) useApi's reload() runs once when the page appears
Why: Strict Mode checks that every effect cleans up by running it twice in development. The cleanup aborts the first request, and the aborted check ignores its error. The build sends one request (Lesson 4.1).
A is wrong: Then it would loop or repeat forever, not exactly twice.
B is wrong: fetch never retries on its own.
D is wrong: reload() runs only when someone presses Try again.
Q8
useApi(key, load) keeps load in useEffectEvent and lists only [key, viewer, attempt]. Name the three things that make it load again.
A new key (what is loaded), a new viewer (who asks), and reload() (Try again bumps attempt)
Why: The key says WHAT is loaded (the API path), the viewer WHO asks (Lesson 5.2), attempt counts Try again clicks. The load function is read through useEffectEvent, so a new arrow on every render does not restart the request (Lesson 4.2). The stage's code: }, [key, viewer, attempt]).
Q9
After Sign out, the button that had the focus is gone. Which of these moves the focus to the Sign in link that replaces it, and only after a sign-out?
A) document.querySelector('a').focus() in the component body
B) A state flag set by the sign-out button, and a callback ref on the link that focuses it when the flag is true
C) autoFocus on the Sign in link
D) useRef on the link and .focus() during render
Why: Lesson 5.2's Exercise 2: a callback ref runs when the element appears (Lesson 4.3), and the flag makes it happen only after a sign-out from this menu, never on a page that simply loads signed out. Touching the DOM during render is never right.
A is wrong: During render, the link is not in the page yet, and it would grab the focus on every render.
C is wrong: autoFocus fires on every page load as a guest: the page must not grab the focus by itself.
D is wrong: During render the ref is still null (it is set after the commit).
Q10
EventDetails calls use(eventPromise(id, viewer)). Why not use(getEvent(id))?
A) getEvent returns a Response, not a promise
B) use() only accepts promises from the cache API
C) Hooks cannot call functions that fetch
D) A new promise on every render suspends again and starts over: use() needs the SAME promise each time
Why: use() suspends until the promise settles, then React renders again; a fresh getEvent() call would create a new promise and suspend forever. eventPromises.ts keeps one promise per cleanup, and since Lesson 5.2 per viewer (Lesson 4.4, temporary until Lesson 7.1).
A is wrong: getEvent returns Promise<CleanupEventDetail>.
B is wrong: Any promise works; the problem is a new one each time.
C is wrong: use() itself is how a component reads a promise; the problem is which promise.
Q11
In the checkpoint's model answer, someone deletes the case 'reset': lines. What happened, and what is the fix?
src/tally.ts:34:26 - error TS2345: Argument of type '{ type: "reset"; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'never'.

34       return assertNever(action)
                            ~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/tally.ts:34
A) A reset action can now reach the default case, so action is not never there: put the case back
B) assertNever is broken: change its parameter to unknown
C) Reducers may not have a default case
D) reset must return a new object, not initialTally
Why: With every case handled, nothing is left in the default and action is never. Remove one and TypeScript names the leftover member: { type: "reset"; }. The same check made Lesson 5.1's SpotsLine exhaustive.
B is wrong: That throws the check away: the error is assertNever doing its job.
C is wrong: The default with assertNever is what makes the switch exhaustive.
D is wrong: Returning the initial object is fine; React compares references only to skip renders.
Q12
As shipped, the checkpoint's npm run typecheck prints this. What does it tell you, and what makes it go away?
src/checkpoint.test.tsx:81:3 - error TS2578: Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive.
src/checkpoint.test.tsx:83:3 - error TS2578: Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/checkpoint.test.tsx:81
TallyAction is still too loose: the test's two impossible actions compile; a discriminated union (add with a kind, undo, reset) makes them errors, so the directives are used
Why: A @ts-expect-error line asserts that the next line is a type error; while the type accepts { type: 'weigh' } and an add without a kind, tsc reports the directive itself as unused (TS2578). It is how the kit tests a TYPE, not a value (Lab 1.1, Lab 5.2, Checkpoint 2 Task 2).

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 5.2 · Typed Context
  1. C EventCard calls useAuth() and reads isOrganizer from the nearest AuthProvider: EventCard calls useAuth(), which reads the nearest AuthContext provider above it. Nothing in between has to carry the value, and when the session changes, React renders the card again.
  2. A An error: property isOrganizer does not exist on type AuthValue | undefined: TS2339: outside a provider the value is undefined, and undefined has no isOrganizer. That is why components call useAuth(), which checks once and returns AuthValue.
  3. D It compiles, then the page goes blank and the console says useAuth() must be used inside <AuthProvider>: Measured: it compiles, then UserMenu's useAuth() throws while rendering, React removes the whole app (the root element was empty) and the console's error says useAuth() must be used inside the AuthProvider component. A loud error that names the fix.
  4. B It survives the page reloads the HQ still has, but stays in one tab and ends when the tab closes: The HQ still reloads on every link (until Lesson 6.1), so memory alone would lose the sign-in. sessionStorage survives reloads but belongs to one tab and ends when the tab closes. It is readable by the page's scripts, like every storage, which is why the front end never decides what is secure.
  5. D useApi has the signed-in person in its dependencies, so it asks again, now without a token, and the API sends no drafts: useApi lists the viewer in its effect's dependencies. A new viewer means the effect runs again, the request goes out without a token, and the API's answer has no drafts. Measured: /events?perPage=50 and /sites were requested again right after Sign out.
What the checks print at the end of the lesson

On the finished Lesson 5.2 code, npx tsc -b prints nothing, and npm run lint and npm run build end like this (the kit's runs; times, thread counts and hashes differ on every machine):

Exit Ticket

  1. AuthValue | undefined: outside a provider there is no sign-in. A fake default compiles and fails silently (signed out forever); undefined in the type makes the compiler force a check, which useAuth() does once, throwing "useAuth() must be used inside <AuthProvider>".
  2. Two files: Vite's Fast Refresh keeps a component's state only when its file exports components and nothing else; the context and its Hook go in AuthContext.ts, the provider in AuthProvider.tsx (oxlint's only-export-components warns otherwise).
  3. Who protects: the API. It sends drafts only with an organizer's token and answers 403 "Only organizers can do that." to a volunteer's roster request; the HQ only decides what is convenient to show.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Has learned "context = shared" and applies it to a direct child. Show EventGroups' showDrafts prop: one level, explicit, clearer.
  • Q2 A or C. Reaches for a cast or a default: the two ways to keep the bug. Run the lesson's QuickSignIn outside a provider: nothing happens, and nothing says why.
  • Q5 B or D. Reads "no properties in common" as "remove it" or "cast it". Read RequestInit's properties aloud: all optional, signal among them.
  • Q6 A or C. Mixes up sending the token (request()) with WHEN to ask again (the dependency), or thinks the front end protects drafts.
  • Q7 A. Has not met Strict Mode's double effect run. Show the canceled request in the Network tab, then the build's single one.
  • Q10 A or C. Has not separated the promise from the component. Draw two renders, each creating its own promise.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 14: Rendering Performance and Component Patterns

Answers and reasoning for every Session 14 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs (the compiler run included), the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 14 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

  1. useAuth() throws when the context value is undefined. Why is that better than a default value? A component rendered outside AuthProvider fails at once with a message that says what to fix, instead of quietly showing a guest (Lesson 5.2). Today's Tabs use the same pattern for their parts.
  2. AuthProvider's value is a new object on every render. Lesson 5.2 said that is fine for now. When would it not be? When the provider renders often for reasons that do not change the value: every reader would render again for nothing. Lesson 5.3 measures it (and finds the HQ's AuthProvider is fine).
  3. The check-in desk keeps its marks in a reducer (Lesson 5.1). What happens to them if the desk component is removed from the page and added back? They are gone: unmounting a component throws its state away, and the roster is fetched again. Lesson 5.4 hides tab panels instead of removing them for exactly this reason.

5.3 · Rendering Performance: self-check

Q1
With the React Compiler on, Maya presses Sign out on /events, and every EventCard still renders. Why?
A) The compiler skipped EventCard because it breaks a rule
B) Their parent rendered, and the compiler never caches JSX
C) Each card calls useAuth(), and the context really changed: no memoization blocks that reason
D) Strict Mode renders every component twice in development
Why: Three reasons to render: own state, a parent that renders, a context the component reads. The cards read the auth context, which changed, so they render; inside each card the compiler reused the badges and buttons. Measured: 82 component calls in the sign-out's commit without the compiler, 19 with it (Lesson 5.3, Exercise 1).
A is wrong: EventCard follows the rules; the compiler optimized every HQ component.
B is wrong: The compiler does cache JSX; that is how typing went from 560 renders to 14.
D is wrong: Strict Mode doubles calls in development, but render counts are the same in a build.
Q2
oxlint prints this for the supply experiment. You ignore it and click Remove Gloves, then Remove Bags. What does the list show?
  ⚠ react-hooks(exhaustive-deps): React Hook useCallback has a missing dependency: 'supplies'
