 30 │     setSupplies(supplies.filter((item) => item !== supply))
    ·                 ────┬───
    ·                     ╰── useCallback uses `supplies` here
 31 │   }, [])
    ·      ──
 32 │
    ╰────
  help: Either include it or remove the dependency array.

Found 1 warning and 0 errors.
A) Grabbers
B) Gloves, Grabbers: Gloves comes back
C) An error: supplies is not defined
D) Bags, Grabbers: the second click does nothing
Why: The callback keeps the supplies from the first render forever, so the second removal filters the ORIGINAL three and Gloves reappears (measured in Lesson 5.3). Read the warning as a bug report; the updater form setSupplies((current) => ...) needs no dependency.
A is wrong: That is what correct code shows.
C is wrong: supplies is defined; it is just old.
D is wrong: The second click does run; it uses the stale array.
Q3
You wrap EventGroups in <Profiler onRender={logRender}> and open the app with npm run build and npm run preview. What does the console show while you type?
Nothing at all: production builds of React do not call onRender
Why: Profiling is left out of production builds. Measure on the dev server (Lesson 5.3 measured one line per key there), compare before and after on the same machine, and never treat development times as promises about users.
Q4
You copied an older tutorial's vite.config.ts. Which change fixes what npx tsc -b prints?
vite.config.ts:8:7 - error TS2353: Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'babel' does not exist in type 'Options'.

8       babel: {
        ~~~~~


Found 1 error in vite.config.ts:8
A) Install @babel/core so the babel option exists
B) Cast the options with as any so the config compiles
C) Keep react() and add babel({ presets: [reactCompilerPreset()] }) from @rolldown/plugin-babel
D) Downgrade TypeScript: version 7 does not check config files
Why: plugin-react 6 (Vite 8) has no babel option any more; its Options type says so. The compiler runs as a Babel preset through @rolldown/plugin-babel, the way the react-compiler-ts template sets it up (Lesson 5.3, Step 3). TypeScript checks vite.config.ts because tsconfig.node.json includes it.
A is wrong: The option is gone from the plugin, not from Babel.
B is wrong: That hides the error and the plugin still ignores the option: no compiler.
D is wrong: TypeScript 7 checks it; that is how you found the mistake before building.
Q5
A report screen sorts 100,000 log entries on every key press in its note box. Which change skips the sort while you type?
A) Wrap the report component in memo
B) Wrap the sort function in useCallback
C) Move the sort into a useEffect
D) const top = useMemo(() => heaviest(logs, 3), [logs])
Why: useMemo caches a calculated value until a dependency changes. memo skips a whole component, but here the component itself must render (its note changed). Measured in Lesson 5.3: a median of 30 ms per key press without it, 0.5 ms with it.
A is wrong: The report's own state changed; memo only skips renders caused by a parent.
B is wrong: useCallback keeps a function the same; calling it still sorts every time.
C is wrong: An effect runs after rendering and would need state to show the result: more renders, not fewer.
Q6
Lab 5.3's untouched starter, run with the React Compiler on (npm run test:compiler), ends like this. Which test still fails, and what fixes it?

Task 3 (the weight report still adds up the logs on every key press); an explicit useMemo, which the compiler keeps
Why: The compiler stabilized the roster's function and the context value without a single Hook, but it still called totalsByBeach 5 times while the note was typed: the same honest limit Lesson 5.3 measured on its report experiment. With the solution's useMemo, the compiler run passes all seven tests.

5.4 · Component Patterns: self-check

Q7
Why does Tabs.tsx NOT export TabsContext?
A) To keep the bundle smaller
B) So only the parts in the same file can read it: the rest of the app sees four components
C) Because a .tsx file may not export a context
D) Because createContext needs a default value to be exported
Why: A private context is what makes Tabs, TabList, Tab and TabPanel a family: nobody outside can read or fake the selected tab, and useTabs's error names the part that was used outside its parent (Lesson 5.4, Compound Components).
A is wrong: Exporting a value costs nothing measurable.
C is wrong: It may, but Fast Refresh's lint rule warns about non-component exports; that is a reason, not a rule of the language.
D is wrong: The default is undefined on purpose (T | undefined + a throwing Hook).
Q8
npx tsc -b reports this for <Button href="/events/new">Plan a cleanup</Button> (first two lines). What is the right fix?
src/Scratch.tsx:6:15 - error TS2322: Type '{ href: string; children: string; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & { as?: "button" | undefined; variant?: ButtonVariant | undefined; } & Omit<DetailedHTMLProps<ButtonHTMLAttributes<HTMLButtonElement>, HTMLButtonElement>, "as" | "className">'.
  Property 'href' does not exist on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & { as?: "button" | undefined; variant?: ButtonVariant | undefined; } & Omit<DetailedHTMLProps<ButtonHTMLAttributes<HTMLButtonElement>, HTMLButtonElement>, "as" | "className">'. Did you mean 'ref'?
A) Add as="a": a link needs the props of an a element
B) Rename href to ref, as TypeScript suggests
C) Add href to ButtonProps
D) Give the Button variant="link"
Why: Without as, C is the default 'button', and a button has no href. The props follow the as prop: with as="a" the same href is valid. Read a long error from its last line; the Did you mean is a spelling guess, not a design answer.
B is wrong: ref is a real prop, but it is not what you meant: the guess comes from the spelling.
C is wrong: Then every button would accept an href it ignores.
D is wrong: The variant changes the look, not the element: it would still be a button.
Q9
Three errors, one cause. Which do you fix first, and how does DataList avoid the problem altogether?
src/Scratch.tsx:21:18 - error TS2322: Type 'DebrisCount[]' is not assignable to type '{ id: string; }[]'.
src/Scratch.tsx:21:62 - error TS2339: Property 'category' does not exist on type '{ id: string; }'.
src/Scratch.tsx:21:80 - error TS2339: Property 'count' does not exist on type '{ id: string; }'.
Found 3 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:21
The first: DebrisCount has no id, so it does not satisfy the constraint; DataList asks for a getKey function instead of requiring an id
Why: When T fails its constraint, TypeScript falls back to { id: string }, which is why the next two errors say category and count do not exist. Fix the first error, then check again. A constraint should ask for exactly what the component uses (Lesson 5.4, Why getKey and not item.id?).
Q10
In moveBetweenTabs, why must the code check move === undefined and next === undefined before next.focus()?
A) noUncheckedIndexedAccess types moves[event.key] and tabs[...] as possibly undefined
B) React requires every event handler to return early
C) Arrow keys can fire twice in Strict Mode
D) oxlint's jsx-a11y rules ask for it
Why: The HQ has had noUncheckedIndexedAccess on since Lesson 2.4: reading a Record or an array by index gives T | undefined, so TypeScript will not let you call next.focus() until you have ruled undefined out. A key that is not in moves returns early, without preventDefault.
B is wrong: React has no such rule.
C is wrong: Strict Mode doubles renders and effects, not key events.
D is wrong: The a11y rule in this lesson is about a key handler on an element that cannot take focus.
Q11
On Storm Debris Sweep (Details and Impact tabs), the focus is on Details. You press End, then ←, then the letter a. Which tab is selected?
A) Impact
B) None: the letter a clears the selection
C) The focus has moved into the panel
D) Details
Why: End jumps to the last tab (Impact); ← moves back and wraps, here to Details; a is not in moves, so nothing happens. Each move focuses AND clicks the tab: automatic activation (Lesson 5.4, Exercise 1).
A is wrong: That is the state after End alone.
B is wrong: Unknown keys return without doing anything.
C is wrong: Tab (the key) moves focus into the panel; arrow keys stay in the tab list.
Q12
You switch the HQ's Tabs to the dot form (Tabs.List, Tabs.Tab, Tabs.Panel) with Object.assign. It type-checks; what does npm run lint say (first warning shown), and what does it cost?
  ⚠ react(only-export-components): Fast refresh only works when a file only exports components. Move your component(s) to a separate file.
    ╭─[src/components/Tabs.tsx:37:10]
 36 │ // The parent: owns which tab is selected and shares it with the parts inside
 37 │ function TabsRoot({ defaultTab, children }: TabsProps) {
    ·          ────────
 38 │   const [selected, setSelected] = useState(defaultTab)
Four react(only-export-components) warnings, one per part; Fast Refresh may reload the whole page instead of keeping the components' state
Why: The file now exports an object, not only components. Named exports (import { Tab, TabList, TabPanel, Tabs }) keep editing smooth and say just as clearly that the parts belong together (Lesson 5.4, Common Questions).

5.4 · Component Patterns: predict, then check

P3
npx tsc -b on src/Scratch.tsx
import type { ReactNode } from 'react'
import type { DebrisCount } from './api/types.ts'

interface IdListProps<T extends { id: string }> {
  items: T[]
  renderItem: (item: T) => ReactNode
}

// Only for items that have an id: the constraint lets the list use item.id as the key
function IdList<T extends { id: string }>({ items, renderItem }: IdListProps<T>) {
  return (
    <ul>
      {items.map((item) => (
        <li key={item.id}>{renderItem(item)}</li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  )
}

export function PiecesCounted({ items }: { items: DebrisCount[] }) {
  return <IdList items={items} renderItem={(item) => `${item.category}: ${item.count}`} />
}
src/Scratch.tsx:21:18 - error TS2322: Type 'DebrisCount[]' is not assignable to type '{ id: string; }[]'.
  Property 'id' is missing in type 'DebrisCount' but required in type '{ id: string; }'.

21   return <IdList items={items} renderItem={(item) => `${item.category}: ${item.count}`} />
                    ~~~~~

  src/Scratch.tsx:10:29 - 'id' is declared here.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 5.3 · Rendering Performance
  1. B Their parent rendered, so React rendered them too: The search text is EventsPage state. When EventsPage renders, it creates EventGroups, the lists and the cards again, and without memo or the compiler React renders them all.
  2. D onRemove gets a new function on every render of the parent: A function declared in the parent's body is a new function on each render, so memo sees a changed onRemove prop. useCallback, or the React Compiler, keeps it the same.
  3. C Nothing: production builds of React do not call onRender: Production builds of React leave profiling out, so onRender is never called. Measure on the dev server (or with a special profiling build), and compare numbers on the same setup.
  4. A It skips the whole component and leaves it as written, without failing the build: The compiler only optimizes code that follows the Rules of React. It leaves this component exactly as written, without a message in the build; the lint warning, with its note that the compiler skipped the component, is your signal.
  5. D In the provider, keep the value the same object with useMemo (and toggleUnit with useCallback): The provider builds a new value object on each render, and every reader renders when the value changes. useCallback for toggleUnit plus useMemo for the object keep the value the same until unit really changes. Measured: zero renders while typing.
Lesson 5.4 · Component Patterns
  1. B It reads the selected id from a private context that Tabs provides: Tabs keeps the selected id in state and provides it through TabsContext. Each Tab reads the context with useTabs and compares its own id, so the caller never wires anything by hand.
  2. C DebrisCount, inferred from items: TypeScript infers the type parameter T from the items prop: DebrisCount. Then getKey and renderItem are checked against it, so item.category is known.
  3. A An error: disabled does not exist on the props of an a element: With as set to a, the props are an anchor's props, and an a element has no disabled attribute, so TypeScript reports TS2322: Property disabled does not exist. The props follow the as prop.
  4. D So the panels stay mounted and keep their state, like the check-in marks: A removed panel unmounts its content, and the state in it is lost: measured, the check-in marks went back to 0 of 7 and the roster was fetched again. A hidden panel stays mounted and keeps its state.
  5. B Only the call site: as={Link} to="/events/new" replaces as="a" href="/events/new": as accepts any ElementType, components included, and ComponentPropsWithRef of Link brings in its to prop. Only the call site changes, from as a with href to as Link with to; Button itself stays as it is.

Exit Ticket

  1. Three reasons: its own state changed (a set call or dispatch); its parent rendered; a context it reads changed.
  2. A memo component with no props that still renders: it reads a context whose value is a new object on every render of the provider; keep the value the same object with useMemo and its functions with useCallback (or let the compiler do it). memo never blocks a context update.
  3. A pattern and its HQ component: compound components, Tabs (TabList, Tab, TabPanel); render prop + generic component, DataList<T>; polymorphic as prop, Button.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or B. Thinks the compiler caches everything, or nothing. Return to the three reasons: a context change always renders its readers.
  • Q2 A. Reads the warning as style advice. Run the lesson's Gloves case: the stale value is a real bug.
  • Q4 A or B. Fixes the symptom (the type) instead of the setup. The option is gone from plugin-react 6.
  • Q5 A or B. Mixes the three tools. memo skips a component, useCallback keeps a function, useMemo keeps a value.
  • Q8 B. Follows the Did you mean. Read the last line, then ask what element the props belong to.
  • Q11 A. Forgets that ← wraps. Walk moveBetweenTabs's moves record with the two tabs on the board.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 15: React Router 8, Search Params and Nested Routes

Answers and reasoning for every Session 15 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 15 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: From Session 14

  1. W1. Only the call site: as={Link} to="/events/new". Button's props follow as (ComponentPropsWithRef), so to becomes required and href an error; Button.tsx itself does not change. Today's demo does it.
  2. W2. Only when the component MOUNTS. If React keeps the same component for the next cleanup, the old choice stays: today's key={eventId}.
  3. W3. The whole app loads again: HTML, JavaScript and fonts are fetched or re-run, the session is read again from sessionStorage, every page's data is fetched again, and anything in memory is gone. Today's router keeps the app running.

6.1 · React Router 8: self-check

Q1
Measured in the HQ after Lesson 6.1: from the home page, through Events, one cleanup, Back, Forward and the logo back home. How many HTML pages did the browser load?
A) Six, one for each step
B) One: the first
C) Three: one for each different address
D) None: the dev server keeps the page
Why: A router link renders a real <a href> but cancels the browser's page load on a plain click; the router writes the address with the History API and renders the matching route. Back and Forward work without a page load too (Lesson 6.1, Links That Do Not Reload).
A is wrong: That was the HQ before the router: every plain link reloaded the whole app.
C is wrong: The address changes each time, but the document does not.
D is wrong: The very first visit still loads the app's index.html.
Q2
What does npx tsc -b say about this route list?
import { createBrowserRouter } from 'react-router'
import { EventsPage } from './pages/EventsPage.tsx'
import { NewEventPage } from './pages/NewEventPage.tsx'

// An index route is the page AT its parent's address: it cannot have pages of its own
export const router = createBrowserRouter([
  {
    index: true,
    element: <EventsPage />,
    children: [{ path: 'new', element: <NewEventPage /> }],
  },
])
A) Nothing: React Router ignores children on an index route
B) error TS2339: Property 'children' does not exist
C) error TS2322: children must be undefined, because index: true picked the index member of the RouteObject union
D) Nothing at build time; the browser shows an error screen
Why: RouteObject is a discriminated union: IndexRouteObject has children?: undefined. The real message ends with Type '[{ path: string; element: Element; }]' is not assignable to type 'undefined'.: read it from the bottom up. An index route is the page AT its parent's address, so it cannot have pages of its own.
A is wrong: TypeScript checks every route object's shape.
B is wrong: children exists, but its type on this member is undefined.
D is wrong: The type checker catches it before anything runs.
Q3
tsc prints this for a new back link. What is the fix?
src/Scratch.tsx:4:16 - error TS2322: Type '{ href: string; children: string; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & LinkProps & RefAttributes<HTMLAnchorElement>'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:4
Use to instead of href: <Link to="/events">
Why: A router Link takes to; it still renders a real <a href> in the browser. TypeScript lists Link's props, so the typo is caught (and the Did you mean points at ref, which is not the fix). The same rule makes <Button as={Link}> require to (Lesson 6.1, Link through the polymorphic Button).
Q4
The sites page throws while rendering. The errorElement sits on the LAYOUT route (path: '/' with AppLayout). What does the visitor see?
A) The header with Sites marked current, the message and the footer
B) React Router's own Unexpected Application Error! screen
C) A blank page with an error in the Console
D) The message, but no header and no footer: the error element replaced the layout too
Why: An error element replaces the route it sits on, layout included. On a pathless route INSIDE the layout it replaces only the page, which is what the HQ does (Lesson 6.1, When a Page Crashes, measured three ways).
A is wrong: That is the pathless route inside the layout: the HQ's choice.
B is wrong: That is what happens with no errorElement on any route.
C is wrong: React Router catches the error; the page is not blank.
Q5
The route is written 'events/:id' while EventPage reads eventId from useParams(). What happens?
A) npx tsc -b and npm run lint pass; on /events/e08 the page shows "Something went wrong on this page" and the Console names EventPage's error
B) npx tsc -b reports TS2339: eventId does not exist
C) The cleanup page works: React Router matches the names by position
D) Page not found
Why: Both sides are just strings, so TypeScript stays quiet (the kit ran it: npx tsc -b printed nothing). eventId is undefined, EventPage throws its own message, and the errorElement shows the crash page. Lesson 6.2's useParams<'eventId'>() names the parameter; it still cannot see the route list.
B is wrong: Untyped useParams() allows any name; each value is string | undefined.
C is wrong: Parameters are matched by name, not position.
D is wrong: The address still matches 'events/:id'; only the name differs.
Q6
Why does EventPage put key={eventId} on its ErrorBoundary?
When only the parameter changes the route stays the same, so React keeps the components and their state; a new key mounts fresh ones (boundary, Suspense, tabs)
Why: Measured in the lesson: without a key, going from one cleanup to another kept the old selected tab and showed no panel. A key is React's identity for a component (list keys, Lesson 3.3).

6.1 · React Router 8: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then try it.
import { BrowserRouter, Route, Routes } from 'react-router'
import { EventsPage } from './pages/EventsPage.tsx'
import { HomePage } from './pages/HomePage.tsx'

// Declarative mode: the same routes as JSX. Fine for small apps, but no loaders or actions.
export function DeclarativeApp() {
  return (
    <BrowserRouter>
      <Routes>
        <Route index element={<HomePage />} />
        <Route path="events" element={<EventsPage />} />
      </Routes>
    </BrowserRouter>
  )
}
npx tsc -b
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: Declarative mode is still part of React Router 8 and imports from react-router; this version compiles in the HQ. It cannot load data per route or handle form actions, which the HQ uses from Lesson 6.3, so the HQ starts in data mode (the lesson's FAQ).
P2
Predict, then try it.
// App.tsx: only the route's parameter is renamed
{ path: 'events/:id', element: <EventPage /> },

// EventPage.tsx is unchanged
const { eventId } = useParams()
npx tsc -b
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: Both sides are just strings, so TypeScript and the linter stay quiet (run for real: the route renamed in a copy of the HQ, then npx tsc -b). In the browser, /events/e08 shows "Something went wrong on this page" and EventPage's own message in the Console. Self-check Q5.

6.2 · Search Params, Nested Routes and Typed Navigation: self-check

Q7
What does the second line print?
const params = new URLSearchParams('q=pelican point&status=completed')
console.log(params.get('q'), params.get('page'), params.has('status'))
A) pelican point null true
B) pelican+point undefined true
C) pelican point '' true
D) pelican%20point null false
Why: get decodes the value (+ or %20 become a space) and returns null for a missing name: string | null. toString() writes spaces as + again (q=jetty&status=completed after a set). URLSearchParams is a browser class, so this ran in Node too.
B is wrong: Spaces come back as spaces, and a missing name is null, not undefined.
C is wrong: Missing means null, not an empty string.
D is wrong: get decodes, and has('status') is true.
Q8
const status: StatusFilter = searchParams.get('status') ?? 'all' gives this. How does the HQ fix it, and why not with as StatusFilter?
src/Scratch.tsx:6:9 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'StatusFilter'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:6
readParam(searchParams, 'status', isEventStatus) ?? 'all': a type predicate checks the text; an as would let ?status=banana through
Why: The address is input from outside the app, like the API's JSON in Lesson 2.4: check it, then trust it. Measured: /events?status=banana showed All statuses and all 15 cleanups.
Q9
Signed in as Maya on the crew page: Next page →, click Kwame Asante, Next page → again (address /volunteers/v09?page=3), then type ma in the search. Now Back. Where are you?
A) /volunteers/v09?page=3
B) /volunteers/v09?q=m
C) /volunteers/v09?page=2
D) /volunteers
Why: Typing uses replace: true, so the first letter REPLACED the ?page=3 entry and the letters added none; Back goes to the entry before it (Lesson 6.2, Exercise 1, measured).
A is wrong: That entry was replaced by the typing.
B is wrong: Replace means the letters are never places to go back to.
D is wrong: That is several entries further back.
Q10
Someone removes <Outlet /> from VolunteersPage. Signed in as Maya, they click Aisha Rahman. What happens?
A) npx tsc -b fails: the child route has nowhere to render
B) React Router shows its error screen
C) The card replaces the list
D) The address becomes /volunteers/v20 and her name turns bold, but no card appears and no request is sent
Why: The router matched the child route; there is simply nowhere to draw it, and nothing complains (tsc, lint and the Console were all quiet, measured). A nested page that does nothing: check the parent's Outlet first.
A is wrong: Routes and outlets are not connected by types.
B is wrong: Nothing throws.
C is wrong: Children render at the parent's Outlet, never instead of the parent.
Q11
These four lines are in a Scratch.tsx in the HQ (data mode). How many errors does npx tsc -b report?
import { generatePath, href } from 'react-router'

// href(): checked only in framework mode, where React Router generates the list of routes
export const typoInPath = href('/volunters/:volunteerId', { volunteerId: 'v04' })
export const missingValue = href('/volunteers/:volunteerId')

// generatePath(): reads the parameter names from the pattern you pass
export const fine = generatePath('/volunteers/:volunteerId', { volunteerId: 'v04' })
export const typoInName = generatePath('/volunteers/:volunteerId', { volunterId: 'v04' })
A) Four: each line has a mistake
B) One, on the generatePath line with volunterId
C) Two, on the two href lines
D) None: all four compile
Why: href() is typed from the generated route list of framework mode; in data mode it accepts any path and any params, so the typo path and the missing value compile (and fail at run time). generatePath reads the names from the pattern: error TS2741.
A is wrong: The fine line is fine, and both href lines compile.
C is wrong: That is what href would report in framework mode.
D is wrong: generatePath does check the parameter names.
Q12
In the crew list, names link with to={{ pathname: volunteer.id, search: searchParams.toString() }}; the Pagination links use to={{ search }} with no pathname. What does each keep?
The name links keep the search (page, q, skill) and change the path; the page links keep the path (the open card) and change only the search
Why: Links resolve relative to their route, and a path alone drops the query: on page 2, a name link without the search jumped back to Page 1 of 3 (Common Mistakes). Next page from /volunteers/v16?page=2 went to /volunteers/v16?page=3.

6.2 · Search Params, Nested Routes and Typed Navigation: predict, then check

P3
Predict, then check.
import { readPageNumber, withParam } from './searchParams.ts'

for (const search of ['', 'page=2', 'page=zero', 'page=2.5', 'page=0']) {
  console.log(JSON.stringify(search), '->', readPageNumber(new URLSearchParams(search)))
}
const current = new URLSearchParams('q=pelican&status=completed')
console.log(withParam(current, 'status', '').toString(), '|', current.toString())
"" -> 1
"page=2" -> 2
"page=zero" -> 1
"page=2.5" -> 1
"page=0" -> 1
q=pelican | q=pelican&status=completed
Why: A missing page is Number(null), which is 0, so it fails page > 0 and becomes 1; zero is NaN, 2.5 is not an integer, 0 is not above 0: all page 1. withParam deleted the empty status on a COPY: the original still has both names.
P4
Predict, then check.
http://localhost:4000/api/volunteers/v99
HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "not_found",
  "message": "No volunteer with that id."
}
Why: 404 with the API's own message. In the HQ, the crew page's card shows "Could not load this volunteer. Nothing was found at this address. Check the link.": describeError's new 404 sentence from Step 3 (Lesson 6.2, Step 7).

✓ Task 1: /sites shows the list of sites inside the layout

✓ Task 1: a page that crashes shows the error page, and the header stays

What each task shows: (1) the layout route keeps the header; a pathless route INSIDE it holds errorElement: <PageProblem /> and the pages, so /reports shows the error page with the header, and /sites/driftwood-cove/photos falls through to * (a parameter matches one segment); (2) <Link to={/sites/${site.id}}> (relative to={site.id} works too), useParams<'siteId'>() narrowed by a throw, an unknown id answered by a heading instead of a crash, and NavLinks in the header; (3) the lesson's focus effect, keyed on the pathname and guarded by a ref so the first page keeps the focus where the browser put it. Accept Component: SitesPage instead of element, and a separate NotFound for unknown ids.

Handout: labs/module-15/l6_1-react-router-8/debug_outside_router.test.tsx (deliberately broken)

Copied into src/ and run with npx vitest run src/debug_outside_router.test.tsx, it fails with (first line of the error):

TypeError: Cannot destructure property 'basename' of 'React$1.useContext(...)' as it is null.
Tests  1 failed (1)

The lesson's "Link outside the router" message. The variable name before .useContext depends on the bundler (the browser build says import_react, Vitest says React$1); the rest is the same. SitesPage's Links look for a router in React context and find null. Fix the test: render(<RouterProvider router={createMemoryRouter([{ path: '/sites', element: <SitesPage /> }], { initialEntries: ['/sites'] })} />), then findAllByRole('link'). Then the test passes; the page was never the problem.

Lab 6.2 (guided): labs/module-15/l6_2-search-params-nested-routes-and-typed-navigation/starter → solution

As shipped, npm test fails all 8 (the first message is TODO 1), and npm run typecheck reports one error, in the test file, because the non-generic readChoice returns string | undefined:

Tests  8 failed (8)
src/lab.test.tsx:21:9 - error TS2322: Type 'string | undefined' is not assignable to type 'EventStatus | undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/lab.test.tsx:21

The solution: npm test (npm run typecheck prints nothing after its two > lines; lint as in Lab 6.1):

✓ Task 1: readChoice keeps an allowed value and refuses anything else
✓ Task 1: withParams changes a copy, keeps the rest and removes empty values
✓ Task 2: the filters come from the address
✓ Task 2: a value that is not allowed is ignored, an allowed one is kept
✓ Task 2: choosing a site keeps the status, and Back undoes it
✓ Task 3: /log shows the hint where the details go
✓ Task 3: a title opens the cleanup beside the list and keeps the filters
✓ Task 3: an id that is not in the log says so
Tests  8 passed (8)

What each task shows: (1) readChoice<T extends string>(params, name, allowed: readonly T[]): T | undefined returns allowed.find((choice) => choice === value): the comparison does the checking and find hands back a value that is already a T, so there is no as; withParams copies with new URLSearchParams(params) and deletes empty values; (2) the filters read from the address with readChoice, written with setSearchParams((current) => withParams(current, { [name]: value })) (a push, so router.navigate(-1) brings back ?status=scheduled), Clear filters = setSearchParams({}) (the address becomes /log); (3) /log gets an index child (the hint) and :cleanupId, <Outlet /> sits in CleanupLog, and the links are { pathname: cleanup.id, search: searchParams.toString() } NavLinks, so opening Sunset Stairs Cleanup from ?status=completed keeps the filter and marks the title current. Accept a predicate-based helper like the lesson's readParam if the learner explains the difference.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 6.1 · React Router 8
  1. C Changes the address without a page load, and the router renders the sites page in the layout's Outlet: Link cancels the normal page load, the router writes the new address into history, matches the routes, and React renders SitesPage into the Outlet. The header, the session and the loaded code stay.
  2. A string | undefined, because TypeScript cannot know which route rendered the page: useParams cannot see which route rendered the component, so every parameter may be missing. The page narrows it with a check before using it as a string.
  3. D On a pathless route inside the layout route, around the pages: The error element replaces the route it belongs to, including that route's element. On a pathless route inside the layout, only the page area is replaced, so the header, footer and navigation keep working.
  4. B The route stayed the same, so React kept the components and the old selected tab, which the new cleanup does not have: The route was the same, so React kept the same components and their state. Tabs kept impact from the old cleanup, and useState ignores its default after the first render. A key from the event id makes React mount fresh components.
  5. A The reload asks the server for /events, and the host has no such file until a SPA fallback serves index.html for every address: A reload asks the server for the address. The host has only index.html and the assets, so it answers 404 unless a SPA fallback rule serves index.html for every address, after which the router takes over.
Lesson 6.2 · Search Params, Nested Routes and Typed Navigation
  1. B /events?q=pelican: The status change pushed a new entry, so Back removes it. The typing replaced entries instead of adding them, so the search stays and the menu shows All statuses again.
  2. D string | null: URLSearchParams.get returns the text of the first value with that name, or null when there is none. The HQ narrows it with readParam and a type predicate before using it as a StatusFilter.
  3. A At the <Outlet /> inside VolunteersPage, beside the list: The :volunteerId route is a child of the volunteers route, so it renders at the Outlet inside VolunteersPage. The search, the list and the layout stay mounted around it.
  4. C Nothing: in data mode href accepts any path, so the link would lead to "Page not found": href only checks paths against the route list that framework mode generates. In data mode it accepts any string, so the typo compiles and the link leads to Page not found. The measurement showed exactly that.
  5. A Router updates run as low-priority transitions; without it the box lost letters and the cursor jumped to the end: React Router applies its updates as transitions, which can wait. A text box whose value comes from the address then shows a stale value for a moment, so the cursor jumps and fast letters are lost. flushSync applies that one update immediately.

Exit Ticket

  1. Three routes: the layout route (AppLayout: header, footer, <Outlet />, focus and scroll after a navigation), the pathless route (errorElement: <PageCrashed />, so a crashing page keeps the layout), the index route (HomePage at /).
  2. Who reads what: the router reads the path (/events) to choose the page; the page reads the query string (q, status) to decide what to show. searchParams.get('status') is string | null.
  3. Keep the search: a link with only a path drops the query string, so on page 2 the list would jump back to Page 1 of 3 (and lose a search or skill filter). { pathname, search } passes it along.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A ("six"). Still thinks of links as page loads. Open the Network panel filtered to Doc and click through the HQ: one document.
  • Q2 A or D. Expects routes to be untyped configuration. Hover createBrowserRouter's parameter: RouteObject[].
  • Q4 A. Has not separated "the route the error element sits on" from "the page that crashed". Draw the tree; cross out everything under the route with the error element.
  • Q5 B. Believes useParams knows the route list. It does not, even with a type argument (Lesson 6.2).
  • Q7 B or D. Has not met URL decoding: run P3's first line in the browser Console.
  • Q9 A. Treats replace as "no history at all" rather than "overwrite the current entry". Walk Exercise 1 on the projector.
  • Q11 C or D. Trusts a function's name. That is exactly the lesson's point: test the promise with a deliberate typo.

Session 16: Data Loading and Protected Routes

Answers and reasoning for every Session 16 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson page's own quiz, the Part 2 kickoff reference and the exit ticket.

Session 16 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: From Session 15

  1. W1. The planning form, for anyone who knows the address. What stops a guest is the API: POST /api/events answers 401 without a token (Lesson 5.2). Today's guard adds the courtesy: guests are sent to sign in, then straight back.
  2. W2. string | null; readParam with a type predicate (isEventStatus) and ?? 'all', never as. Today ?next= is the same kind of outside input, and nextPath checks it.
  3. W3. At the parent's <Outlet />; the parent stays mounted. Today a route with no path holds the guard, and every page below it is a child. New twist: the parent's loader and a child's loader run at the same time.

6.3 · Data Loading and Protected Routes: self-check

Q1
Measured with the API slowed down (--latency=1500): 300 ms after clicking a cleanup in the events list of the finished HQ, what is on screen?
A) The events list, a little faded, the address still /events, and a small "Loading…" sign at the bottom
B) The cleanup page with "Loading the cleanup…" where the details will go
C) A blank main area until the cleanup arrives
D) The address /events/e17 with the old list still under it
Why: With a loader the router waits before it swaps the page: the old page stays (AppLayout fades it with aria-busy) and useNavigation().state is 'loading', which shows the one role="status" sign. "Loading the cleanup" never appeared (Lesson 6.3, While the loader runs).
B is wrong: That is render then fetch: the HQ before this lesson.
C is wrong: The current page stays until the next one is ready.
D is wrong: The address changes when the navigation is done, together with the page.
Q2
These lines are in a scratch file in the finished HQ. Which line does npx tsc -b NOT report?
  const sites: number = useLoaderData<typeof sitesLoader>()
  const page: number = useLoaderData<typeof eventLoader>()
  const result: number = useActionData<typeof signInAction>()
  const untyped: number = useLoaderData()
A) The sites line
B) The event page line
C) The sign-in result line
D) The untyped line
Why: The kit ran it: three TS2322 errors, on the lines with a type argument (Site[], the event loader's object, SignInResult | undefined). useLoaderData() with no type argument is any, and any is assignable to number and everything else: always pass typeof yourLoader.
A is wrong: Site[] is not a number: reported.
B is wrong: An object is not a number: reported.
C is wrong: SignInResult | undefined is not a number: reported.
Q3
After Step 3 of the project (the session store, signIn removed from the context), tsc prints this. Is it bad news, and what fixes it?
src/pages/SignInPage.tsx:50:20 - error TS2339: Property 'signIn' does not exist on type 'AuthValue'.
Found 1 error in src/pages/SignInPage.tsx:50
Good news: the compiler lists the one place that still signs in the old way. Step 4 fixes it: signInAction signs in, and SignInPage reads useActionData
Why: Signing in moved out of React into the route action, so signIn left AuthValue. A red error after a refactor is the compiler's to-do list (the lesson's own words).
Q4
A loader for a cleanup's page can await a request or return it as a promise. Which one should it AWAIT?
A) The impact report, so the Impact tab never says Loading
B) Nothing: awaiting in a loader blocks the browser
C) The cleanup itself: the page makes no sense without it, and a 404 must decide the answer
D) Both, always, so the page appears complete
Why: Await what the page cannot do without and what decides the answer (404, redirect); return a promise for what can follow. The impact report is returned unawaited and use()d inside Suspense, so a missing report costs one tab, not the page (Lesson 6.3, Await what the page needs).
A is wrong: Then everyone waits for two requests, and an error in the report costs the whole page.
B is wrong: The router awaits the loader; the old page stays on screen meanwhile.
D is wrong: That makes every visit as slow as the slowest part.
Q5
Why does the HQ's ErrorPage write answer?.statusText || 'Something went wrong on this page' with || and not ???
A) ?? does not work after optional chaining
B) statusText is a number
C) A response without a status text has '', which should fall back too; ?? only falls back for null and undefined
D) || narrows unknown, ?? does not
Why: An empty string is not null, so ?? would keep it and show an empty heading. || falls back for every falsy value, which here is exactly the empty text (Lesson 6.3, Step 1).
A is wrong: ?? works fine after ?.; it just falls back too rarely here.
B is wrong: statusText is a string; status is the number.
D is wrong: Neither operator narrows unknown; isRouteErrorResponse does.
Q6
The HQ's nextPath gets the address /sign-in?next=%2F%2Fexample.com. What does it return?
export function nextPath(url: URL): string {
  const next = url.searchParams.get('next')
  if (next === null || !next.startsWith('/') || next.startsWith('//') || next.startsWith('/\\')) {
    return '/'
  }
  return next
}
A) //example.com
B) /
C) /example.com
D) It throws, and the sign-in page shows the error page
Why: The value is //example.com: it starts with / but also with //, which browsers read as another site, so nextPath gives the home page. The kit ran the stage's own function: //example.com gives /. Without the check, signing in would hand the person to someone else's page (an open redirect).
A is wrong: That is the open redirect the check exists to stop.
C is wrong: nextPath never rewrites a value; it accepts it or returns /.
D is wrong: It returns a string for every input.
Q7
Theo (a volunteer) is signed in and opens /events/new. Why does the guard answer 403 "Organizers only" instead of redirecting him to sign in?
A) Signing in again would not help him: he is signed in, just not allowed, so a redirect would only loop
B) A redirect cannot be thrown from a loader
C) The API answers 403, and the guard copies it
D) 403 pages are faster than redirects
Why: 401 means "I don't know who you are" (sign in), 403 means "I know, and no". The guard throws data(message, { status: 403, statusText: 'Organizers only' }); the shared ErrorPage shows the heading and the sentence (the lesson's screenshot).
B is wrong: Guards throw redirect() for guests all the time.
C is wrong: The guard runs before any request; it never asks the API.
D is wrong: Speed has nothing to do with it.
Q8
Someone writes organizersOnly with the Hook they already have. tsc is quiet; oxlint says this. Why, and what does the HQ do instead?
  × react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "useAuth" is called in function "organizersOnly" that is neither a React function component nor a custom React Hook function. React component names must start with an uppercase letter. React Hook names must start with the word "use".
A loader runs outside React, before any component renders, and Hooks only work while a component renders; the guard reads the store with readSession()
Why: It is an error, not a warning (exit 1). That is why the session moved into a store in client.ts: the guards read it, the sign-in action writes it, request() clears it on a 401, and React subscribes with useSyncExternalStore.
Q9
The guard route has NO shouldRevalidate. As Maya on /volunteers you type "mra", move the cursor two places left and type "a". What happens (measured)?
A) Nothing unusual: the guard is instant
B) npx tsc -b reports that the route needs shouldRevalidate
C) oxlint warns about the guard route
D) The box shows "mara" but the cursor jumps to position 4 instead of 2
Why: Every letter changes the search, the router re-runs the route's loaders by default, and a navigation with a loader waits for it, even an instant one; flushSync cannot help with that wait. onlyWhenThePathChanges skips the guard when only the search changed. Both versions type-check and lint cleanly: only the behavior tells you.
A is wrong: Instant is not the same as no wait: the navigation still waits for the loader.
B is wrong: Types cannot see this; it is runtime behavior.
C is wrong: Both versions lint cleanly.
Q10
Exercise 3 gives the nested :volunteerId route its own volunteerLoader, but WITHOUT calling organizersOnly(args) inside it. A guest opens /volunteers/v16. What happens?
A) The card appears: the child loader ran first
B) The error page shows "Organizers only"
C) The guest ends on the sign-in page, but the browser has already sent GET /api/volunteers/v16 (answered 401)
D) Nothing is sent: the parent guard always runs before the child loader
Why: Loaders of a route and its children run at the same time, not one after the other; the guard's redirect wins, but the child's request is already out. Calling organizersOnly(args) first in the child loader stops it (measured: no request). Today's lab tests the same thing: without the call, 3 tests fail.
A is wrong: The guard's redirect still wins.
B is wrong: A guest is redirected; 403 is for signed-in volunteers.
D is wrong: That is the assumption Exercise 3 disproves.
Q11
Maya types a wrong password into the HQ's sign-in page (a React Router <Form> with signInAction). Without the lesson's passwordRef effect, what would the password box hold afterwards?
A) Nothing: React resets every form after an action
B) The wrong password she typed: a router Form keeps what was typed
C) The text "Email or password is incorrect."
D) Her previous, correct password from sessionStorage
Why: React 19's form actions reset the fields; React Router's actions do not (the lesson's comparison table, measured before the fix). The effect on [result] empties the password through its ref and moves the focus, keeping Lesson 5.2's promise: keep the email, never the password.
A is wrong: That is the React 19 form action of Lesson 5.2, not the router's.
C is wrong: The message is shown in the role="alert" paragraph, not in the field.
D is wrong: Passwords are never stored.
Q12
useSyncExternalStore(onSessionChange, readSession): why does readSession() return the saved variable instead of reading and parsing sessionStorage on each call?
getSnapshot must return the SAME object until the session really changes; parsing creates a new object on every call, so React would see a change on every render
Why: React compares snapshots with Object.is: a fresh object each time means "changed" every time (React warns and re-renders without end). The store keeps current, and only saveSession replaces it and calls the listeners.

6.3 · Data Loading and Protected Routes: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then check.
import { useLoaderData } from 'react-router'

// No type argument: useLoaderData() returns any, so this typo compiles
export function Scratch() {
  const sites = useLoaderData()
  return <p>{sites[0].nmae}</p>
}
npx tsc -b
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: useLoaderData() without a type argument is any, so sites[0].nmae compiles (run for real in a copy of the finished HQ) and shows nothing on screen. With useLoaderData<typeof sitesLoader>() the typo is an error and sites[0] is Site | undefined.
P2
Predict, then check.
// nextPath, copied from src/auth/guards.ts: it needs nothing but URL
export function nextPath(url: URL): string {
  const next = url.searchParams.get('next')
  if (next === null || !next.startsWith('/') || next.startsWith('//') || next.startsWith('/\\')) {
    return '/'
  }
  return next
}

// What the guard puts in ?next=: an address inside an address, so it is encoded
console.log(encodeURIComponent('/volunteers?skill=first-aid'))

for (const next of ['/volunteers?skill=first-aid', 'events', '//example.com', 'https://elsewhere.example', '/\\elsewhere.example']) {
  const url = new URL(`http://localhost:5173/sign-in?next=${encodeURIComponent(next)}`)
  console.log(next.padEnd(28), '->', nextPath(url))
}
%2Fvolunteers%3Fskill%3Dfirst-aid
/volunteers?skill=first-aid  -> /volunteers?skill=first-aid
events                       -> /
//example.com                -> /
https://elsewhere.example    -> /
/\elsewhere.example          -> /
Why: The first line is what the guard writes into ?next= for this address. Only a path that starts with ONE / survives: events has no slash, //example.com and /\elsewhere.example are read by browsers as another site, and a full address is another site. Everything else goes home (/).
P3
Predict, then check.
http://localhost:4000/api/volunteers
HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "forbidden",
  "message": "Only organizers can do that."
}
Why: 403 with the API's own message: Theo is signed in, just not allowed. The HQ's guard answers the same way before any request ("Organizers only"), and the API's answer is still what keeps the crew's contact details safe.

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 6.3 · Data Loading and Protected Routes
  1. D On /sign-in?next=%2Fvolunteers%3Fskill%3Dfirst-aid: organizersOnly finds no session and throws a redirect to the sign-in page, with the path and search you asked for encoded into next. Nothing on the crew page renders and no request for the crew is sent.
  2. B any: The type parameter defaults to any, so anything you do with the result compiles, typos included. Pass typeof yourLoader and TypeScript works out the real type from the loader's return.
  3. C A loader runs outside React, and Hooks only work while a component renders: The router calls loaders before any component renders, and Hooks only work during a component's render. That is why the session moved into a store in client.ts that plain functions can read, and React subscribes to with useSyncExternalStore.
  4. A The page appears once the cleanup arrives; the Impact tab says "Loading the impact report…" until the report follows: The router waits only for what the loader awaited. The page appears with the cleanup, and the Impact tab's Suspense boundary shows its fallback until the report's promise resolves, as measured with a slowed API.
  5. B She is sent to /sign-in?next=%2Fvolunteers%2Fv20, and after signing in she is on Aisha's card: The card's request comes back 401, so request forgets the session. The store tells its listeners: the layout revalidates, the guard finds no session and redirects to sign-in with next set to Aisha's address, which is where the sign-in action sends her afterwards.

Part 2 Project Kickoff: reference

Exit Ticket

  1. First function and address: organizersOnly, the loader of the pathless guard route, finds no session and throws a redirect; the browser ends on /sign-in?next=%2Fvolunteers%3Fskill%3Dfirst-aid (path and search encoded), and no crew request is sent.
  2. Answer or crash: route answers for what the app expects: an unknown id (404 "Cleanup not found"), the wrong role (403 "Organizers only"). Let through: the API being down or a bug (the ErrorPage's alert with describeError and Try again).
  3. Milestone: R1 and R2 working, R3 started. Pairs as named at the kickoff (alone is fine).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B. Still pictures render then fetch. Show the lesson's pending screenshot: the old list, faded, with the sign.
  • Q2 A, B or C. Has not seen that any is assignable to everything; run P1 together.
  • Q4 A or D. Treats "complete" as better than "fast". Ask what one failing report would cost each version.
  • Q6 A. Believes a leading / is enough; the open redirect is exactly that belief.
  • Q7 C. Thinks the guard asks the API. It runs before any request, from the store.
  • Q9 A, B or C. Expects the tools to catch timing problems. Only the behavior tells you; type mra in the crew search on the projector.
  • Q10 D. The lesson's Exercise 3 is the counterexample; the lab's guard-only-in-parent run is the same in a test.

A1
An effect fetches one cleanup with getEvent(eventId, controller.signal), lists [eventId] as its dependencies and returns () => controller.abort(). What does that cleanup function protect against? (1 point)
A) Nothing in the browser: it only matters in tests
B) A slow answer for the previous eventId arriving late and overwriting the page after the id changed (and a request left running after the component is gone)
C) The API answering with a 500
D) Strict Mode running the effect twice in production
Why: Each new id runs the cleanup first: the old request is aborted, and the .catch returns early when controller.signal.aborted, so only the latest answer reaches the state (Lesson 4.1: race conditions).
A is wrong: Race conditions happen in the browser whenever answers arrive out of order.
C is wrong: A 500 is an answer: fetch resolves with ok false, the request function turns it into an ApiError.
D is wrong: Strict Mode's double run happens only in development.
A2
tsc prints this for the component below. Which change fixes it and keeps the effect correct? (1 point)
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react'
import { getSites } from './api/client.ts'
import type { Site } from './api/types.ts'

export function SiteCount() {
  const [sites, setSites] = useState<Site[]>([])
  useEffect(async () => {
    setSites(await getSites())
  }, [])
  return <p>{sites.length} beach sites</p>
}
src/Review.tsx:7:13 - error TS2345: Argument of type '() => Promise<void>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EffectCallback'.
Found 1 error in src/Review.tsx:7
A) Put // @ts-expect-error above useEffect
B) Return the promise instead: useEffect(() => getSites().then(setSites), [])
C) Change the dependency array to [sites]
D) Keep the effect a plain function: call getSites(controller.signal).then(...) (or an async function declared inside it) and return a cleanup that aborts
Why: An effect may return nothing or a cleanup function (void | Destructor); an async function always returns a promise. The asynchronous work goes inside the effect, which stays synchronous and returns () => controller.abort().
A is wrong: Silences the message and keeps the bug: React would treat the promise as a cleanup.
B is wrong: That still returns a promise: the same error.
C is wrong: The dependencies are not the problem; [sites] would also fetch again after every answer.
A3
The HQ's useApi(key, load) lists key in its effect's dependencies but reads load through useEffectEvent. Why? (1 point)
A) A new key means new data, so the effect must run again; the caller's load is usually an inline arrow, a new function on every render, which would restart the effect (and the request) on every render
B) useEffectEvent makes the request faster
C) Hooks may not take functions as arguments
D) TypeScript cannot type load any other way
Why: Key = what to load, load = how. Listing the arrow as a dependency made the first version send hundreds of requests a second with tsc and the linter silent (Lesson 4.2). useEffectEvent always calls the latest load without being a dependency.
B is wrong: It changes when the effect runs, not how fast the request is.
C is wrong: Hooks take any arguments; useApi itself takes a function.
D is wrong: load is typed (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T> either way.
A4
tsc prints this for a search box whose button moves the focus into the input. Which fix is right? (1 point)
import { useRef } from 'react'

export function SearchBox() {
  const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null)
  function focusSearch() {
    inputRef.current.focus()
  }
  return (
    <>
      <input ref={inputRef} aria-label="Search cleanups" />
      <button type="button" onClick={focusSearch}>Search</button>
    </>
  )
}
src/Review.tsx:6:5 - error TS18047: 'inputRef.current' is possibly 'null'.
Found 1 error in src/Review.tsx:6
A) useRef<HTMLInputElement>(), without the null
B) Keep the input element in useState instead of a ref
C) inputRef.current?.focus(), or check inputRef.current !== null first
D) (inputRef.current as HTMLInputElement).focus()
Why: A DOM ref is null until React attaches the element and again after it is removed, so its type is HTMLInputElement | null: check it, then use it (Lesson 4.3).
A is wrong: React 19's useRef needs an argument (TS2554), and null is the honest start value.
B is wrong: An element is not something the screen is drawn from: state would add renders for nothing.
D is wrong: An assertion hides the case where there is no element; the button would crash instead.
A5
A component reads its data with use(promise) inside a <Suspense> boundary. Where must that promise come from? (1 point)
A) From a call such as getEvent(id) in the component's body, so every render has fresh data
B) From a try block around use, so errors are caught
C) From an effect's cleanup function
D) From something stable that hands back the SAME promise for the same id: a cache (Lesson 4.4's eventPromises) or a route loader (Lesson 6.3)
Why: A promise created while rendering is a new pending promise on every render: the component suspends forever while requests keep going out. use also may not sit in a try/catch: errors go to an error boundary.
A is wrong: Each render would suspend on a new promise: the fallback forever.
B is wrong: use cannot be called in a try/catch (the linter reports an error); a boundary catches the rejection.
C is wrong: A cleanup runs when the effect is undone; it has nothing to do with use.
A6
An ErrorBoundary wraps <EventDetails>. Which error does it NOT catch? (1 point)
A) An error thrown in the onClick handler of a button inside EventDetails
B) An error thrown while EventDetails renders
C) A rejected promise that EventDetails reads with use()
D) An error thrown while a child of EventDetails renders
Why: Boundaries catch errors thrown during rendering (a rejected use() throws while rendering). Event handlers and async code run after rendering: catch those in the handler and show the message with state.
B is wrong: Rendering errors are exactly what a boundary catches.
C is wrong: use throws the rejection while the component renders: the boundary catches it.
D is wrong: The nearest boundary above catches errors from the whole subtree.
A7
A reducer's switch ends in default: return state. A teammate adds { type: 'startOver' } to the action union but writes no case for it. What happens? (1 point)
A) tsc reports the switch that misses the new action
B) It compiles, and dispatching 'startOver' silently does nothing
C) React throws at runtime: unknown action
D) The linter warns about the missing case
Why: default: return state accepts every action, so a forgotten one is swallowed without a sound. default: return assertNever(action) turns the same mistake into a tsc error that names the missing member (Lesson 5.1).
A is wrong: Only with assertNever (or a return type and no default).
C is wrong: The default branch handles it: nothing is thrown.
D is wrong: No oxlint rule checks a reducer's cases.
A8
The HQ writes createContext<AuthValue | undefined>(undefined) and reads it only through useAuth(), which throws when the value is undefined. What does that design give you? (1 point)
A) Faster renders for every component that reads the session
B) The session saved in sessionStorage
C) A component used outside <AuthProvider> fails loudly at once, and every caller gets an AuthValue, never undefined
D) Nothing: createContext requires undefined as its default
Why: A made-up default value compiles and silently does nothing when the provider is missing (no request, no error: measured in Lesson 5.2). undefined plus a Hook that narrows by throwing makes the mistake visible and the type clean.
A is wrong: The design is about correctness, not speed.
B is wrong: Storage is AuthProvider's job, not the context's type.
D is wrong: createContext takes any default value; choosing undefined is a decision.
A9
The Profiler counted hundreds of component renders while someone typed a search word, but the Elements panel showed only the search box and one button changing. How can both be true? (1 point)
A) The Profiler counts each render twice in production
B) Rendering means calling the components and comparing the result; React changes the page only where the result differs
C) The browser caches the DOM between key presses
D) Strict Mode hides DOM changes
Why: Render and commit are different steps: most renders produce the same result, so the commit changes almost nothing. Measure renders before optimizing; the React Compiler (on in the HQ) cut the count from 560 to 14 (Lesson 5.3).
A is wrong: Production builds do not profile at all.
C is wrong: There is no such cache: React decides what to change.
D is wrong: Strict Mode doubles renders in development; it hides nothing.
A10
DataList<T> takes items: T[], getKey: (item: T) => string and renderItem: (item: T) => ReactNode. Why a getKey function rather than the constraint T extends { id: string }? (1 point)
A) A key function renders faster than a property
B) Generic constraints are not allowed in .tsx files
C) React requires keys to come from a function
D) A constraint should ask only for what the component uses: with getKey, items without an id (such as the debris counts) work too, and T is still inferred from items
Why: With T extends { id: string }, DebrisCount failed (TS2322, then T fell back to the constraint). The caller knows what identifies its items; the list only needs a string (Lesson 5.4).
A is wrong: Speed is not the reason; flexibility is.
B is wrong: They are allowed (write <T,> in an arrow function in .tsx).
C is wrong: React only needs a key prop; where it comes from is up to you.
A11
A teammate writes <a href="/sites">Sites</a> in the header of the router app instead of <NavLink to="/sites">. What changes? (1 point)
A) Nothing: the router intercepts every link on the page
B) tsc reports that href is not allowed
C) The click loads the whole page again from the server (in-memory state is lost), and the link never gets aria-current
D) The router throws an error
Why: A plain <a> is a normal page load; Link and NavLink change the address without one, and NavLink marks the current page with aria-current="page" (measured in Lesson 6.1).
A is wrong: The router only handles its own Link and NavLink.
B is wrong: An <a> with href is valid TSX.
D is wrong: Nothing fails: the page just loads again.
A12
tsc prints this for a Hook that reads the status filter from the address. Which fix keeps the types honest? (1 point)
import { useSearchParams } from 'react-router'
import type { StatusFilter } from './components/EventFilters.tsx'

export function useStatusFilter(): StatusFilter {
  const [searchParams] = useSearchParams()
  const status: StatusFilter = searchParams.get('status') ?? 'all'
  return status
}
src/review.ts:6:9 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'StatusFilter'.
Found 1 error in src/review.ts:6
A) readParam(searchParams, 'status', isEventStatus) ?? 'all': a type predicate checks the value
B) searchParams.get('status') as StatusFilter
C) Change the type of status to string
D) // @ts-ignore above the line
Why: The address is input from outside: anyone can type ?status=banana. A predicate (value is EventStatus) checks it; an unknown value falls back to 'all' (Lesson 6.2).
B is wrong: An unchecked promise: ?status=banana would reach the filter.
C is wrong: Every later use loses the union.
D is wrong: Silences the check and keeps the bug.
A13
volunteers/:volunteerId is a child route of volunteers. Clicking a name changes the address to /volunteers/v20, but no card appears, no request goes out and there is no error. What is missing? (1 point)
A) <Outlet /> in VolunteersPage, where the child route renders
B) A key on the volunteers list
C) An errorElement on the child route
D) A loader on the parent route
Why: A child route renders at its parent's <Outlet />. Without one the router matches the child but has nowhere to put it: nothing renders, so nothing fetches (measured in Lesson 6.2, with tsc and the linter silent).
B is wrong: Keys matter for lists; the card is not in the list.
C is wrong: There is no error to show.
D is wrong: Nothing needs to load before the parent renders.
A14
Theo, a signed-in volunteer, opens /volunteers, which sits under the organizersOnly guard loader. What happens? (1 point)
A) He is redirected to /sign-in?next=%2Fvolunteers
B) The guard throws a 403 answer (data(..., { status: 403 })), and the ErrorPage shows "Organizers only" inside the layout
C) The page renders with an empty list
D) He is signed out
Why: 401 means "who are you?" (guests go to sign-in with ?next=); 403 means "I know you, and no". Signing in again would not help Theo, so the guard answers 403 and the page never renders.
A is wrong: That is what a guest gets: Theo is already signed in.
C is wrong: The guard runs before the page renders: the page never starts.
D is wrong: Nothing about his session is wrong.
A15
A page keeps const [loading, setLoading] = useState(true), const [error, setError] = useState<string | null>(null) and const [sites, setSites] = useState<Site[] | null>(null). Name one problem with three separate pieces of state, and write the type Lesson 4.1 uses instead. (2 points)
Three booleans/nullables allow impossible combinations (loading and an error at once, an old error left over next to new data), and every update must remember to set all three. Lesson 4.1: type LoadState<T> = { kind: 'loading' } | { kind: 'failed'; message: string } | { kind: 'ready'; data: T }, one useState<LoadState<Site[]>>({ kind: 'loading' })
Marking (2 points): 1 point: a real problem (impossible or contradictory combinations, or three setters to keep in step). 1 point: a union with a discriminant (kind or similar) whose ready member alone carries the data and whose failed member carries the message. A union without data in it, or any, scores 0 for the type.
A16
A timer's reducer ends its switch with default: return assertNever(action), and tsc prints the error below. What does it tell you, and what is the fix? (2 points)
import { assertNever } from './utils/assertNever.ts'

interface TimerState {
  running: boolean
  seconds: number
}

type TimerAction =
  | { type: 'start' }
  | { type: 'tick' }
  | { type: 'pause' }
  | { type: 'reset' }

export function timerReducer(state: TimerState, action: TimerAction): TimerState {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'start':
      return { ...state, running: true }
    case 'tick':
      return state.running ? { ...state, seconds: state.seconds + 1 } : state
    case 'pause':
      return { ...state, running: false }
    default:
      return assertNever(action)
  }
}
src/review.ts:23:26 - error TS2345: Argument of type '{ type: "reset"; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'never'.
Found 1 error in src/review.ts:23
No case handles the 'reset' action, so in the default branch action is still { type: 'reset' } instead of never. Fix: add case 'reset': return { running: false, seconds: 0 } (a new state object), and the error disappears.
Marking (2 points): 1 point: reads the message (the 'reset' action has no case; whatever reaches default must be never). 1 point: a case 'reset' that returns a new state. Removing the action from the union is accepted only with a reason; deleting assertNever or adding as never scores 0 for the fix.
A17
A teammate says: "/volunteers is protected: guests and volunteers never see its link in the header." Explain in two or three sentences why that is not enough, and what the HQ does instead (both sides). (2 points)
Anyone can type the address, follow an old link or call the API directly: hiding a link is courtesy, not protection. The HQ puts the route under the organizersOnly guard loader, which runs before the page renders (guests are redirected to /sign-in?next=..., volunteers get a 403 answer), AND shoreline-api checks the token on every request (401 / 403): the API is the lock.
Marking (2 points): 1 point: the address or API can be reached without the link (hiding is not security). 1 point: names both protections, the guard loader in the router (redirect for guests and/or 403 for other roles) and the API's own check. Only one of the two: 1 point total.
