Task 2, src/test/render.tsx and src/VolunteerCard.test.tsx:

// Render a component inside the providers the app puts around it (here: the crew's name)
import { render } from '@testing-library/react'
import type { ReactElement } from 'react'
import { CrewProvider } from '../CrewContext.tsx'

export interface RenderOptions {
  /** The crew whose name the card thanks you in (default: Shoreline Crew) */
  crewName?: string
}

export function renderWithCrew(ui: ReactElement, { crewName = 'Shoreline Crew' }: RenderOptions = {}) {
  return render(<CrewProvider crewName={crewName}>{ui}</CrewProvider>)
}
import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react'
import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest'
import { makeShift } from './test/factories.ts'
import { renderWithCrew } from './test/render.tsx'
import { VolunteerCard } from './VolunteerCard.tsx'

const theo = { volunteerId: 'v04', name: 'Theo Nakamura' }

describe('VolunteerCard', () => {
  it('is an article named after the volunteer', () => {
    renderWithCrew(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={[]} />)
    expect(screen.getByRole('article', { name: 'Theo Nakamura' })).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.getByRole('heading', { level: 3, name: 'Theo Nakamura' })).toBeInTheDocument()
  })

  it('thanks the volunteer for the hours worked, in the crew name', () => {
    renderWithCrew(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={[makeShift({ hours: 12 })]} />, {
      crewName: 'Pelican Point Friends',
    })
    expect(screen.getByText('12 hours worked with Pelican Point Friends')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.getByText('Bronze helper')).toBeInTheDocument()
  })

  it('shows no badge and no missed shifts for a new volunteer', () => {
    renderWithCrew(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={[makeShift({ status: 'planned' })]} />)
    expect(screen.getByText('0 hours worked with Shoreline Crew')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.queryByText(/helper$/)).not.toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.queryByRole('list', { name: 'Missed shifts' })).not.toBeInTheDocument()
  })

  it('lists each missed shift with its date and beach', () => {
    const shifts = [makeShift(), makeShift({ id: 'sh2', status: 'missed', date: '2026-07-11', site: 'Driftwood Cove' })]
    renderWithCrew(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={shifts} />)
    const missed = screen.getByRole('list', { name: 'Missed shifts' })
    expect(missed).toHaveTextContent('2026-07-11 at Driftwood Cove')
    expect(screen.getAllByRole('listitem')).toHaveLength(1)
  })

  it('links to the profile with a name a screen reader can read', () => {
    renderWithCrew(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={[]} />)
    expect(screen.getByRole('link', { name: 'Profile of Theo Nakamura' })).toHaveAttribute('href', '/volunteers/v04')
  })

  it('says so when it is rendered without its provider', () => {
    expect(() => render(<VolunteerCard {...theo} shifts={[]} />)).toThrow('useCrewName must be used inside <CrewProvider>')
  })
})

The whole suite, then the type check prints nothing after its two > lines:

 Test Files  2 passed (2)
      Tests  18 passed (18)

Accept other test names and other sample values, as long as each rule is pinned down: the 10-hour boundary, planned AND missed counting 0, absence checked with queryBy, the link's full accessible name. The throw test needs no helper: rendering without the provider is the point.

Lab 9.1 Task 3: the planted bugs

debug_hours.ts counts planned shifts (!== 'missed' instead of === 'worked') and gives bronze only ABOVE 10 hours. With the solution's tests:

× counts a planned shift as 0 hours
× gives 10 hours the badge bronze

 Test Files  1 failed (1)
      Tests  2 failed | 10 passed (12)

debug_VolunteerCard.tsx always renders the Missed shifts list (>= 0) and puts the space inside the hidden text, so jsdom names the link Profileof Theo Nakamura, the lesson's own finding:

× shows no badge and no missed shifts for a new volunteer
× links to the profile with a name a screen reader can read

 Test Files  1 failed (1)
      Tests  2 failed | 4 passed (6)

A learner whose tests miss a bug has a missing test: the 10-hour boundary (a table without exactly 10), planned shifts (only missed tested), the empty list (absence never checked) or the name (a regex such as /Profile/ instead of the full name).

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 9.1 · Testing Components
  1. B An organizer's navigation has a link named Volunteers: A role and an accessible name are what a person (and a screen reader) meets on the page, so the test survives any rewrite of the header that keeps the same result.
  2. D expect(screen.queryByRole('region')).not.toBeInTheDocument(): queryByRole returns null when nothing matches instead of throwing, so the expect can check that the summary is not in the document.
  3. A The display store kept the compact setting from the earlier test: The display store is module state shared by every test in the file. The compact test changed it, and without a reset in afterEach the next test still sees compact lists.
  4. C tsc -b reports an error: the key's type says the data must be a whole ImpactReport: The key made by eventImpactQuery is typed with ImpactReport, so setQueryData expects a whole report. tsc -b (and npm run build) report the missing properties, even though Vitest alone would run the test.
  5. B Move the space outside the visually hidden text, so every tool computes the same name: The name is part of what people hear, so the test pointed at a fragile spot: a space at the start of hidden text, which some accessibility engines drop. Moving the space outside the hidden text gives the same name everywhere.

Warm-up and Exit Ticket

The warm-up's answers are in the Facilitator Guide (Opening Segment).

  1. Three sizes: unit, component, end-to-end. Only end-to-end sees the real browser, the build and the server together: layout, colors, real files, reloads (Lesson 9.3).
  2. getBy / queryBy / findBy: here now (throws when missing) / not here (null) / soon (waits up to a second).
  3. Passes alone, fails together: shared state from an earlier test: the query cache, the session, the display store, storage, or a component that was not cleaned up. Reset it in afterEach (setup.ts).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q2 A (the app is wrong). Trusts the test over the code by default. Run the month: 'long' break: now two tests fail and the test is right.
  • Q3 A or D. Has not internalized that getBy and getAllBy throw. Have them run it and read the message.
  • Q5 A. Thinks green tests mean correct types. Show Exercise 2's TS2322 on weightKg: '18.5'.
  • Q7 A or C. The viewer goes last on viewer-dependent keys; invalidation matches prefixes.
  • Q10 B. Confuses "data the organizer sees" with "data the browser owns". Ask: who sends it?
  • Q12 A. Index keys: remove row 2 live in the impact form and watch row 3's value jump.

Session 22: Interactions, Async, End-to-End and Accessibility Tests

Answers and reasoning for every Session 22 item: both self-checks, the labs' reference tests, the planted bugs and accessibility bugs they catch, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 22 12 self-check items Confidential

9.2 · Interactions and Async: self-check

Q1
Why does every user.click and user.type need await?
A) It slows the test down to a person's speed
B) It waits for the API to answer the request the click sends
C) Each action returns a Promise that resolves once its events have fired and React has updated the page
D) Without it, user-event falls back to fireEvent
Why: user-event fires the whole chain of browser events (pointer, mouse, focus, click; key down, input, key up for every letter) one after another; await lets React finish each update, so the next line sees the page after the action. A request the click starts still needs findBy (Lesson 9.2, Common Mistakes: "Unable to find" right after a click).
A is wrong: Speed has nothing to do with it: the events fire as fast as the test can.
B is wrong: The click's Promise does not wait for network answers: that is findBy's job.
D is wrong: There is no fallback; without await the next line simply looks too early.
Q2
renderApp('/events/e08/edit', organizer) has just returned. Which line finds the title field?
A) screen.getByLabelText('Title (5 to 80 characters)')
B) await screen.findByLabelText('Title (5 to 80 characters)')
C) screen.queryByLabelText('Title (5 to 80 characters)')
D) screen.getByTestId('title')
Why: Right after render the page shows the first-load placeholder ("Loading Shoreline Crew HQ…") while the loader fetches; findBy keeps looking for up to a second. After the first findBy the page is there and getBy is fine (Lesson 9.2, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: getBy looks once, now, and finds the loading placeholder.
C is wrong: queryBy returns null now and never waits.
D is wrong: There is no test id, and a label is what a person uses.
Q3
The guard line in editEventLoader was commented out. The guest still ends up on the sign-in page, and the guard test prints this. What went wrong that nobody clicking through would see?
AssertionError: expected [ 'GET /api/events/e08', …(1) ] to deeply equal []

- Expected
+ Received

- []
+ [
+   "GET /api/events/e08",
+   "GET /api/sites",
A) The guest saw the edit form for a moment
B) The child loader asked the API for the cleanup and the sites before the guest was sent away
C) The sign-in page loaded twice
D) MSW answered requests it should have refused
Why: The guard route's own loader still redirects, so the screen looks right; but editEventLoader ran at the same time and fetched data a guest may not see. recordRequests() makes the invisible half of the rule testable: expect(requests).toEqual([]) (Lesson 9.2, Step 5).
A is wrong: The redirect still happens before anything renders.
C is wrong: One sign-in page; the extra requests are the problem.
D is wrong: MSW answered exactly what was asked: the asking was the bug.
Q4
A test calls server.use(...) to make POST /api/photos answer 413. What does the NEXT test get?
A) The API's own answer again, because server.resetHandlers() runs after every test
B) 413 again until the file ends
C) An error: two tests cannot change the same address
D) Whatever the slower test set last
Why: server.use puts a handler in front of the API's own for the rest of the test; setup.ts's afterEach calls resetHandlers(). Tests stay independent, in any order (Lesson 9.2, One answer, for one test).
B is wrong: resetHandlers removes it after each test.
C is wrong: Overrides stack and are removed; nothing forbids them.
D is wrong: Tests in a file run one after another, and each starts clean.
Q5
With the retry lines removed from setup.ts, the "page whose data does not load" test fails after about a second with this. The app does show its alert. Why does the test not see it?
TestingLibraryElementError: Unable to find role="alert"
The app's cache was still retrying the 500 (with pauses) when findByRole gave up after its 1-second default; tests set retry: false
Why: A test that fails after a round thousand milliseconds, or only on a slow machine, is often waiting for a built-in delay: retries, debouncing, animations. Turn the delay off for tests or control the clock (Lesson 9.2, Common Mistakes; Exercise 1).
Q6
Lab 9.2's sign-up form must not send anything without a name. Which line in the test proves the "nothing"?
A) expect(api.signups).toEqual([])
B) expect(screen.getByText('Add your name.')).toBeInTheDocument()
C) expect(screen.getByRole('button')).toBeEnabled()
D) expect(requests).toEqual([]) after recordRequests()
Why: Only a log of the requests themselves proves none was SENT. api.signups would stay empty also if a request went out and the API refused it; the message proves only the screen. The planted bug in debug_SignUpForm.tsx sends first and checks later: the screen looks right and the request log catches it.
A is wrong: An empty list of sign-ups does not prove no request was made.
B is wrong: That proves the message, not the network.
C is wrong: An enabled button says nothing about requests.

9.3 · End-to-End and Accessibility Tests: self-check

Q7
Right after fill('pelican'), a test checks expect(await page.getByText('Found 3 cleanups').isVisible()).toBe(true) and fails. Why?
A) Playwright cannot read text in main
B) The search needs the API restarted
C) Strict mode: the text matches several elements
D) isVisible() checks once, during the search's 300 ms pause; a web-first assertion keeps checking
Why: await expect(locator).toBeVisible() retries for up to 5 seconds until it is true; isVisible() answers "right now". The same goes for waitForTimeout: wait for what you expect to see, never for a number of milliseconds (Lesson 9.3, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: Playwright reads any text a person could see.
B is wrong: Nothing is wrong with the API.
C is wrong: Strict mode applies to actions and assertions on locators; this one fails with Received: false.
Q8
Why does the HQ's Playwright config say workers: 1?
A) All tests share one real API whose data each test resets and changes; two at once would get in each other's way
B) Chromium opens one tab at a time
C) One worker is faster
D) Playwright's free version allows one worker
Why: A reset in one worker would wipe data another worker's test is using. With one shared server the HQ keeps tests in a line, resets before each and cleans up after the last (globalTeardown). Big teams give each worker its own server or account instead.
B is wrong: Chromium can run many pages and contexts.
C is wrong: More workers are usually faster; safety is the reason.
D is wrong: Playwright is free and open source in full.
Q9
After a CSS change, every light-theme axe test passes and every dark-theme one fails with color-contrast. Which is true?
A) axe is unreliable in dark mode; remove those tests
B) tsc or oxlint would have caught it too
C) Some text in dark mode really is too faint; only a real browser computing the dark colors could find it
D) A Vitest test in jsdom would have shown it
Why: Colors are computed by a real browser from your CSS and the color scheme; jsdom computes none, and neither type checker nor linter reads colors. Lab 9.3 shows the same split: the starter's dark --muted fails only the dark scan.
A is wrong: The tests found a real problem.
B is wrong: Neither tool reads CSS colors.
D is wrong: jsdom has no layout and computes no colors.
Q10
New rule: a photo bigger than 5 MB is refused with the API's message. Where does its test belong?
A) An end-to-end test uploading a real 6 MB photo
B) An axe scan of the impact report page
C) A component test with an MSW override that answers 413
D) A unit test of the API's core only
Why: Rare error answers are what the component level is for: an override causes the 413 in milliseconds, and the test checks what the person sees. The end-to-end level keeps one good journey with one good photo (Lesson 9.3, What to Test Where).
A is wrong: Slow, and a big file in the repository for an edge case the level below covers.
B is wrong: axe checks accessibility, not upload rules.
D is wrong: That proves the API refuses; not that the HQ shows the message.
Q11
What does the check-in test's page.reload() prove that Lesson 9.2's tests could not?
The mark came back from the REAL server after the page and its cache were thrown away: the API really saved it
Why: Lesson 9.2's mock API lives inside the test run; a reload in a real browser throws away the page, the cache and the session's memory, so whatever shows afterwards came from shoreline-api itself.
Q12
A learner's first e2e run prints this. What do they run?
  ✘  1 e2e/plan.spec.ts:13:1 › an organizer plans a cleanup and finds it in the list (35ms)
...
    Error: browserType.launch: Executable doesn't exist at ~/.cache/ms-playwright/chromium_headless_shell-1254/chrome-headless-shell-linux64/chrome-headless-shell
npx playwright install chromium: the npm package is Playwright's code; the browser is a separate download
Why: About 330 MB, stored once per computer in Playwright's cache folder, not in the project. On Linux, npx playwright install-deps chromium adds missing system libraries; in CI, --with-deps does both (Lesson 10.2).

 Test Files  1 failed (1)

The solution's src/SignUpForm.test.tsx (the first block is given):

import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react'
import userEvent from '@testing-library/user-event'
import { http, HttpResponse } from 'msw'
import { afterAll, afterEach, beforeAll, beforeEach, describe, expect, it } from 'vitest'
import { SignUpForm } from './SignUpForm.tsx'
import { api, API_URL, recordRequests, server, type ApiErrorBody } from './test/server.ts'

// Given (do not change): the mock API starts once, is fresh for every test, and fails on unanswered requests
beforeAll(() => server.listen({ onUnhandledFrame: 'error' }))
beforeEach(() => api.reset())
afterEach(() => {
  server.resetHandlers()
  server.events.removeAllListeners()
})
afterAll(() => server.close())

/** The form for Weekend Cove Cleanup, and a user who types and clicks like a person */
function renderForm() {
  const user = userEvent.setup()
  render(<SignUpForm apiUrl={API_URL} eventId="e08" />)
  return { user }
}

describe('SignUpForm', () => {
  it('shows the spots left once the API answers', async () => {
    renderForm()
    expect(screen.getByText('Checking the spots…')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(await screen.findByText('6 spots left')).toBeInTheDocument()
  })

  it('signs a volunteer up and counts the spot', async () => {
    const { user } = renderForm()
    await user.type(screen.getByLabelText('Your name'), 'Diego Herrera')
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign up' }))

    expect(await screen.findByText('You are on the list, Diego Herrera.')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.getByText('5 spots left')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(api.signups).toEqual(['Diego Herrera'])
  })

  it('works with the keyboard alone', async () => {
    const { user } = renderForm()
    await user.tab()
    expect(screen.getByLabelText('Your name')).toHaveFocus()
    await user.keyboard('Ana Lima{Enter}')
    expect(await screen.findByText('You are on the list, Ana Lima.')).toBeInTheDocument()
  })
})

describe('SignUpForm when something is wrong', () => {
  it('asks for a name and sends nothing', async () => {
    const { user } = renderForm()
    await screen.findByText('6 spots left')
    const requests = recordRequests()

    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign up' }))

    expect(screen.getByText('Add your name.')).toBeInTheDocument()
    expect(screen.getByLabelText('Your name')).toHaveAttribute('aria-invalid', 'true')
    expect(requests).toEqual([])
  })

  it('shows the API message when the cleanup is full', async () => {
    api.fill()
    const { user } = renderForm()
    await user.type(screen.getByLabelText('Your name'), 'Diego Herrera')
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign up' }))

    expect(await screen.findByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent('This cleanup is full.')
    expect(api.signups).toEqual([])
  })

  it('says what went wrong when the server fails', async () => {
    server.use(
      http.post(`${API_URL}/api/events/:eventId/signups`, () =>
        HttpResponse.json({ error: 'server_error', message: 'The crew database is asleep.' } satisfies ApiErrorBody, {
          status: 500,
        }),
      ),
    )
    const { user } = renderForm()
    await user.type(screen.getByLabelText('Your name'), 'Diego Herrera')
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign up' }))

    expect(await screen.findByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent('The crew database is asleep.')
    expect(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign up' })).toBeEnabled()
  })
})

npm test (and npm run typecheck prints nothing after its two > lines):

 Test Files  1 passed (1)
      Tests  6 passed (6)

Accept other names and values. What must be there: userEvent.setup() before render and await on every action; findBy for the spots and for anything after a click that needs the API; recordRequests() started after the page loaded; the full cleanup through api.fill() (the real 409), not an override; the 500 override typed with satisfies ApiErrorBody.

import { expect, test } from '@playwright/test'

test('a visitor finds a beach by searching', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/')
  await expect(page.getByRole('heading', { level: 1, name: 'Beach sites' })).toBeVisible()
  await expect(page.getByRole('status')).toHaveText('Found 6 sites.')

  await page.getByLabel('Search sites').fill('cove')
  // A web-first assertion keeps checking through the search's 300 ms pause
  await expect(page.getByRole('status')).toHaveText('Found 2 sites.')
  await expect(page.getByRole('listitem')).toHaveText([/^Driftwood Cove/, /^Seal Rock Cove/])
  await expect(page).toHaveURL(/\?q=cove$/)
})

test('the search survives a reload', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/?q=harbor')
  await expect(page.getByLabel('Search sites')).toHaveValue('harbor')
  await expect(page.getByRole('status')).toHaveText('Found 2 sites.')
  await page.reload()
  await expect(page.getByRole('listitem')).toHaveCount(2)
})

test('Clear the search shows every site and puts the focus back', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/?q=jetty')
  const clear = page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Clear the search' })
  await expect(clear).toBeVisible()
  await clear.click()
  await expect(page.getByRole('status')).toHaveText('Found 6 sites.')
  await expect(page.getByLabel('Search sites')).toBeFocused()
})
import AxeBuilder from '@axe-core/playwright'
import { expect, type Page } from '@playwright/test'

/** Run axe (WCAG 2.0 to 2.2, A and AA) on the page as it is now; fail with one readable line per problem */
export async function expectNoAxeViolations(page: Page): Promise<void> {
  const results = await new AxeBuilder({ page })
    .withTags(['wcag2a', 'wcag2aa', 'wcag21a', 'wcag21aa', 'wcag22aa'])
    .analyze()
  const problems = results.violations.map(
    (violation) => `${violation.id}: ${violation.help} (${violation.nodes.length})`,
  )
  expect(problems).toEqual([])
}
import { expect, test } from '@playwright/test'
import { expectNoAxeViolations } from './helpers.ts'

for (const colorScheme of ['light', 'dark'] as const) {
  test.describe(`${colorScheme} theme`, () => {
    test.use({ colorScheme })

    test('the beach sites page has no axe violations', async ({ page }) => {
      await page.goto('/?q=cove')
      // Scan the page with its results, not before the script has run
      await expect(page.getByRole('status')).toHaveText('Found 2 sites.')
      await expectNoAxeViolations(page)
    })
  })
}

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 9.2 · Interactions and Async
  1. B Each action returns a Promise that resolves once its events have fired and React has updated the page: Each action fires a chain of events and lets React finish its updates in between. Its Promise resolves when that is done, so the next line checks the page after the click, not before it.
  2. D await screen.findByLabelText('Title (5 to 80 characters)'): The page appears only after the loader has fetched the cleanup and the sites. findByLabelText keeps looking (up to a second by default) and resolves when the field is there.
  3. A The API's own answer again, because server.resetHandlers() runs after every test: setup.ts calls server.resetHandlers() after every test, which removes the overrides and leaves only the API's own handlers, so the next test talks to the real rules again.
  4. C A child loader could ask the API for data before the guard sends the guest away, while the page still looks right: With the guard call removed from editEventLoader, the guest is still redirected, so the page looks right, but the loader has already asked the API for the cleanup and the sites. Only the request list shows it.
  5. D The app's cache is still retrying with pauses when findByRole gives up after one second: The app's query client retries a failed request twice with a pause before each try, so the error message is still on its way when findByRole gives up after its default one second.
Lesson 9.3 · End-to-End and Accessibility Tests
  1. C isVisible() checks once, while the search is still waiting; a web-first assertion would keep checking: isVisible checks once, at that instant, and the search is still in its 300 ms pause. await expect(locator).toBeVisible() keeps checking until the text appears or the timeout ends.
  2. A All tests share one real API whose data each test resets and changes, so two at once would get in each other's way: All tests share one shoreline-api, and each test resets its data first. Two tests at once would reset or change the data the other one is using, so they run one after another.
  3. D Some text in dark mode really is too faint, and only a real browser rendering the dark theme could find it: Only a real browser computes the colors each theme produces. In dark mode the text no longer has enough contrast with its background, which is a real problem for people using dark mode, and only the dark scan can see it.
  4. B A component test with an MSW override that answers 413: It is an error case on one screen: a component test with an MSW override answering 413 (as in Lesson 9.2) checks the message quickly and reliably. End-to-end tests are kept for the main journeys.
  5. A The mark came back from the real server after the page and its cache were thrown away, so the API really saved it: A reload empties the page and the query cache, so the pressed button that comes back was loaded from shoreline-api. That shows the real server saved the mark, which no in-memory test can show.

Warm-up and Exit Ticket

The warm-up's answers are in the Facilitator Guide.

  1. The API's own code: the tests follow the real rules, status codes and messages, and cannot drift from the API; hand-written answers can.
  2. Only in a real browser: a real photo upload, CSS colors (dark-mode contrast), layout and overflow, a reload against the real server, the production build itself (any one).
  3. Where it goes: many cases → unit; one screen, including errors and what must not happen → component with MSW; only works when everything is real together → one end-to-end test for the main path.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B. Thinks the click waits for the network. Show the missing-await message, then where findBy comes in.
  • Q2 A. Has not seen the first-load placeholder. Print screen.debug() right after renderApp.
  • Q3 A. Expects a visible symptom. The point of the request log is the invisible half of a rule.
  • Q7 C. Mixes up strict mode and timing. Strict mode errors say "resolved to N elements".
  • Q9 A or D. Blames the tool or overestimates jsdom. Run Lab 9.3's dark-only failure.
  • Q10 A. Wants every rule end-to-end. Ask what the test costs and what it can see.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 23: Performance and the Build

Answers and reasoning for every Session 23 item: the self-check, the lesson's mistakes as measured, the lab's reference solution and budget runs, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 23 12 self-check items Confidential

10.1 · Performance and the Build: self-check

Q1
npm run build prints "Some chunks are larger than 500 kB after minification". Best first step?
A) Raise build.chunkSizeWarningLimit until it goes away
B) Treat it as a failed build and roll back
C) Look inside the bundle, then split out what most visitors do not need
D) Replace React with a smaller library
Why: It is a warning, not an error: one big file usually means everyone downloads code they do not need. Measure (who downloads what), look (the treemap), then split (Lesson 10.1). Raising the limit changes nothing a visitor downloads.
A is wrong: That silences the message and changes no byte.
B is wrong: The build worked; the warning is advice.
D is wrong: React DOM is the price of using React; the treemap shows bigger wins.
Q2
Before Lesson 10.1, the HQ's build ends like this. What does every visitor download, guest or organizer?
dist/assets/index-B-Vm3u1y.js                            581.69 kB │ gzip: 182.31 kB
A) All 581.69 kB: React, the router, the query library, the form libraries and every page
B) About 182 kB to parse and run
C) Only the code for the pages they open
D) Nothing until they click
Why: One JavaScript file holds everything. The network carries the gzip size (182.31 kB), but the phone still parses and runs all 581.69 kB. The lesson's Network table: 1 file, 581.69 kB, for every kind of visit.
B is wrong: Gzip is what travels; the browser unpacks and runs the full size.
C is wrong: Without dynamic imports there is nothing to load later.
D is wrong: The single file is the first thing index.html loads.
Q3
In a data router, a route's lazy downloads the page's file when?
A) When the app starts
B) After the loader's data has arrived
C) At the same time as the route's loaders, after the click
D) Only after the page has rendered
Why: React Router starts the loaders and the file download together and renders when both are done (measured: the Impact page's file was requested before /api/impact answered). React.lazy inside a page starts only when React renders it: a waterfall.
A is wrong: That would put it back in the first visit.
B is wrong: That is React.lazy's waterfall, not the route's lazy.
D is wrong: The page needs its code to render at all.
Q4
A guest opens a shared link to /events/e08/edit. With the HQ's lazy routes, what happens?
A) The guest sees the edit form: lazy pages skip the guard
B) Nothing is downloaded: the guard runs before any file is requested
C) The build refuses lazy routes behind guards
D) The edit page's file is downloaded while the guard decides, then the guest is sent to sign in
Why: The file loads in parallel with the loaders, so guests following organizer links download that page (measured: 557.32 kB in 7 files). Fine: nothing in front-end code is secret. Splitting is for speed, not for secrets; the guards decide what a person sees, the API what anyone may do.
A is wrong: The guard's loader still redirects.
B is wrong: Loaders and the lazy file start together.
C is wrong: There is no such rule.
Q5
This line gives a type error. Why?
const ImpactChart = lazy(() => import('../components/ImpactChart.tsx'))
A) Charts cannot be loaded lazily
B) React.lazy needs a module with a default export, and ImpactChart is a named export
C) ImpactChart.tsx has its own type error
D) lazy() must be called inside the component
Why: Read the bottom line of TS2322: "Property 'default' is missing". Return { default: ImpactChart } from your own async function (Lab 10.1 Task 2 does the same for TideTables). And call lazy at the top level, never inside a component.
A is wrong: Any component can be lazy.
C is wrong: The error is about the import's shape.
D is wrong: Inside a component, a new lazy component on every render would reload and reset.
Q6
Why do React, React DOM, React Router and TanStack Query get their own vendor group?
A) It makes a guest's first download smaller
B) Vite requires a vendor group
C) It loads those libraries only on the pages that use them
D) Its file keeps its name when only the HQ's own code changes, so returning visitors reuse it
Why: The bytes are the same; the caching is not. Measured: after a tagline change only index got a new name; vendor (355.82 kB) kept vendor-DenlHpDj.js. Lab 10.1's Task 3 shows the same with React alone.
A is wrong: Every page needs those libraries; a group does not remove bytes.
B is wrong: Groups are optional.
C is wrong: Groups decide which file, not when it loads.
Q7
A teammate replaced the two groups with { name: 'vendor', test: /node_modules[\\/]/ },. The build prints this. What happened?
dist/assets/vendor-DJqBY5YG.css                            0.69 kB │ gzip:   0.35 kB
dist/assets/index-CQFHV4Rj.css                             8.33 kB │ gzip:   2.42 kB
dist/assets/index-CDLAbaOo.js                             62.72 kB │ gzip:  21.37 kB
dist/assets/vendor-CUGVkuZG.js                           483.56 kB │ gzip: 149.12 kB
A) Nothing: the forms file was just renamed
B) Zod and React Hook Form moved into the vendor file every page loads, so guests download them again
C) The organizer pages are no longer lazy
D) The build is smaller, so it is better
Why: A group that catches a module the main file needs makes the main file load the whole group. There is no forms file any more, so a test that only looked for that name would pass; the budget test counts bytes and fails (Lesson 10.1, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: The bytes moved into a file every visit loads.
C is wrong: The pages are still lazy; their libraries are not.
D is wrong: Fewer files, but every guest downloads 128 kB more.
Q8
An old tutorial's manualChunks: { vendor: [...] } in vite.config.ts. What does Vite 8 do?
A) Works as in Vite 7
B) Ignores it silently
C) tsc reports TS2769 (manualChunks must be a function) and the build stops: use codeSplitting.groups
D) Splits every package into its own file
Why: Vite 8 bundles with Rolldown; rollupOptions is a deprecated alias, and Rolldown accepts only the function form of manualChunks (itself deprecated). TypeScript reads the config and says so before the build fails with "manualChunks is not a function".
A is wrong: Vite 7 bundled with Rollup; Vite 8 does not.
B is wrong: It fails loudly, twice.
D is wrong: That is not what it does in any version.
Q9
The e2e budget test checks two things about a guest's visit. Name both, and why a name check alone was not enough.
No /assets/forms- file, AND the total JavaScript under 440 kB; the catch-all vendor group had no forms file yet sent the form libraries to every guest
Why: A budget fails on any cause, including ones you did not think of: one static import of an organizer page, a new library, a group that catches too much (Lesson 10.1, Step 6).
Q10
Why does toCsv put a ' in front of a cell such as =HYPERLINK(...)?
A) Spreadsheets need it for every text cell
B) A cell starting with = + - @ can run as a formula when the file is opened; the quote makes it plain text
C) To mark the cell as a number
D) RFC 4180 requires it
Why: CSV injection: a name or note typed into the app could become a formula on the council's computer. RFC 4180 is about quoting commas, quotes and line breaks; the formula guard is an extra safety rule (Lesson 10.1, Step 4; Lab 10.1 Task 1).
A is wrong: Only cells that start with a formula character.
C is wrong: Numbers are written as they are.
D is wrong: RFC 4180 does not mention formulas.
Q11
In Lab 10.1 the starter's toCsv takes rows and columns typed with any, and the handed-out test file has a @ts-expect-error line. npm run typecheck prints this. What does it mean?
src/csv.test.ts:37:5 - error TS2578: Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive.
The directive expected a type error that never came: the loose toCsv accepts a column that reads a property the rows do not have; a generic toCsv<Row> makes that line an error, and the directive is then used
Why: Testing a type, not a value: @ts-expect-error turns "this must NOT compile" into a check. Vitest runs the code without checking types, so only tsc can run this test.
Q12
Lesson 10.1's Exercise 1 lazy-loaded the 1.31 kB impact chart with React.lazy and measured it. The verdict?
A) Not worth it: it already arrives with the lazy Impact page, and splitting adds a request after the data
B) Worth it: every kilobyte counts
C) It broke the tests
D) It made the vendor file smaller
Why: Measure, then decide. A second request that starts only after /api/impact answered, plus a "Loading the chart…" moment, to save about a kilobyte. With a 100 kB charting library the same few lines would be a clear win (Lab 10.1's tide tables are that case).
B is wrong: Requests cost too; a kilobyte is not worth a waterfall.
C is wrong: The tests passed.
D is wrong: The chart is the HQ's own code, not a vendor library.

Task 1, src/csv.ts:

// Rows of data as a CSV file that spreadsheets open (RFC 4180: CRLF line endings, "…" with doubled quotes)

/** One column of the file: its heading, and how to read its value from a row */
export interface CsvColumn<Row> {
  heading: string
  value: (row: Row) => string | number
}

// A text cell starting with = + - @ could run as a formula when a spreadsheet opens the file ("CSV injection"):
// a ' in front makes the spreadsheet show it as text.
function cell(value: string | number): string {
  if (typeof value === 'number') return String(value)
  const text = /^[=+\-@\t\r]/.test(value) ? `'${value}` : value
  return /[",\r\n]/.test(text) ? `"${text.replaceAll('"', '""')}"` : text
}

/** A heading line, then one line per row, each ending with CRLF */
export function toCsv<Row>(rows: readonly Row[], columns: readonly CsvColumn<Row>[]): string {
  const lines = [
    columns.map((column) => cell(column.heading)),
    ...rows.map((row) => columns.map((column) => cell(column.value(row)))),
  ]
  return lines.map((line) => line.join(',')).join('\r\n') + '\r\n'
}

Task 2, src/App.tsx:

import { lazy, Suspense, useState } from 'react'

// The tables' code (and their data) arrive in a file of their own, the first time someone opens them.
// React.lazy wants a module with a default export, so name the one TideTables.tsx exports.
const TideTables = lazy(async () => {
  const { TideTables } = await import('./TideTables.tsx')
  return { default: TideTables }
})

/** Tide Watch: most visitors only read the next cleanup's tide; few open the full tables */
export function App() {
  const [showTables, setShowTables] = useState(false)
  return (
    <main>
      <h1>Tide Watch</h1>
      <p>Next cleanup: Saturday at Driftwood Cove. Low tide at 09:40, the best time to reach the rocks.</p>
      <button type="button" aria-expanded={showTables} onClick={() => setShowTables((shown) => !shown)}>
        {showTables ? 'Hide the tide tables' : 'Show the tide tables'}
      </button>
      {showTables && (
        <Suspense fallback={<p role="status">Loading the tide tables…</p>}>
          <TideTables />
        </Suspense>
      )}
    </main>
  )
}

Task 3, the group in vite.config.ts:

/// <reference types="vitest/config" />

// Vitest reads its settings from here, like the course app (Lesson 9.1)
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react()],
  test: { environment: 'jsdom', setupFiles: ['./src/setupTests.ts'] },
  build: {
    rolldownOptions: {
      output: {
        codeSplitting: {
          groups: [
            // React on every visit, in a file whose name stays the same when only the app's code changes
            { name: 'vendor', test: /node_modules[\\/](react|react-dom|scheduler)[\\/]/ },
          ],
        },
      },
    },
  },
})

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 10.1 · Performance and the Build
  1. D Look inside the bundle, then split out what most visitors do not need: Measure first: the treemap shows what is inside the file and who needs each part. Then split what most visitors never use, as the HQ did with the organizer forms.
  2. A The edit page's file is downloaded while the guard decides, then the guest is sent to sign in, which is fine because code is not secret: The router downloads a lazy route's file while its loaders run, so the edit page's file arrives while organizersOnly decides; then the guest sees Sign in. That is fine: front-end code is never secret, and the API protects the data.
  3. C React.lazy needs a module with a default export, and ImpactChart is a named export: React.lazy wants a promise of a module whose default export is a component. ImpactChart.tsx only has a named export, so the function must return an object with default set to ImpactChart.
  4. B Its file keeps its name when only the HQ's own code changes, so returning visitors reuse it from the cache: The vendor file only changes when the libraries change, so its hashed name stays the same when you fix the HQ's own code, and returning visitors reuse it from their cache. Step 5 measured it.
  5. A It fails on the budget: Zod and React Hook Form are now in the vendor file every page loads: Zod and React Hook Form move into vendor, which the main file needs for React, so every guest downloads them. No forms file exists, but the byte budget catches the extra kilobytes.

Warm-up and Exit Ticket

The warm-up's answers are in the Facilitator Guide.

  1. Before splitting: measure what each visitor downloads (Network tab on the preview), look inside (treemap), then split what most visitors do not need; measure again.
  2. Guest and edit page: React Router downloads the lazy file with the loaders, before the guard redirects. Fine: front-end code is public; the guard decides what a person sees, the API what anyone may do.
  3. Budget vs warning: the warning fires only above 500 kB per chunk; the budget catches any change that sends a guest more JavaScript (a static import, a new library, a catch-all group), and names the forms file if it appears.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Treats warnings as noise. Ask what the change did for a visitor.
  • Q2 B. Confuses transfer with cost. The phone runs the full size.
  • Q3 B. Mixes up React.lazy and route lazy: the waterfall is React.lazy's.
  • Q4 B or C. Believes splitting hides code. Open any lazy file in the Network tab.
  • Q6 A. Thinks groups reduce bytes. They change names and caching.
  • Q12 A. Splits by reflex. Exercise 1 measured the opposite.

10.2 · Deploying: self-check

Q1
You deploy the NORMAL build of the HQ (VITE_API_URL=http://localhost:4000) and send the link to a friend. What do they see?
A) The HQ with your computer's data, while your API runs
B) Pages that say they could not reach the server: localhost is THEIR computer
C) The demo with sample data: Netlify switches MSW on
D) A blank page: Netlify refuses React apps
Why: Every VITE_ value is written into the JavaScript at build time; in a visitor's browser localhost is the visitor's own machine. Three honest ways out: the demo build, a hosted API, or a site that only works for you (Lesson 10.2, Environments).
A is wrong: Your laptop is not reachable from their browser.
C is wrong: Only the demo build starts MSW, and only because the build said so.
D is wrong: Static hosts serve any folder of files.
Q2
The live home page works, but reloading /events/e08 shows "Not Found". What fixes it?
A) /* /index.html 200 in public/_redirects
B) A Cache-Control rule in _headers
C) Adding events/:eventId to the router twice
D) Deploying the normal build instead
Why: React Router changed the address without asking the host; on reload the host looks for a file /events/e08 and has none. The rewrite sends index.html with 200 for every address that is not a real file (the SPA fallback); real files still win.
B is wrong: Caching does not create missing files.
C is wrong: The router is fine; the host never reaches it.
D is wrong: Both builds need the fallback.
Q3
A teammate starts the demo API with a hostname check instead of the mode check. The normal build then prints this. What is wrong?
dist/assets/browser-urx2_hst.js                          429.68 kB │ gzip: 164.46 kB
A) Nothing: the browser file is never downloaded
B) tsc should have refused it
C) The minifier failed
D) The API's code is in the normal build, and any HQ deployed to Netlify would answer from sample data
Why: The bundler cannot know the hostname at build time, so the branch and its import stay. import.meta.env.MODE is replaced with text, the dead branch removed. A build decision belongs in the build (Lab 10.2, Task 1).
A is wrong: It is in the build, and on any .netlify.app address it runs.
B is wrong: It type-checks: the bug is about builds, not types.
C is wrong: The minifier did its job; the condition was simply not constant.
Q4
Why may files in /assets/ be cached for a year, but not index.html?
A) Netlify ignores caching for HTML
B) index.html is too small to matter
C) Hashed files get a new name when they change; index.html keeps its name and points at the newest files
D) The service worker caches index.html
Why: A new version of an asset is a new URL, so an old copy can never be used by mistake. index.html must be checked on every visit, or returning visitors keep the old page (and the old file names) for a year.
A is wrong: Hosts apply your headers to any file.
B is wrong: Size is not the point; freshness is.
D is wrong: MSW's worker answers /api requests; it does not cache the page.
Q5
npm run lint prints this and exits with 0. Why does the CI workflow call npm run lint -- --deny-warnings?
⚠ eslint(no-unused-vars): Variable 'password' is declared but never used. Unused variables should start with a '_'.
...
Found 1 warning and 0 errors.
A) To make oxlint faster
B) Without it a warning never fails the step; the HQ's rule is zero warnings
C) --deny-warnings fixes the warnings
D) GitHub requires it
Why: oxlint exits 0 with warnings; with --deny-warnings it exits 1 (measured), which is what turns a CI step red. The -- passes the flag through npm to oxlint.
A is wrong: Speed is unchanged.
C is wrong: It only changes the exit code.
D is wrong: GitHub runs whatever you write.
Q6
In the HQ's workflow, why does the e2e job say needs: checks?
A) The first job installs shoreline-api
B) The e2e job deploys when it passes
C) GitHub runs only one job at a time
D) It waits for the quick checks and skips itself when they are red: no browser minutes for a change that does not compile
Why: needs orders jobs and stops the chain on failure. The e2e job then tests the NORMAL build against the real shoreline-api, which is already in the repository and needs no install.
A is wrong: shoreline-api needs no install; playwright.config.ts starts it.
B is wrong: Nothing in CI deploys; deploying is a step you take on purpose.
C is wrong: Jobs run in parallel unless needs says otherwise.
Q7
Why does the course folder (with shoreline-api and shoreline-hq side by side) become ONE repository?
A) The e2e job starts ../shoreline-api, and workflows live in .github/workflows at the TOP
B) GitHub allows one repository per account
C) Netlify deploys whole repositories
D) So node_modules is shared
Why: playwright.config.ts starts the API from ../shoreline-api, and GitHub only reads workflows at the top of a repository. The top-level .gitignore keeps node_modules, .env.local and .netlify out of all three projects.
B is wrong: Accounts can have many repositories.
C is wrong: The CLI uploads one folder (dist); the repository is for code and CI.
D is wrong: node_modules is never committed.
Q8
What does this print in the course folder, and what does each line prove?
git check-ignore -v shoreline-hq/.env.local shoreline-hq/node_modules shoreline-hq/dist
One line per path naming the .gitignore rule that ignores it: .env.local (shoreline-hq/.gitignore line 13, *.local), node_modules (line 10), dist (line 11): none of them will be committed
Why: git check-ignore -v is proof, not hope: run it before the first commit, then read git status (188 files staged in the course's copy, .env.example yes, .env.local no).
Q9
On Netlify's free plan, which costs credits the course tells you to watch?
A) Every page view of the demo, 15 credits each
B) Each production deploy (15 credits), so deploy by hand after green checks
C) Draft deploys only
D) Nothing: free means unlimited
Why: Netlify Free has 300 credits a month as a hard limit; a production deploy costs 15, bandwidth and requests a little. Twenty production deploys use a month, which is why the lesson deploys by hand (draft, look, then --prod) instead of on every push.
A is wrong: 1 GB of bandwidth costs 20; a visit is about a megabyte.
C is wrong: The lesson only says production deploys cost 15; read the current terms.
D is wrong: The limit is hard: the site pauses until next month.
Q10
Why npx netlify-cli deploy --no-build --dir dist and not plain deploy?
A) --no-build makes the upload smaller
B) --no-build skips the draft
C) Without it the CLI runs a build of its own; you have just built the demo you want
D) dist is the only folder Netlify accepts
Why: The CLI builds before deploying unless told not to (its deploy --help says so), and its build would not be your build:demo. --dir dist uploads the folder as it is, with _redirects, _headers and the worker file; without --prod it is a draft at a temporary address.
A is wrong: Size is the same folder.
B is wrong: --prod decides draft or live.
D is wrong: --dir names any folder.
Q11
Name two checks of the live launch checklist that ONLY make sense from outside your computer.
Any two: the phone on mobile data opens the link; deep links reload; curl -I shows the security headers and the immutable cache on /assets; http redirects to https; no console errors on the live site
Why: Your laptop has your cache, your Wi-Fi and maybe your API running; a visitor has none of them. The checklist is the dress rehearsal for a deploy (Lesson 10.2, Step 7).
Q12
The launch-readiness check prints FIX for "shoreline-hq/.env.local is in the repository". What do you do?
A) Delete the repository on GitHub
B) Nothing: VITE_ values are public anyway
C) Add .env.local to .gitignore again
D) git rm --cached shoreline-hq/.env.local, commit, and keep the file on disk
Why: .gitignore only stops UNtracked files; a file already committed stays tracked until you remove it from the index. Anything in it that is a real secret also has to be changed, because the history keeps it (VITE_ values are public by design, which is why they must never hold secrets).
A is wrong: The local repository has the same problem.
B is wrong: The habit matters: next time it may hold a real key.
C is wrong: It is already ignored; ignoring does not untrack.

Task 2, public/_redirects and public/_headers:

# Netlify: every address the host has no file for gets index.html, and the app shows the page
/*    /index.html    200
# Netlify: extra response headers

# Files with a content hash in their name never change: browsers may keep them for a year
/assets/*
  Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable

# Every response: no type guessing, no full addresses to other sites, no framing by other sites
/*
  X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff
  Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin
  X-Frame-Options: DENY

      - run: npx vite build

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 10.2 · Deploying
  1. B Pages that say they could not reach the server, because localhost is their own computer: localhost in a visitor's browser is the visitor's own computer, where no API runs, so every page reports that it could not reach the server. That is why the course deploys a demo build that carries the API's code.
  2. C /* /index.html 200 in public/_redirects, so the host sends the HQ's page for every address it has no file for: The host looks for a file called events/e08 and has none. The rewrite rule in public/_redirects sends index.html with status 200 for every path without a file, and React Router shows the cleanup.
  3. A The API's code is now in the normal build too, and any HQ deployed on Netlify answers from sample data: Vite can only remove branches it can decide while building. The hostname is known only in the browser, so the API's code ships in the normal build too, and a real HQ deployed to Netlify would quietly answer from sample data.
  4. D Hashed files get a new name when they change, while index.html keeps its name and points at the newest files: A hashed file gets a new name whenever its content changes, so an old copy can never be used by mistake. index.html keeps its name and points at the current file names, so browsers must check it on every visit to see a new deploy.
  5. B It skips the slow browser tests when the quick checks fail, and it tests what a production HQ does: the normal build talking to a real API: needs makes the browser tests wait for the quick checks and skip a change that does not even compile. The normal build against the real API is what a production HQ does, and the budget test keeps proving that no demo code reached it.

Exit Ticket

  1. MODE vs hostname: Vite writes the mode into the code as text, so 'production' === 'demo' is removed with its import; the hostname exists only while the app runs, so the bundler keeps the code (measured: a 429.68 kB browser file in the normal build).
  2. The two files: _redirects rewrites every address without a file to index.html (deep links, reloads); _headers caches hashed assets for a year, leaves index.html to be checked on every visit, and adds nosniff, Referrer-Policy and X-Frame-Options.
  3. Red in CI, green locally: a file not committed (or ignored), the lockfile out of sync with package.json (npm ci refuses), something only on the laptop (.env.local, a running API, a global tool), a Node version difference, a workflow path that assumes another folder.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Thinks of localhost as "the server". Ask: whose computer is localhost in your friend's browser?
  • Q2 B or D. Has not separated routing from caching. Reload a deep link on netlify dev without the file.
  • Q3 A. Believes unused code is harmless. Show the build table with the browser file.
  • Q6 B. Expects CI to deploy. Deploying is a step you take on purpose.
  • Q9 D. Reads "free" as unlimited. Do the credit arithmetic together.
  • Q12 C. Believes .gitignore untracks files. Run git ls-files | grep env.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 25: Final Project Workshop and Next Steps

Answers and reasoning for every Session 25 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 25 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Part 3 in Five Questions

  1. Query keys: everything the query function uses is in the key; the key is the answer's address in the cache (Lesson 7.1). Rubric row 5.
  2. Form output: the transform makes an empty Notes box null; the API receives the output, so its request schema accepts null (Lessons 8.1, 11.1). Rubric row 6.
  3. Finding a button: by role and accessible name (Lesson 9.1). Rubric row 8.
  4. _redirects: every address answers index.html with 200, so deep links and reloads work on a static host (Lesson 10.2). Rubric rows 3 and 9.
  5. Demo honesty: a banner that says what the demo is (data in the browser tab, reset on reload), the same mock-API rules as the tests (Lesson 10.2). Rubric row 9.

11.1 · Final Project: self-check

Q1
You open the final-project starter for your brief. What do you write first?
A) The home page, so the client sees progress
B) The domain types in src/api/types.ts, next to the example Item
C) The Playwright journeys
D) The deploy to Netlify
Why: Types first: the mock API, the guards, the queries, the forms and the tests all depend on them, and they are the most expensive thing to change once screens stand on them. Keeping the example Item until Milestone 2 keeps every check green while you add yours (Lesson 11.1, Step 4).
A is wrong: Components before types is the lesson's first Common Mistake: the card wants a property the API never sends.
C is wrong: End-to-end journeys come with Milestone 5, once there are screens to walk through.
D is wrong: Milestone 2 makes a draft deploy; the first job is the description of the data.
Q2
In Lab 11.1 you add 'conflict' to ErrorCode in src/api/types.ts. npx tsc -b answers this. What do you do?
src/api/client.ts:34:7 - error TS2741: Property 'conflict' is missing in type '{ validation_failed: true; not_found: true; server_error: true; }' but required in type 'Record<ErrorCode, true>'.

34 const errorCodes: Record<ErrorCode, true> = {
         ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/api/client.ts:34
A) Change the Record to an array of error codes, so it compiles
B) Remove 'conflict' again: the client cannot handle it
C) Add conflict: true to the errorCodes table in client.ts
D) Add // @ts-expect-error above line 34
Why: The client keeps its error codes in a Record<ErrorCode, true>, so the type checker lists the one place that must learn about the new code. An array would compile and quietly treat every 409 as an unknown error (Lesson 11.1, Types First).
A is wrong: That is exactly the version that compiles and is wrong: the lesson's warning.
B is wrong: The workshop's and the tuner's APIs need a 409; the client must learn the code, not lose it.
D is wrong: Silencing the error keeps the bug: the 409 body would fail the client's check.
Q3
What does this print, and how does the final project store prices because of it?
node -p "1.10 + 2.20"
3.3000000000000003; prices are whole cents (a Cents type), formatted only for display
Why: JavaScript numbers are binary fractions, so most decimal prices are not stored exactly. In cents the same sum is exact (node -p "110 + 220" prints 330). Divide by 100 only when you format, with Intl.NumberFormat.
Q4
A repair ticket has no promised day until a mechanic has looked at the bike. Which property fits?
A) promisedFor: DateString | null
B) promisedFor?: DateString
C) promisedFor: string, empty until known
D) promisedFor: Date | undefined
Why: null says "known to be empty": the API sends the property, with no value yet. An optional property means "not sent", a different thing; an empty string hides the state from the type checker (Lesson 11.1, Types First).
B is wrong: Optional means the property may be missing from the answer altogether.
C is wrong: An empty string is still a string: nothing forces the code to handle "no day yet".
D is wrong: JSON has no Date objects, and undefined is not a JSON value: the API sends strings and null.
Q5
Which Bramblekey rule (Lab 11.1) must the mock API's handler check, and not only the booking form?
A) The request text is at most 500 characters
B) The day looks like YYYY-MM-DD
C) The piano kind is one of three
D) A day holds at most three visits
Why: Only the server knows every visit, so only it can count a day's visits; the form cannot. Field rules (length, format, one of a list) belong in a schema the form and the API share, and the server checks them again (Lesson 11.1, Step 5; Lesson 8.1).
A is wrong: A field rule: in the shared schema, checked on both sides.
B is wrong: A format rule: the same.
C is wrong: A union of three values: the type and the schema hold it.
Q6
When is a final project complete under Lesson 11.1's rubric?
A) When the total is 14 or more out of 20
B) When no row is at "not yet"
C) When every row is "strong"
D) When the instructor has seen the demo
Why: The rubric's own definition: complete when no row is "not yet". The total is information; the "strong" column is what makes it a portfolio piece; "not yet" is the list of what to do next.
A is wrong: There is no points threshold; one row at 0 means not complete, whatever the total.
C is wrong: Strong rows are welcome, not required.
D is wrong: The demo is evidence for some rows; the rubric decides.

11.1 · Final Project: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run it.
node -p "1.10 + 2.20"
3.3000000000000003
Why: Binary fractions: 1.10 and 2.20 have no exact binary form, so their sum is a hair over 3.3. A basket that shows this, or a comparison with 3.3 that is false, is a bug report waiting to happen.
P2
Predict, then run it.
node -p "110 + 220"
330
Why: Whole cents add exactly. Name the type (Cents) and divide by 100 only when you format for people (Intl.NumberFormat with style: 'currency').

11.2 · Next Steps: self-check

Q7
pageTitles is written with an annotation instead of satisfies. What does npm run check say about the last line?
const pageTitles: Record<string, string> = {
  events: 'Cleanup events',
  sites: 'Beach sites',
  impact: 'Impact',
}

type PageName = keyof typeof pageTitles

function title(page: PageName) {
  return `${pageTitles[page]} · Shoreline Crew HQ`
}

console.log(title('volunteers'))
A) Nothing: the annotation made PageName plain string
B) TS2345: 'volunteers' is not one of the three page names
C) TS2741: Property 'volunteers' is missing
D) TS7016: no declaration file
Why: An annotation replaces the object's own type with Record<string, string>, so keyof typeof pageTitles is string and any page name passes. Run for real on the Lesson 11.2 ts-lab: the check printed only its two header lines. With satisfies it is TS2345 (Lesson 11.2, Step 1).
B is wrong: That is the satisfies version's answer.
C is wrong: Nothing is missing from the table.
D is wrong: TS7016 is about a JavaScript import without types.
Q8
You deleted one line of statusTones in advanced.ts. What does this tell you?
advanced.ts:23:3 - error TS2741: Property 'cancelled' is missing in type '{ draft: "neutral"; scheduled: "info"; completed: "success"; }' but required in type 'Record<EventStatus, "danger" | "info" | "neutral" | "success">'.
Found 1 error in advanced.ts:23
A) satisfies changed the values' types to string
B) The file needs as instead of satisfies
C) satisfies checked the table against Record<EventStatus, …>, and cancelled is missing
D) EventStatus has a new status
Why: satisfies checks the value against the type (every status needs a tone) while the values keep their literal types ("neutral", "info"...). With as the same table would compile and crash on the first cancelled cleanup.
A is wrong: The message itself shows the literal types "neutral", "info" and "success".
B is wrong: as would silence the check: the Common Mistake.
D is wrong: The union is the API's own, unchanged.
Q9
In Lab 11.2, tides.ts imports ./tide-clock.js, a JavaScript file with no types, and tsc reports TS7016. Which fix keeps every call checked?
A) Turn off strict in tsconfig.json
B) Add // @ts-ignore above the import
C) Rename tide-clock.js to tide-clock.ts
D) Write tide-clock.d.ts next to it, declaring its two exports with real types
Why: A declaration file is a promise about the JavaScript, made once: every call is then checked against it. Its parameter is a string, so the lab's test line nextLowTide(42) becomes an error, as the test's @ts-expect-error requires (Lesson 11.2, Declaration Files).
A is wrong: That switches off checking everywhere, not only for this import.
B is wrong: The import becomes any: nothing about the calls is checked.
C is wrong: Renaming does not add types: the file would be full of implicit any errors.
Q10
Read it from the outside in: what is the type of store.setDensity?
interface Settings {
  theme: 'system' | 'light' | 'dark'
  density: 'roomy' | 'compact'
}

type Setters<T> = {
  [K in keyof T & string as `set${Capitalize<K>}`]: (value: T[K]) => void
}
(value: "compact" | "roomy") => void
Why: For each string key K of Settings, a property named set + K with a capital letter, whose value is a function taking T[K]: density becomes setDensity, taking 'roomy' | 'compact'. TypeScript 7 prints a literal union sorted, so the hover shows "compact" first (Lesson 11.2, Step 2).
Q11
Step 4 of Lesson 11.2 hands Theo's id to eventLink. What does the error protect against?
advanced.ts:106:50 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'VolunteerId' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EventId'.
Found 1 error in advanced.ts:106
A) A volunteer's id silently ending up in a cleanup's address
B) A runtime crash in toEventId
C) Slower code: brands add checks at run time
D) A cleanup id with capital letters
Why: Both ids are strings, and TypeScript compares by shape, so without the brand /api/events/v04 would compile. The brand is a type-only property: nothing exists at run time, and only the checking constructor may cast (Lesson 11.2, Common Mistakes: branding with a cast anywhere).
B is wrong: toEventId is the place that checks; the error is about which kind of id goes where.
C is wrong: Brands disappear when the types are stripped: they cost nothing at run time.
D is wrong: The pattern check is toEventId's job, at run time; the brand only says it happened.
Q12
A review comment says: "The CSV has only the current page's 10 volunteers, not all 24." Which label fits?
A) nit
B) Blocking: the feature gives wrong data
C) suggestion
D) No comment needed
Why: It changes what the user gets: a bug, so it must be fixed before merging. A good blocking comment says what, where and why, as this one does (Lesson 11.2, Exercise 3).
A is wrong: A nit is about tidiness, such as blank lines.
C is wrong: A suggestion makes a working change better; this one is not working.
D is wrong: A note "checked, fine" is for a worry that turned out fine; this one did not.

Finished in …ms on 42 files with 150 rules using … threads.

What each task shows: (1) unions, null for "known to be empty" (a client with only a phone, a visit with no day), no stored total, NewVisit = Pick<...> and VisitChanges = Partial<Pick<...>>; the TS2578 lines are type tests that fail until the types are precise. (2) Values computed, never stored: visitTotal (reduce from 0, so the awkward visit totals 0), isOverdue (only scheduled visits, "YYYY-MM-DD" strings compare in order), isVisitStatus through the label Record, a nextSteps table. (3) A rule only the server can check (three visits a day, the visit itself not counted) and the error-code chain of Lesson 11.1. Accept a switch or a Set for the status rule; the messages must match the tests.

Lab 11.2 (guided): labs/module-25/l11_2-next-steps/starter → solution

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 11.1 · Final Project
  1. B npx tsc -b, because every table of error codes is a Record<ErrorCode, …> that must list every value: A Record keyed by the union must list every value, so npx tsc -b reports TS2741 at each table that is missing the new code. An array of the union would compile without it.
  2. D Not stored at all: computed when needed from parts and labor, and from promisedFor and today: Both can be worked out from data the ticket already has: parts and labor for the total, promisedFor and today for overdue. Stored, they disagree with that data the first time a line changes or a day passes.
  3. A The request body is the form schema's output (notes: null), but the schema checks its input (text), so it refuses null: The form schema's input is what the fields hold (text), its output is what gets sent (null for empty notes). Checking the sent body against the input side refuses null, so the server needs its own request schema built from the same rules.
  4. C The thinnest app that runs end to end: layout, a list and a detail page from your types with their states, and a draft deploy of the demo: The thinnest version that goes all the way through: your types, the mock API, the routes and states, the build and a draft deploy. It proves every joint works while changes are still cheap.
  5. D Stop and re-plan: put accounts in the charter's "nice later" list and cut or simplify a nice-to-have, not the checks: When a milestone runs 50 % over, stop and re-plan: cut or simplify a nice-to-have, and put new ideas like accounts in the charter's nice-later list. Cut features before you cut checks.
Lesson 11.2 · Next Steps
  1. C TS2345: 'volunteers' is not assignable to "events" | "impact" | "sites": satisfies checks the object without replacing its type, so keyof typeof pageTitles is exactly events, sites and impact. Volunteers is not one of them, so the check reports TS2345.
  2. A Install its @types/ package if one exists; otherwise write a .d.ts that declares the functions you use, with real types: Types from DefinitelyTyped are the first stop. If there are none, a small declaration of the functions you call, with real parameter types, keeps every call checked.
  3. D Public pages in a framework with server rendering such as Next.js; the HQ stays a Vite single-page app: Public pages that must be readable before any JavaScript runs suit a framework with server rendering, such as Next.js. The signed-in HQ works well as a single-page app and can stay one.
  4. B Stop using it in both apps first, release them, then remove it from the API: Stop using before you remove: once neither app reads parking, the API can drop it without breaking anyone. Adding goes the other way round: the API first, then the apps.
  5. A Nothing urgent: it is a new major outside your range; if you want it, read the release notes and try it alone on a branch with CI: Latest beyond your range is a new major. It is information: read its release notes, try it on its own branch with the full checks, and merge only when CI is green. The course holds Babel at 7 on purpose for the React Compiler setup.

Exit Ticket

  1. Two type decisions: any two of unions for statuses and categories, null for "known to be empty", money in cents, computed values never stored, request types with Pick / Partial, a Record table for every union.
  2. satisfies: when the value must be checked against a type and keep its own precise type (keys, literal values); an annotation replaces the type, as checks almost nothing.
  3. Dates and reviewer: the learner's own; Milestone 2 about a week away. No reviewer or no dates: message the learner this week.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A (the home page first). Still thinks in screens; show the Cobalt types, then ask what the page would have used.
  • Q2 A (switch to an array). Wants the error gone, not the code right. Show the lesson's sentence on arrays and 409s.
  • Q4 B (optional). Has not separated "not sent" from "known to be empty": Lab 11.1's email row.
  • Q7 B. Assumes the annotation keeps the keys: run P3 together.
  • Q9 A or B. Prefers silencing to describing: the lesson's nextLowTide(42) Common Mistake.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 26: Final Project Showcase and Final Assessment

Answers for Session 26: the Part 3 review, where the written final's key lives (with its points and pass mark), the evidence check's reference outputs and notes for the retrospective.

Session 26 12 self-check items Confidential

Part 3 Review: Self-check

Q1
What goes into a TanStack Query key?
A) Everything the query function uses, from general to specific, such as ['events', 'list', search]
B) Only the endpoint's address
C) A random id, so answers never mix
D) The component's name
Why: The key is the answer's address in the cache: two requests that can answer differently need different keys, and general-to-specific keys let one invalidation cover a whole family (['events']).
B is wrong: Two pages of the same address would share one cached answer.
C is wrong: A random key never finds its cached answer again.
D is wrong: Two components asking the same question should share the answer.
Q2
A volunteer's mark should appear at once, before the API answers. What does an optimistic mutation need besides changing the cache first?
A) Nothing: the API always agrees
B) A page reload after the request
C) A rollback to the saved value when the API refuses, then invalidating the keys when it settles
D) A second mutation that repeats the request
Why: Optimistic means the screen goes ahead of the server; when the server says no (a 409, a 500), the old value comes back and the person sees why. Lesson 7.2's test checks exactly that.
A is wrong: Servers refuse: rules, conflicts, outages.
B is wrong: A reload throws away the app's state and the message.
D is wrong: Repeating a refused request does not undo the screen.
Q3
Which value belongs in an app-wide store such as Zustand, saved in the browser?
A) The list of cleanups from the API
B) The status filter a link should share
C) Whether a form field is filled in
D) The person's chosen theme and list density
Why: It belongs to this browser, every screen reads it, and it must survive a reload. Server data lives in TanStack Query, shareable views in the address, form values in the form (Lesson 7.3's placement table).
A is wrong: Server state: TanStack Query.
B is wrong: Address state: search params.
C is wrong: Form state: React Hook Form.
Q4
What reported this typo in register('titel')?
src/Scratch.tsx:7:19 - error TS2345: Argument of type '"titel"' is not assignable to parameter of type '"capacity" | "date" | "description" | "endTime" | "meetingPoint" | "siteId" | "startTime" | "title"'.
A) Zod, when the form was submitted
B) TypeScript: register only accepts the field names of the form's values, typed from the schema
C) The browser's console
D) oxlint
Why: React Hook Form types register from the form values, which come from the Zod schema (z.input): a misspelled field is a compile error instead of a field that never saves (Lesson 8.1).
A is wrong: Zod checks values at run time; the name never reaches it.
C is wrong: This is tsc output, before anything runs.
D is wrong: oxlint does not know the form's fields.
Q5
A schema turns an empty Notes box into null with a transform. What type does the field hold (input), and what does the form hand on (output)?
Input string (the box's text); output string | null
Why: z.input is what the fields hold, z.output what onSubmit receives after the checks and transforms. The API receives the output, which is why Lesson 11.1's request schema accepts null.
Q6
A long form fails with four problems. What does the error summary from Lesson 8.2 give people?
A) A list of every problem at the top, each linking to its field
B) A red border around the whole form
C) One alert with the first problem only
D) The raw Zod issues
Why: Keyboard and screen-reader users reach the summary first and jump straight to each field; each field still shows its own message, linked with aria-describedby.
B is wrong: Color alone tells nobody what to fix.
C is wrong: People fix one problem and meet the next one on the next try.
D is wrong: Machine messages; the schema's own messages are written for people.
Q7
Why do the course's tests find a button with getByRole('button', { name: 'Save' }) rather than a test id?
A) It is faster to run
B) Test ids are not allowed in React 19
C) It finds the button the way people and screen readers do, so a button with no accessible name fails the test
D) It works without a DOM
Why: Querying by role and accessible name tests the page as it is used, and makes accessibility mistakes visible as test failures (Lesson 9.1).
A is wrong: Speed is not the reason.
B is wrong: Test ids work; they just test nothing a person sees.
D is wrong: Testing Library needs a DOM (jsdom).
Q8
A test calls server.use(...) with a handler that answers 500. What happens in the other tests?
A) They all get 500s
B) Nothing: the setup resets the handlers after each test, so the override lives for one test only
C) MSW stops answering
D) The real API is called
Why: Overrides test the error states (the message, the Try again button) without breaking the shared handlers; afterEach(server.resetHandlers) puts them back (the starter's setup does it).
A is wrong: Only until the test ends.
C is wrong: The other handlers stay in place.
D is wrong: Unhandled requests fail the test; nothing reaches a real server.
Q9
What did this end-to-end test find?
  ✘   8 e2e/accessibility.spec.ts:23:7 › dark theme › / has no axe violations (561ms)
...
    Error: expect(received).toEqual(expected) // deep equality
    - Array []
    + Array [
    +   "color-contrast: Elements must meet minimum color contrast ratio thresholds (1)",
...
A) A broken link on the home page
B) A JavaScript error in the dark theme
C) A slow page
D) Text in the dark theme without enough contrast against its background
Why: axe ran on the page in the dark theme and reported a color-contrast violation: the tokens need a dark value that reaches 4.5:1. Rubric row 7 asks for 0 violations in light and dark, at both widths.
A is wrong: axe found a contrast rule broken, not a link.
B is wrong: The page ran; the test compared axe's list with an empty one.
C is wrong: Nothing here measures time.
Q10
The new-event page is a lazy route. What does a guest who only browses the public pages download because of that?
A) Not the new-event page's code, and not the forms chunk it needs
B) Everything, a little later
C) Only the HTML, never JavaScript
D) The page's code, but not its styles
Why: A lazy route's code (and the libraries only it imports, such as the forms chunk) loads when someone opens it; the budget test measures what a guest really downloads.
B is wrong: Lazy code that is never needed is never fetched.
C is wrong: The app is JavaScript; lazy routes split it.
D is wrong: The page's code is not downloaded at all.
Q11
What does this output prove before the first push?
git check-ignore -v shoreline-hq/.env.local shoreline-hq/node_modules shoreline-hq/dist

shoreline-hq/.gitignore:13:*.local      shoreline-hq/.env.local
shoreline-hq/.gitignore:10:node_modules shoreline-hq/node_modules
shoreline-hq/.gitignore:11:dist shoreline-hq/dist
That .gitignore keeps .env.local, node_modules and dist out of Git (line 13's *.local rule covers .env.local)
Why: Each line names the .gitignore line that ignores the path. A path that is not ignored prints nothing: check before the first commit, because a pushed secret stays in the history.
Q12
Why does Milestone 2 end with a draft deploy instead of leaving deployment for the last week?
A) Netlify requires it
B) Deep-link 404s, missing variables and a CI run nobody saw green are ten-minute fixes in week one and a crisis in week four
C) It makes the app faster
D) Peer reviewers need it before Milestone 3
Why: The walking skeleton proves every joint (types, mock API, routes, build, host) while changes are cheap: Lesson 11.1's "everything on localhost until the last week" mistake.
A is wrong: Nothing forces it; it is a habit.
C is wrong: A deploy changes nothing about speed.
D is wrong: The review is Milestone 7.

The Written Final Assessment

The 24 items, their key and the scoring rule are in final-assessment.html, final project section (written from kit_generators/FINAL/gen_final.py): 27 points (21 multiple-choice items at 1 point, 3 short answers at 2 points), pass mark 19 (19 of 27, 70 %). Correct letters: F1 C, F2 A, F3 B, F4 D, F5 B, F7 B, F8 C, F9 C, F10 B, F11 D, F12 A, F13 B, F15 C, F16 D, F17 A, F18 B, F19 D, F20 A, F22 C, F23 B, F24 D. Short answers F6, F14, F21: 2 points for both halves of the model answer, 1 for one half.

Reading it: each line names the rubric row its evidence belongs to. FIX means evidence the rubric needs is missing (the verdict is NOT READY and the exit code 1); CHECK means a person must look (READY WITH NOTES). any in code is a CHECK, not a FIX: the rubric asks the learner to explain each one. The check never reads the live site, the CI run or the Git history.
